# Performance: counters, budgets and timings

The home of LBTerm's performance measuring: what it counts, how fast each thing it does should be, the checks that hold it to that, and the tooling for what's hard to time. Every stage of this work keeps this file up to date.

## Why

Lachlan, 2026-10-29, after an LBTerm window was found using about 80% of a CPU core for as long as its Conductor's view was open:

> the whole conductor using 80% CPU should have been caught by a test or something as soon as it was implemented. We should have checks that what we do uses the expected resources. There might be 1000s of these bugs hidden in our core already, wezterm. We should also have expected timings, so if something takes longer than expected, theres probably a bug or issue causing it. If some things are too hard to time, create the proper tooling to do it. We really want LBTerm to be the number 1 terminal. We want speeds that we can boast about. With animations people only dream about.

The Conductor's view painted a frame every refresh while it was open and nothing on screen changed. No test looked at what a window costs when it's doing nothing, so it went unseen until he noticed the fan. What was there to build on: the slow-frame log (`lbterm-extras/src/slow.rs`, which logs one thing holding the GUI thread over `lbterm_slow_ms`, but nothing below it and nothing about how often), upstream's `metrics` histograms (`wezterm-gui/src/stats.rs`, printed to stderr every `periodic_stat_logging` seconds and otherwise unread), the state file for test windows (lbterm/STATE.md: frames, `animating`, `settled`), the control pipe (lbterm/CONTROL.md) and the harness (`lbterm/harness.psm1`). None of them said how many frames a window painted, who asked for them, or what the process spent.

## The plan

Five parts, from the brief of 2026-10-29. Each says where it stands.

### 1. Counters, always on (done: the first stage)

In the GUI, cheap enough to leave on in Lachlan's daily LBTerm: atomics in fixed tables, nothing allocated and no lock taken per event.

- Every site slow.rs times (a window message, a main-thread task by where it was spawned, a window's paint by its parts): how many runs, their total and the longest, every run, not only those over the threshold.
- Frames painted, per window, and who asked for each: every repaint request (`Window::invalidate`, and the one place in window.rs that asks for a paint without it) by its caller's source line, through `#[track_caller]`, which a call through a trait object loses, so the key, mouse and rail handlers, which hold their window as a `&dyn WindowOps`, ask with `lbterm_invalidate()` (window/src/lib.rs), which passes its caller on. A frame's reasons are the requests made for its window since its last.
- Timers by where they're armed, with how many fired and how many were dropped first; the GUI thread's wakes.
- The mux client's PDUs, sent and received, by type, with their bytes.
- The process: its CPU, the GUI thread's own (GetThreadTimes and its cycle count), each thread's by name, private bytes, the working set, handles, threads, GDI and USER objects.
- Upstream's histograms (stats.rs), with their percentiles, and its counters.
- The control pipe's `metrics` answers with all of it, since the process started or since the last `"reset": true`.

### 2. Warnings in the daily build (done: the second stage)

- **At rest, nothing paints.** A window that paints while it's at rest (no input, no pane output, no animation, fade or wait in flight: the test the state file's `animating` and `settled` make) logs a WARN on target `lbterm::budget`, rate-limited, naming who asked: `window-0 painted 312 frames in 10 s at rest, asked by render/conductor_view.rs:338 (300), render/lights.rs:120 (12)`. What repaints at rest on purpose (a blinking cursor, a clock in view, a spinner on a busy pad) is named and allowed its rate, no more. The same for timers that fire and tasks that run at rest.
- **Expected times for actions.** A table (its names and defaults in `config/src/lbterm.rs`, where LBTerm keeps its names) of actions and the time each should take, from what starts it (the key or click message, the cli request, the mux notification) to the first frame that shows it: a key's echo in a pane, a tab switch, a new tab, a closed tab, a split, the rail opening, the InMail list, the Conductor's view, a config reload, a paint. Over its time, a WARN on `lbterm::budget` says by how much and what the time went on (the sites slow.rs saw in between). Each one's histogram in `metrics` (its counts and percentiles in `budget.actions`). `lbterm.ps1 logs -Slow` (or a new `-Budget`) reads the lines.

Built as the brief says, but for three things it decided: a spinner on a busy pad needed no allowance, since DWM turns it and the GUI paints nothing for it (the rail's ages, which tick each second, did: they're a clock in view); an action started by the cli or the mux server, which the GUI only hears of as a notification, isn't timed in the daily build (the runner times those from outside); and a window's first three frames aren't timed as paints but are start-up's. "Warnings in the daily build" below has all of it.

### 3. The budget runner (done: at rest the third stage, the actions the fourth)

`lbterm/perf/budgets.ps1` with `lbterm/perf/budgets.json`, and `lbterm.ps1 budgets -Only a,b [-Bin <dir>] [-Runs n]` (every scenario only with `-AllScripts`). Scenarios in test windows through the harness; each measured several times, its median held to its budget; a failure names the top sites from `metrics` and every `lbterm::budget` line the window logged. A report in `target\budgets\<run>\report.json` and a table, and one line per run appended to `target\budgets\history.jsonl` in the main checkout (the build, the date, every number, one file for every slot and worktree), so a slow drift shows, and each run says what moved since the scenario's last.

- **At rest** (settle, then measure 5 s or more: the GUI process's and the GUI thread's CPU, frames, repaint requests by site, timers fired, task time, the mux server's CPU): one tab; twelve tabs; a cursor blinking; the rail's panel open; the pointer resting on a pad; the objectives strip with a stand-in session; the scroll strip on fake-claude; the Conductor's pad with a played hub; the Conductor's view open with a played seat (`conductor-view.ps1` plays one); the InMail list open; the projects card; the tasks card; a thumbnail in view; the strip's detail card; a link under the pointer; search; copy mode; the command palette; a zoomed pane; the window unfocused; minimized; four windows.
- **Actions** (median and 95th percentile of N runs, from the posted message or cli call to the state-file frame that shows it): a typed character's echo; a tab switch by key and by click; a new tab, local and on a mux domain; a close; a split; a zoom; the rail opening; the InMail list; the projects card; the Conductor's view; a config reload; `cli list`; start to first frame; a resize; output throughput (MB/s of a writer program).
- **Budgets** come from what the current build measures, with headroom (1.5 times the median, with a floor), except where the measured value is itself the bug (anything at rest above a few frames or a few ms of CPU a second): there the budget is what it should be, and the runner fails until it's fixed. Those failures are listed; budgets are never loosened to pass them.
- The pure parts (reading budgets.json, judging a run, the report) get Pester tests in `lbterm/tests/`.

The at-rest half is built ("The budget runner" below), as the brief says but for five things it decided. CPU is read by cycles, not Windows' CPU times, which come in 15.625 ms ticks and so read 5 s at rest as 0 or 3.1 ms a second. The state file's writer thread, a test window's alone, is left out of the process's CPU. Every scenario runs on a mux domain of the test's own, as Lachlan's tabs do, but for `one-tab-local`. The Conductor's view is played two ways, its seat sending its view every 250 ms as the real one does while sessions run (`conductor-view`) and silent (`conductor-view-quiet`), since a silent seat costs nothing and so hid what the real one costs. And a locked PC doesn't stop a run as it stops e2e, since nothing rests on a capture: each scenario measured while it was says so.

The actions half is built too ("An action", under "The budget runner" below), as the brief says but for six things it decided. Every action is timed from just before what starts it is posted to the `time_ms` of the first frame the state file says shows it, and the frames are kept as they're written (the harness's `Start-FrameRecorder`): a poll of the file reads and parses it in a few milliseconds and sleeps for a timer tick between, while frames come 8 ms apart at `max_fps` 120 and each overwrites the last, so a poll can miss the frame that first shows it and time it a frame long. Each also has its cost, not only its time: the GUI thread's, the process's and the mux server's cycles from just before it to the window quiet again, the frames it painted and, from `metrics`, its messages; a new tab that takes 66 ms but 130 ms of the GUI thread and 80 of the server's is a finding the time alone hides. The first run in a window is a warm-up, said as `first_ms` and kept out of the rest. Where the GUI times the action itself (`gui`), its own time from `metrics`' `budget.actions` is beside the outside time, and the window's `lbterm_budget` time for it is the scenario's own budget, so a run over it has the GUI's line saying what the time went on. A start is timed from the process's creation time, to its first frame and to its tab's pane (on a mux domain the server's, after the "Connecting..." pane). And besides the brief's list, a key's echo and the throughput are measured locally as well as on a mux domain, so what the mux costs shows.

### 4. What's hard to time (done: the fifth stage)

- **Key to screen.** The GUI stamps each key message's arrival (QPC) and, for the frame that first shows its effect, when the frame was presented and when it reached the screen (DXGI or DirectComposition frame statistics). Key to paint, to present and to screen as histograms in `metrics`, and as actions in the runner. For a key typed into a pane the effect is the frame after the pane's next output; for LBTerm's own keys, the frame after the action.
- **Frames missed during motion.** Across each animation (a split, a close, the panel, a tab switch), DWM's and DirectComposition's frame statistics, so the runner can say whether every refresh got a frame, and any paint longer than a refresh while animating.
- **Throughput and latency under load**: a writer at full speed in one pane, keys echoed in another.

Built as the brief says ("What's hard to time" below), but for six things it decided, and one thing it couldn't do on this PC.

- Every action the budget times is timed to the screen, not only keys, and every frame's own paint too, each from what started it. For a key that's slow.rs's `cause`, the message's arrival in `wnd_proc` on QueryPerformanceCounter's clock; GetMessageTime, the time Windows stamped on the input, counts in whole milliseconds and isn't used.
- When a frame went up comes from GetFrameStatistics on the composition swapchain of the visual that shows it. IDXGISwapChainMedia, the statistics media swapchains give, isn't there on these (E_NOINTERFACE).
- Whether every refresh got a frame comes from DirectComposition's compositor clock statistics (Windows 11): each of DWM's frames keeps every screen's count of refreshes and of presents. DwmGetCompositionTimingInfo's counts are per caller here (finding 41), and DXGI's statistics are one swapchain's, while DWM plays the motion on its own.
- A window others hide shows its frames and its motion to nobody, and its screen gets no frame for them, so the GUI looks at what's over its window before it trusts either, and says hidden, never missed or lost.
- Latency under load is two runner scenarios, `key-echo-load` and `key-echo-load-local`; and a pane's close (`close-pane`) joins the actions, so the four motions the brief names (a split, a close, the panel, a tab switch) are all among them.
- No outside tool was built. Desktop Duplication would give the screen's updates unelevated and without focus, but sees only what's on screen too, so it can add nothing while the test windows are hidden; PresentMon's ETW would see every present, but needs elevation.

What it couldn't do: measure what reached the screen in a test window on this PC. On 2026-10-30 a topmost Dashboards box covers the whole right-most screen, where test windows open, so every test window is hidden under it (finding 39). The GUI's times to its paints and presents need no window in view, and are measured; its times to the screen and the misses wait on a test window being seen, or on Lachlan's own LBTerm once this is installed, whose `lbterm control metrics --present on` turns the timing on (it's off until asked since the review, "What's hard to time") and whose next replies will say them for his windows.

### Rules

- Every counter, warning and table name goes in `config/src/lbterm.rs` or LBTerm's own crates; upstream files change as little as they can (a line calling into lbterm-extras), so upstream merges stay easy.
- Cheap is part of the spec: what the measuring costs is measured (a pane printing at full speed, typing) before and after, and said here.
- The measuring is held to the same standard as what it measures: it costs nothing on the GUI thread to ask, and its own cost is in its reply.

## The counters

The pure part is `lbterm-extras/src/counters.rs`: the tables, what's counted, the report and its JSON, tested with `cargo test -p lbterm-extras counters`. The GUI side is `wezterm-gui/src/counters.rs` (the process as Windows counts it, the threads, the reply) and a line at each place that counts:

| What | Counted in |
|---|---|
| messages | `window/src/os/windows/window.rs`, `wnd_proc` (slow.rs's scope, which every run now goes through) |
| tasks | `window/src/spawn.rs`, each run of a main-thread task, by its spawn site (promise's spawn functions, `Window::notify` and `Connection::with_window_inner` carry `#[track_caller]`) |
| paints | `wezterm-gui/src/termwindow/render/paint.rs` (pass, draw) and `render/composited.rs` (present), by mux window |
| requests | `Window::invalidate` in `window/src/os/windows/window.rs` (`#[track_caller]`), and `nc_mouse_move`'s NeedRepaint there. `#[track_caller]` doesn't survive a call through a trait object: one on a `&dyn WindowOps` (the `context` the key, mouse and rail handlers are given) named the implementation's own line, `window.rs:1046`, for 15 of a key's 32 frame credits in the review's run. Those callers ask with `context.lbterm_invalidate()`, an inherent method of `dyn WindowOps` (window/src/lib.rs) that hands its caller to the trait's `lbterm_invalidate_at`; the ten in `render/conductor_view.rs` and `render/inbox.rs`, the other track's files, still call `invalidate` and are named by `window.rs`'s line until they move. A wrapper that asks for every window's frame names whoever called it, through `#[track_caller]` of its own: `state_files::repaint_windows` (the Conductor's hub, drafts, the InMail watcher, the seat client, thumbnails) and `lbterm_strip_notice`. Elsewhere a site is where `invalidate` is written |
| windows (frames) | the start of `paint_impl` (`render/paint.rs`): each frame, credited to the requests pending for its window; a window's going (`WindowEvent::Destroyed`) frees its bit. A request for a window that has gone (a task that outlived it) claims no bit (IsWindow, asked only of a handle without one), and once all 64 are taken that's said once on `lbterm::counters` |
| timers | `lbterm_extras::counters::timer(...)` around each `Timer::after` and `Timer::at` on the GUI's paths (wezterm-gui, spawn.rs's slow-log waker, and window.rs's paint throttle until main's perf/frame-loop put a frame's hold on the message loop's own high-resolution timer, which the state file's `pace` counts, lbterm/STATE.md); `render/conductor_view.rs`'s two (a notice's end, its clock) since main's Conductor's view fix came in. Its clock repaints when an age it shows reads differently, by the minute at most ("now" to "1m"), so it declares no tick: a few frames a minute are under the at-rest allowance |
| gui (wakes) | `window/src/os/windows/connection.rs`, `wait_message`: each return from `MsgWaitForMultipleObjects`, and the time it waited |
| mux | `wezterm-client/src/client.rs`'s client thread, through a hook wezterm-gui sets (`lbterm_set_traffic_hook`); codec notes the payload's size of the last PDU each thread encoded or decoded (`codec::lbterm_last_size`) |
| process, threads | `wezterm-gui/src/counters.rs`: GetProcessTimes, GetThreadTimes and QueryThreadCycleTime on a handle to the GUI thread opened when the counts start, GetProcessMemoryInfo, GetProcessHandleCount, GetGuiResources, and every thread by ntdll's NtGetNextThread with GetThreadDescription for its name |
| histograms | upstream's recorder, `wezterm-gui/src/stats.rs`'s `lbterm_snapshot` (an addition at its end); each thread keeps the handles it's recorded with (`lbterm_extras::recorder`, "What upstream's metrics cost") |
| caches | `lfucache`'s counts (`lfucache::counts`): every lookup of a window's line, shape and image caches, and the glyph cache's (`wezterm-gui/src/glyphcache.rs`) |

### Asking

- `lbterm control metrics` (`--reset` to start the counts again after the reply), `--class <class>` for another LBTerm than the daily one.
- From a test script: the harness's `Get-Metrics [-Window] [-Reset]`, `Format-Metrics` to say a reply in a few lines, `ConvertTo-MetricsTable` to look an entry up by site, and `Get-GuiCpu` for a build without metrics (the GUI process's and GUI thread's CPU as Windows counts them).
- The request itself is lbterm/CONTROL.md's "Metrics". It's answered on the pipe's own thread, never the GUI thread, so asking adds no task, no wake and no frame, and it answers while the GUI thread is held.

To see what something costs, ask with `reset` before it and again after it: the second reply is what happened in between.

### The reply

Every list is sorted most first and leaves out what ran none. Times are in milliseconds, to the microsecond.

| Field | What it says |
|---|---|
| `span_ms` | how long the counts cover: since the process started, or since the last reset |
| `totals` | the lists below added up: `frames`, `unasked`, `requests`, `messages` and `message_ms`, `tasks` and `task_ms`, `paints` and `paint_ms` (each part of each frame is one), `timers_fired`, `wakes`, `pdus_sent`, `pdus_received` |
| `gui` | `wakes`: how often the GUI thread came back from waiting for a message or a task; `waited_ms`: how long it waited in all; `busy_ms`: the span less that |
| `windows` | each mux window's `frames` and how many were `unasked` (nobody asked: Windows' own WM_PAINT, as the window is shown, uncovered or sized) |
| `requests` | each site that asked for a frame: `requests` (how many times it asked) and `frames` (how many frames it was among the reasons for). Requests made between two frames make one frame, so `requests` can be far above `frames`. A site is `<source file>:<line>`, as slow.rs names tasks |
| `messages` | each window message by name (`WM_PAINT`, `WM_USER+3`, `WM_0xC0F1`): `count`, `total_ms`, `max_ms`, its own time as slow.rs charges it (less a paint or a task run inside it) |
| `tasks` | each main-thread task by where it was spawned, the same three. A task runs once per wake of its future, so a long-lived one (a connection, a watcher) runs many times |
| `paints` | each window's paint parts: `window-0/pass` (building the frame), `window-0/draw` (into the swapchains), `window-0/present` (the compositor's present and commit, which waits on DWM for up to a refresh) |
| `timers` | each site that arms timers: `armed`, `fired`, `dropped` (dropped before firing: its task ended or was replaced). Only the timers wrapped in `counters::timer`; others still show as the tasks they wake |
| `mux` | each PDU type the mux client exchanged with its server: `sent`, `sent_bytes`, `received`, `received_bytes` (the serialized payload, compressed when it was; a frame's header adds a few bytes each) |
| `process` | over the span: `cpu_ms` (`user_ms` and `kernel_ms`), `cpu_percent` (of one core), `gui_thread_ms`, `gui_thread_percent`, `gui_thread_cycles`; now: `private_bytes`, `working_set`, `peak_working_set`, `handles`, `threads`, `gdi_objects`, `user_objects` |
| `threads` | each thread that spent any CPU over the span: `tid`, `name` (Rust names its threads: `main` is the GUI thread; empty for one started by a library), `cpu_ms`, `cpu_percent` |
| `histograms` | upstream's: `name` (with labels in braces, `pdu.size{pdu=GetPaneRenderChanges}`), `unit` (`ms`, `value` for `.size` keys, `per_second` for `.rate` keys), `count`, `mean`, `p50`, `p95`, `p99`, `max`; reset with the rest. The caches' hits and misses aren't among them since 2026-10-31: they're `caches` |
| `words` | the words the cards, the lists and the tab panel laid out (`laid_out`) and kept from the frame before (`kept`) over the span (lbterm_extras::kept; What the cards cost, below) |
| `quads` | the parts kept as quads (render/kept_quads.rs): how many times one was made again (`made`) and copied in as it was (`kept`) |
| `upstream_counters` | upstream's counters (`rpc.count{method=...}`), reset with the rest |
| `fonts` | what font setup did ("What font setup costs", below), each `{count, ms}`: font configurations made, fonts made, the font locator's lookups, system font files read and parsed, GDI substitutes turned down by name, the bundled fonts parsed, fallback searches and the characters answered from what another font found, shapers built, reloads that kept the fonts, glyph atlases made, cleared and grown, a window's shapes all cleared at once (`reshapes`); `atlas_side` the largest side an atlas has had (kept through a reset) |
| `start` | how the process's start went (`lbterm-extras/src/start.rs`), never reset: `marks` in ms since `main` (its config read, the GPU begun, the first tab, the first paint and its end), the last `window`'s, `webgpu`'s and `gpu-adapter`'s steps with when each began, and the compositor's `surfaces` made |
| `caches` | what the caches did ("What the caches keep"), by name: `hits`, `misses`, `evictions`, every window's caches of a name together (`line_quad_cache`, whose misses are lines laid out again, `line_state_cache`, `line_to_ele_shape_cache`, `shape_cache`, `glyph_cache.image_cache`, `glyph_cache`); a name that did nothing left out |
| `reply_ms` | what this reply cost on the pipe's thread, by part: `counters`, `process`, `threads`, `histograms`, `json` |
| `pid`, `build`, `reset` | the process, its version, and whether this reply started the counts again |

### Reading it

- **At rest** a window should show no frames, no requests and no paint parts, and next to no CPU. A frame there names its asker in `requests`; a timer firing over and over shows in `timers`; a polling task in `tasks`; a thread spinning in `threads`. Windows' `WM_GETTEXTLENGTH` a few times a second is another program reading the window's title (the harness's window watch does).
- **Frames against requests**: the state file's `frame` and `metrics`' frames count the same paints; `perf/metrics.ps1` holds them equal.
- **The GUI thread**: `process.gui_thread_ms` is what it spent; `gui.busy_ms` is the span less the time it waited, which counts time it was ready but not running too. Many `messages` with few `wakes` means the thread is going round its loop without waiting.
- GetProcessTimes and GetThreadTimes count in clock ticks of 15.625 ms, so a short span's CPU is a multiple of that; `gui_thread_cycles` is exact.

### What they cost

Each event, measured by `counters::tests::an_event_costs_well_under_a_microsecond` (a million in a loop, release build, this PC, 2026-10-29, five runs): a timed run (a message, a task or a paint part) 23.5 to 34.9 ns to count, a repaint request 8.4 to 9.1 ns, against 45.3 to 50.2 ns for each `Instant::now` (QueryPerformanceCounter). slow.rs already took two `Instant::now` a run before the counters whenever `lbterm_slow_ms` was on (its default is 33), so what a run costs more is the count. A GUI thread wake costs two `Instant::now` more (about 90 ns).

A `metrics` reply: about 0.5 ms on the pipe's thread (`reply_ms`: counters 0.06 to 0.2, the process 0.07 to 0.17, the threads 0.14 to 0.33, the histograms 0.05 to 0.23, the JSON 0.02 to 0.24 ms), 1 to 2 ms from a client's connect to the reply (the first after start 40 ms). The first build listed the threads from a Toolhelp snapshot: Thread32Next walks every thread on the machine (8,552 here) and that took 130 to 160 ms of CPU a reply, which showed in the next span's CPU as the window "at rest" using 3% of a core. It walks the process's own threads now.

The whole of it, as GUI CPU with the counters and without (`perf/metrics.ps1 -Measure`, 2026-10-29): 400 keys typed into a pwsh prompt 8 ms apart, then a pane printing as fast as pwsh writes for 8 s (about 40 MB), in a test window drawn as Lachlan's are (WebGpu with the compositor, max_fps 120). Before is the installed build (20261029-213152-f9d36b5e, main less one InMail change); after is this branch's. Runs interleaved, before then after, with other sessions' builds and test windows running (2 to 5 cargo, rustc or lbterm-gui processes at each run's start). CPU in ms, the median and every run:

| | GUI thread before | GUI thread after | GUI process before | GUI process after | frames before / after |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| typing, mux domain (4 runs each) | 5742 (5625, 6000, 5859, 5625) | 5797 (5688, 5531, 5906, 6000) | 7500 | 6945 | 412 / 406 |
| output, mux domain (4 each) | 7758 (7438, 7828, 7688, 7844) | 7773 (7406, 7688, 7859, 7906) | 12727 | 12383 | 559 / 570 |
| typing, local (3 each) | 3922 (3953, 3922, 3438) | 3875 (4078, 3797, 3875) | 5625 | 5422 | 406 / 410 |
| output, local (3 each) | 7000 (7219, 6813, 7000) | 6906 (6922, 6906, 6750) | 18000 | 17563 | 465 / 484 |

The counters' cost doesn't show above the runs' own spread. Worked out from what they counted instead (`-Counts`): typing on the mux domain made 6,456 tasks, 116,617 messages and 818 paint parts (about 124,000 timed runs, 3.0 ms at 24 ns), 1,599 requests (0.01 ms), 19 wakes and 2,011 PDUs (0.02 ms): about 3 ms of the GUI thread's 5.8 s, 0.05%. Earlier series on the OpenGL front end at max_fps 60 (four runs each local, three on a mux domain) came out the same way.

## Warnings in the daily build

The budget: the GUI judging itself as it runs, in every LBTerm, Lachlan's daily one included, and saying in its log when it costs more than it should. Every line is a WARN on target `lbterm::budget`; `lbterm.ps1 logs -Budget` reads them, grouped, and `-Errors` leaves them out (it says how many). The judging is `lbterm-extras/src/budget.rs` (`Budget`, tested with the time handed in); what the GUI tells it is `wezterm-gui/src/termwindow/budget.rs`, and a line at each place that says something happened:

| What | Said in |
|---|---|
| something happened to a window (input, a resize, focus, a key assignment) | `termwindow/mod.rs`, `dispatch_window_event`: every window event but a repaint, a notification and the window going |
| the mux said something of its open tab or its tabs | the same, on a `MuxNotification`: output or an alert of a pane in the open tab, and what isn't a pane's (a tab added, resized or retitled, the window's title). A background tab's output or alert is news, not activity (At rest, below); while the window is busy for more than half its quiet anyway, a pane's isn't looked at |
| a reload | `config_was_reloaded`, and `config/src/lib.rs`'s `reload`, which notes when it started reading the file (`config::lbterm::note_reload_start`) |
| a frame, with who asked for it | `render/paint.rs`, the start of `paint_impl`: the counters' `frame` hands back the sites credited with it (`lbterm_extras::budget::Reasons`) |
| what's in flight | asked only of a frame that would otherwise be at rest: DWM's animations (the compositor's `settles`, which now records every animation, not only a test window's), the hearts, and the state file's waits (`termwindow/state.rs`'s `lbterm_waits`, which the state file shares) |
| on purpose, a tick | upstream's next animation frame (`render/paint.rs`, through `lbterm_budget_animation`, at the rate it comes), update-status's next tick (`schedule_next_status_update`), the rail's clock (`render/tab_panel.rs`) and the tasks card's ages (`render/tasks_card.rs`) |
| an action begun | a key or paste to a pane (`maybe_scroll_to_bottom_for_input`), a tab switch (`activate_tab`), a new tab or split (`termwindow/spawn.rs`), a tab closed (`last_tab.rs`'s `lbterm_remove_tab`), the rail opening (`tab_panel_wake`), the InMail list (`mouseevent.rs`), the Conductor's view (the pad's click in `render/tab_panel.rs`), a reload; a paint |
| an action shown | the end of `paint_impl`, after the state file's frame |
| a run timed, a timer fired | slow.rs's scopes and counters.rs's timers: a check each (What the budget costs), and more only when the process is at rest or an action waits |

### At rest, nothing runs

A window is at rest once nothing has happened to it for `quiet_ms` (1 s): no input, no output from its open tab, nothing the mux said of its tabs, no reload; and when a frame comes that would be at rest, nothing in flight either (an animation, fade or wait, and `quiet_ms` after it ends). The process is at rest while every window is, its background tabs quiet too.

- **Frames.** A frame a window paints at rest is counted against the sites that asked for it (counters.rs's requests since its last frame). One nobody asked for is Windows' own WM_PAINT, and counted too, named `WM_PAINT` among the sites: a window shown or sized is busy anyway, and a paint Windows sends again and again because nothing validated it (stage 1's finding 1) is a loop like any other, which went unsaid while these weren't counted.
- **Background tabs' news.** What a tab that isn't open says (its output; an alert: its title, its status, a light or progress, which its rail row may show), and under the Conductor's view, which covers every pane, what the open tab says too, doesn't make the window busy, so a window with Claude sessions working behind its open tab, as Lachlan's always has, is still seen painting at rest: while every pane's output kept its window busy, a window with any tab printing was never at rest, and the Conductor's view's four frames a second would have gone unsaid in his daily use. It keeps the process from being at rest for `quiet_ms`, so the news's own tasks aren't counted as at rest; and the frames in the 100 ms after an alert of one of the window's background tabs are the news's, up to `rest_news_frames` (30) in a span, past which they're counted as at rest and the line says how many were.
- **Timers and tasks.** A timer that fires (one armed through `counters::timer`) or a task that runs while every window rests is counted against where it was armed or spawned. Not the measuring's own: the budget's waker (`termwindow/budget.rs`), the slow log's (`window/src/spawn.rs`), present.rs's, and in a test window the state file's wake after DWM's motion ends (`termwindow/state.rs`, which with a blinking cursor came to 23 task runs in 10 s at rest).
- **On purpose.** What repaints at rest on purpose says so as it arms its timer, with a tick: when it's due and the rate it may tick at. What runs from 2 ms before a tick is due to 100 ms after is that purpose's, at most 2 frames, 4 timers and 16 tasks a tick (a frame comes of a timer firing, the task that waited on it and the notify it sends; update-status's tick runs two Lua events and their ends besides). A tick is allowed while fewer than `ceil(rate × 10) + 1` of the purpose's allowed ticks fall in the 10 s before it; one past that brings nothing (what runs in it counts as at rest, and the line says how many frames came of ticks past the rate) and isn't kept toward the rate, so a purpose a little faster than it says loses a tick now and then, not every tick from the first one over (it did until the review: a blink at 1.2 a second said at 1 had all its ticks past the rate once the 12th came).

| Purpose | What | Its rate | Its tick |
|---|---|---|---|
| `animation` | upstream's next frame: a blinking cursor or blinking text, a bell's fade, an animated image, a tab's progress; only while the window has the keyboard | the rate its wait gives, never under `animation_fps` (Lachlan's: 1) and never over the fastest of it and the blinks' rates (`cursor_blink_rate`, `text_blink_rate`, `text_blink_rate_rapid`): with `animation_fps` 1 a blink turns at its own rate, 1.25 a second at `cursor_blink_rate` 800, where an easing comes at `animation_fps` | `render/paint.rs`, as the timer for `has_animation` is set (`lbterm_budget_animation`) |
| `status` | update-status's tick, and what the handler sets (the right or left status) | 1000 / `status_update_interval` (Lachlan's: one in 20 s) | `schedule_next_status_update` |
| `clock` | ages and times that move on with the clock: the rail's rows and links, the card of what a tab is running | one a second for each that counts seconds, and one for the minutes and the idle steps | the rail's paint and the tasks card's, when the next wake is an age's (the card's look at a subagent's transcript each second isn't a clock: finding 15) |

Lachlan's config (`Terminal/lbterm/lbterm.lua`, read 2026-10-30) repaints little at rest on purpose: a steady cursor (`default_cursor_style = 'SteadyBlock'`, `cursor_blink_rate = 0`), `animation_fps = 1`, no clock or right status, `status_update_interval = 20000` with an update-status handler that only does anything when the megaproject's names can't be read. What's left is the rail: each row's age ticks by the second for its first minute after its state changes, then by the minute, and its pads dim in idle steps. The spinner on a busy pad and the Conductor's turning outline and light are DWM's loops: they cost no frames, so they need no allowance.

- **The budget.** `rest_frames` (5) frames a window may paint at rest in 10 s past its purposes' and its news's; `rest_news_frames` (30) frames for its background tabs' news; `rest_timers` (10) timers and `rest_tasks` (20) task runs, taking `rest_task_ms` (20 ms) in all, in 10 s while every window rests. Each counted from the first at rest, for 10 s (a span).
- **The lines.** A span over its budget is said at once as it ends (a timer of the budget's own wakes for it), naming the most first:

  ```text
  window-0 painted 143 frames in 10 s at rest, asked by wezterm-gui/src/termwindow/mod.rs:2303 (143); on purpose, allowed: status 16
  timers fired 181 times in 10 s at rest: window/src/os/windows/window.rs:1821 (181)
  tasks ran 1320 times in 10 s at rest, 84.4 ms in all: lua-api-crates/time-funcs/src/lib.rs:84 (377, 13.0 ms), window/src/os/windows/window.rs:1819 (372, 5.7 ms), wezterm-gui/src/scripting/guiwin.rs:102 (190, 35.1 ms), ...
  ```

  After a kind's first line, the spans over in the next minute are held and said together a minute after it (`window-0 painted 1800 frames at rest in 6 more spans of 10 s in the last 60 s, asked by ...`), once a minute while it goes on, and the next line after a quiet minute is a first again. Notes after the sites: `; 3 of them nobody asked for, Windows' own WM_PAINT` (the sites name them `WM_PAINT (3)`), `; 9 of them after background tabs' news, past the 30 it may bring in 10 s`, `; 289 of them on clock ticks past the 11 it may make in 10 s`, `; on purpose, allowed: clock 11, news 30`.

### Expected times

Each action is timed from what started it to the end of the first frame that shows it (after its present). What started it: the last key press or button press or release (`slow::cause`: the message's arrival, taken by the first action that uses it, if it came in the last second; one that waited in the queue more than two ticks of the clock Windows stamps messages with, by GetTickCount less GetMessageTime, from its stamp less a tick, so a key held up behind a busy GUI thread is timed with the wait it had, finding 44's 5 to 19 ms), else the message or task running; for the rail, the end of the pointer's rest on it; for a reload, when it started reading the file. Every run slow.rs times while an action waits is in its trace, and so are the thread's last 256 runs that ended after it started, so a Lua handler that holds the thread before the tab switch it asks for is named.

| Action | From | To the end of the first frame | Its time | p50 local | p50 mux | p95, the slowest round |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `key` | a key or a paste sent to a pane (its message) | after the pane's next output (its echo) | 100 ms | 20, 20, 41 | 44, 41, 43 | 76 |
| `tab` | a tab switch: a key, a click, Lua | with the tab | 50 ms | 22, 19, 16 | 15, 16, 15 | 31 |
| `new-tab` | a new tab by a key or the menu | with it in the window | 150 ms | 61, 57, 59 | 59, 56, 59 | 99 |
| `close-tab` | a tab closed | without it | 50 ms | 22, 19, 20 | 24, 18, 26 | 34 |
| `split` | a split by a key | with the new pane | 150 ms | 72, 73, 69 | 60, 61, 65 | 92 |
| `rail` | the end of the pointer's rest on the rail | with it opening | 60 ms | 23, 26, 16 | 17, 21, 19 | 39 |
| `inbox` | a click on the InMail button | with its list | 100 ms | 32, 32, 36 | 33, 33, 34 | 58 |
| `conductor-view` | a click on the Conductor's pad | with the view | 100 ms | not measured | | |
| `picker` | a click on the new-tab pad, or a key bound to `LbtermNewTab 'Picker'` | with the picker | 10 ms | 2.1, 2.2 (two projects); 1.6 to 2.4 (49, the review's probes) | | 4.5; the first open in a GUI's life, cold, 42 to 95 (lbterm/NEW-TAB.md, "What the picker costs") |
| `pick` | Enter or a click in the picker | with the Starting row | 15 ms | 4.7, 5.5 | | 9.9 |
| `reload` | a reload starting to read the file | after it | 200 ms | 86, 80, 90 | 88, 79, 80 | 136 |
| `paint` | a frame's paint starting | its own end | 50 ms | 14, 14, 12 | 13, 13, 13 | 32 (the longest 76) |

Measured by `perf\budget-log.ps1 -Measure -Runs 15`, six rounds alternating three local and three on a mux domain (2026-10-30, 00:32 to 00:47; each action 15 times a round, keys 17, reloads 6, paints as they came, 485 to 566 a round), in a test window drawn as Lachlan's (New-TestConfig: WebGpu with the compositor, max_fps 120, the rail), on this branch's build. The PC was locked the whole time (LogonUI running; Lachlan's last real input an hour and a half before), and from the fourth round up to six other sessions' test windows ran beside it: the local keys' p50 doubled in the round they did (41 ms against 20). The histograms keep two significant figures. Each action's time is about one and a half times the slowest round's p95, rounded up: one over is slower than anything these rounds saw, so something is wrong. Where the measured value is itself what's wrong (the key's echo on a mux domain, twice the local, finding 7) the time is still set this way for the daily log, so it says the outliers and not every key; budgets.ps1's will hold it to what it should be. The Conductor's view wasn't measured: it needs a seat played to it, and another branch is changing it; its time is the InMail list's.

Not timed in the daily build: an action the cli or the mux server starts, which the GUI only hears of as a notification (the runner, budgets.ps1, times those from outside); a split from Lua's `pane:split`; a tab closed after its confirmation; a window's first three frames as paints (they build its glyph atlas and the GPU's pipelines: the first took 178 ms in a test window).

- **Over its time** an action is said at once, the first time in 10 s, with what the time went on, the longest first (each over 0.1 ms; `others` the rest of what was traced, `the rest` the time nothing traced ran, the GUI thread waiting on the pane's program, the mux server or DWM):

  ```text
  tab took 76.5 ms, 36.5 ms over its 40 ms: task wezterm-gui/src/termwindow/mod.rs:1878 59.8 ms (4), paint window-0/present 6.1 ms, paint window-0/draw 4.9 ms, paint window-0/pass 2.6 ms, message WM_KEYUP 1.1 ms, task wezterm-gui/src/termwindow/mod.rs:1807 0.3 ms, others 0.6 ms, the rest 1.0 ms
  ```

  The others over in those 10 s are said together at their end: `tab: 3 times over its 40 ms in the last 10 s, max 120.0 ms`.
- **Every one**, over or not, is counted in `metrics`' `budget.actions`: how many (`count`), how many over (`over`), how many given up on (`unshown`), and their `mean_ms`, `p50_ms`, `p95_ms`, `p99_ms` and `max_ms`, from a histogram of its own in fixed atomic buckets (`lbterm-extras/src/hist.rs`: exact to 64 µs, then 32 buckets a doubling, about 3% wide), which recording takes four relaxed atomic adds into and nothing else. They were upstream's `metrics::histogram!` until the review: each call built its key's label list, took the recorder's global lock to find the histogram and then the histogram's own, on the GUI thread, for every frame's paint.
- A key typed while the pane hasn't yet echoed the last isn't timed: one key at a time a pane. A key whose pane prints nothing within three times its time (300 ms by default: a password prompt, a program busy and not reading) is given up on without a line and counted `unshown`: what the program prints later isn't the key's, and wasn't LBTerm's time.

### What the budget costs

Where it's hot it costs a check. Every run slow.rs times now also takes an atomic load, a write into the thread's ring of recent runs and a thread-local read (and, for a task, the at-rest compare); a fired timer an atomic load and an `Instant::now`; a busy window's frame a borrow and a compare; and every frame's own paint is an action, begun and shown: its trace kept in place (24 sites, nothing allocated) and its counts and histogram in atomics, with no lock. Only what's counted at rest, which shouldn't happen at all, takes the lock on the totals `metrics` reads by site. At rest a window spends nothing on it: in the check's 12 s at rest the GUI thread spent no CPU at all.

Each, measured by `budget::tests::what_the_hot_paths_cost` (a million in a loop, release build, this PC, 2026-10-30, five runs, with the budget on and a window busy, as while anything happens): a run timed 7.7 to 20.6 ns (a task 15.4 to 22.0 ns; 33.0 to 34.2 ns while an action waits and the run is traced), a timer fired 52.9 to 62.2 ns (an `Instant::now` is 45.2 to 47.3 ns of it), an action begun and shown 267 to 272 ns. The counters' own, in the same runs: a timed run 23.4 to 32.3 ns, a request 8.4 to 15.4 ns. After the review's fix-up (2026-10-30, five runs, finding 47): a run timed 7.8 to 8.4 ns (a task 15.8 to 16.3, traced 34.0 to 34.3), a timer fired 53.4 to 54.0, an action begun and shown 323 to 326 ns with no lock taken (the trace kept in place is copied as it goes in and out of the waiting list), and its histogram's record (`hist::tests::a_record_costs_a_few_atomic_adds`) 28.9 to 29.2 ns; the GUI no longer adds upstream's `histogram!` to every paint. A `metrics` reply reads each action's histogram, 22 to 26 µs each with a reset.

The whole of it, as GUI CPU with the budget and without (`perf/metrics.ps1 -Measure`, the first stage's workload: 400 keys typed into a pwsh prompt 8 ms apart, then a pane printing as fast as pwsh writes for 8 s, about 41 MB, in metrics.ps1's own test window: WebGpu, max_fps 120). Before is the installed build (20261029-213152-f9d36b5e, which has neither the counters nor the budget); after is this branch's. Runs interleaved, before then after, 2026-10-30 00:48 to 01:02, the PC locked, with one or two cargo, rustc or lbterm-gui processes at each run's start, the daily LBTerm's GUI among them. CPU in ms, the median and every run:

| | GUI thread before | GUI thread after | GUI process before | GUI process after | frames before / after |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| typing, local (4 runs each) | 3914 (4047, 3813, 4016, 3688) | 3852 (3344, 3656, 4047, 4141) | 6055 | 6047 | 409 / 407 |
| output, local (4 each) | 7180 (7375, 7078, 7047, 7281) | 7430 (7453, 7406, 7234, 7563) | 18664 | 18586 | 442 / 464 |
| typing, mux domain (3 each) | 5406 (5953, 5266, 5406) | 5672 (2609, 5719, 5672) | 7469 | 6922 | 407 / 411 |
| output, mux domain (3 each) | 7563 (7313, 7703, 7563) | 7688 (7719, 7688, 7281) | 12281 | 12422 | 565 / 573 |

Every difference is inside a build's own spread. The after build painted 22 more frames of local output in its median (the GUI thread's cost per frame the same, 16.2 against 16.0 ms), and one after run of typing on a mux domain took 2.6 s of the GUI thread where the rest took 5.3 to 6.0 (it painted 561 frames to the others' 406 to 414; why wasn't looked into). Worked out from the counts instead: typing on a mux domain makes about 123,000 runs timed (finding 1), at 8 ns each about 1 ms, and 400 paints at 0.27 µs another 0.1 ms, of the GUI thread's 5.4 s: 0.02%.

## The budget runner

`lbterm.ps1 budgets -Only a,b [-Bin <dir>] [-Runs 3] [-Seconds 5]` holds what LBTerm costs to a budget, scenario by scenario, in test windows: at rest (`kind` `rest`, the default), what it spends while nothing happens to it, and for an action (`kind` `action`, "An action" below), how long it takes to show and what it costs. On demand, like e2e, never part of the check, since some scenarios fail until what they found is fixed. Each scenario of `lbterm/perf/budgets.json` (`-Only` some, by name or wildcard, or `rest` or `action` for every one of a kind; `-Runs` is spans at rest and runs of an action) runs in a process of its own through `perf/budgets.ps1 -Scenario <name>` (as e2e runs a script, `Invoke-E2eAttempt`), its output in `target\budgets\<run>\<scenario>`, and is judged as it ends. One that couldn't be measured (its setup threw) runs again once; one over budget never does, since it was measured `-Runs` times already. At the end, `report.json` in the run's folder, a table, and one line appended to the main checkout's `target\budgets\history.jsonl` ("The report and the history"). The exit is 1 when any is over budget or couldn't be measured. The judging, the report, the table and the history are `lbterm/budgets.psm1`'s; the setups and the measuring `perf/budgets.ps1`'s.

### A scenario

A test window that looks like Lachlan's (New-TestConfig: his `lbterm.lua`, WebGpu with the compositor, `max_fps` 120, the rail, the strip, the objectives panel and the scroll strip), on a mux domain of the test's own as his tabs are (budgets.json's `domain` says when it isn't), with USERPROFILE a scratch folder, so the summaries folder, the prompt history and anything his handlers write are the test's own, and none of his projects. A mux server started for a connecting GUI opens a tab of its own with the default program and never runs the one `connect <domain> -- <program>` names, so a scenario's program gets a tab of its own there and the server's first is closed. The setup does what the scenario needs (tabs, a pointer posted to rest somewhere, a menu, a played hub or seat), gives the window the keyboard with a posted `WM_SETFOCUS` unless it's about not having it, and waits for the state file to say it's there. Then it settles (the state file settled, within 8 s, and `settle_ms` more, 2 s, so the GUI's own budget, whose `quiet_ms` is 1 s, takes it as at rest) and is measured `-Runs` spans of `-Seconds`, nothing sent to the window meanwhile. After each span it must still be what it set up (the menu open, the card there, the window minimized), or the scenario is an error: what was measured isn't it.

Test windows open on the right-most screen, which on this PC is a 60 Hz one; Lachlan's main screen runs at 160 Hz. Since main's perf/frame-loop a window holds its next frame back a period from a paint's start, `max_fps`' 8.3 ms or a refresh of its screen if that's longer, so a test window's frames come at most every 16.7 ms where his come every 8.3: an action whose frame waits on another's hold measures up to a refresh slower here than on his screen. A key's echo did until perf/key-latency (finding 35); keys typed faster than a refresh still do (`key-burst`).

A span is, in this order: `metrics` with `reset` (what follows is the span's), the state file's frame count, a snapshot (`Get-CpuSnapshot`), the sleep, a snapshot, the frame count, `metrics`. So the replies and the measuring's own reads fall outside the CPU it measures, and the pointer is posted once and left (with `LBTERM_POSTED_POINTER` a posted move hovers until a posted `WM_MOUSELEAVE`), never held, which would post a move every 16 ms and keep the window busy.

| What | Unit | How |
|---|---|---|
| `frames` | a second | the state file's frame count, every window of the process: a build without metrics is measured the same way |
| `gui_thread_ms` | ms a second | the GUI thread's cycles (`QueryThreadCycleTime`) over the processor's nominal rate (`~MHz`, 3000 here) |
| `cpu_ms` | ms a second | the GUI process's cycles (`QueryProcessCycleTime`), less those of `lbterm-state`, the state file's writer, which only a test window has |
| `state_ms` | ms a second | that writer's, said apart |
| `server_cpu_ms` | ms a second | the test's mux servers' cycles (those whose command line names its folder) |
| `conpty_cpu_ms` | ms a second | the conpty hosts' (`OpenConsole.exe`, one a pane, children of the GUI or a server) |
| `requests`, `timers`, `tasks`, `task_ms`, `messages`, `wakes` | a second | `metrics`' totals over its own span (from its reset to its reply) |
| `private_mb`, `handles` | | the GUI process's at the span's end, said, not judged |
| `loops` | | how many loops the state file says the window's DWM plays as the span ends (a waiting pad's arc, the Conductor's turning hues and its light: lbterm/COMPOSITOR.md, "Loops"), each keeping DWM composing every refresh; judged only where a scenario gives it a budget |
| `threads` | ms a second | each GUI thread's cycles by its name (`GetThreadDescription`), else the module it started in (`(nvwgf2umx.dll)`, `(ntdll.dll)` for a thread-pool worker), the five that ran most |
| `server_threads` | ms a second | the same of the mux servers' threads (`Get-ServerThreadCycles`), and what threads that started and ended within the span spent, as `(threads gone by the end)`: the process's cycles less every thread's alive at both ends. An action's run has both lists too, in ms, for where a run's CPU went (finding 49) |
| `ticks` | ms a second | Windows' own CPU times for the process and the GUI thread, a check on the cycles |
| `frame_ms` | ms | the GUI thread's cycles a frame painted (`gui_thread_ms` over `frames`): a build without metrics has it too. What a busy scenario holds (below) |
| `pass_ms` | ms | the paint pass a frame, building it: `metrics`' paints, each window's `pass`, its time over its count |
| `words` | a second | the words the cards, the lists and the tab panel laid out over the span (`metrics`' `words.laid_out`; What the cards cost, below) |
| `lines` | a second | the panes' lines laid out as quads again, missing the line quad cache (the state file's `work.lines`, so a build without metrics has it too): a frame should lay out the lines that changed and no other (What a line costs, below) |

Windows' CPU times count in clock ticks of 15.625 ms, so a thread that ran a microsecond across a tick is charged the tick, and 5 s at rest read 0, 3.1 or 6.3 ms a second. Cycles are exact: the time-stamp counter's ticks while each thread ran, kernel mode included, at the processor's nominal rate whatever its clock does. Checked on this PC (2026-10-30): a pwsh spinning 3 s on one thread, its cycles over 3,000,000 a ms said 4,542 ms against Windows' 4,500 ms. With metrics the reply also names who ran, added up over the spans in a failure: who asked for the frames, the tasks and timers by site, the messages, and the GUI's own budget's `at_rest` and `on_purpose`. The lines the GUI logged on `lbterm::budget` while the scenario ran are kept: what ran at rest is the scenario's, an action over its time (start-up's first paints, the setup's own) apart.

### The budgets

budgets.json's `rest.budgets` are the defaults, what rest should cost, not what any build measured: at rest nothing paints and the terminal's processes are asleep. A scenario's own `budgets` lie over them. A busy scenario (`"busy": true`, only at rest) has budgets.json's `busy.budgets` over the rest's first: its open tab rewrites a line every 50 ms, as a working Claude Code session's spinner does, so it paints 20 frames a second on purpose, and what it holds is what a frame costs with a card, a list or the rail open over the panes (`frame_ms`, against `busy-one-tab`'s and `busy-tabs`' with nothing open), what its paint pass costs (`pass_ms`) and that nothing is laid out again while what they show stays the same (`words`). What the cards cost, below, has what they measured. Its `bug` says why a scenario fails, and where it's fixed; it never changes the verdict.

| Metric | Its budget | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `frames` | 0.2 a second | nothing paints: a stray frame in a 5 s span at most |
| `requests` | 1 a second | nothing asks for a frame |
| `timers` | 0.5 a second | Lachlan's update-status tick (every 20 s) and little else |
| `tasks` | 2 a second | that tick's few Lua runs, and the cli listener answering a connection now and then |
| `task_ms` | 1 ms a second | what those take |
| `gui_thread_ms` | 2 ms a second | the GUI thread asleep: 0.2% of a core |
| `cpu_ms` | 5 ms a second | the GUI process asleep, its other threads included: 0.5% of a core |
| `server_cpu_ms` | 3 ms a second | the mux server asleep |
| `conpty_cpu_ms` | 2 ms a second | the conpty hosts asleep, nothing printed |

Only a scenario that repaints on purpose has budgets of its own: `cursor-blink`, whose frames are its blink's (1.25 a second at `cursor_blink_rate` 800, measured 1.2 to 1.4; 2 allowed), and whose CPU budgets are what a cursor's cell changing should cost (4 ms of the GUI thread a blink, 5 ms a second), not the 13 to 18 ms a blink it costs now (finding 14). And `conductor-pad-minimized`, `conductor-pad`'s window with its first pad's arc turning too, minimized: no loop (`loops` 0), since one would keep DWM composing every refresh for a window nobody can see (since 2026-10-31; the installed build 20261030-191144-301f18e6 kept one playing, the outlines' hues, and failed it).

### The PC while it measured

Since 2026-11-03 (Lachlan: "the tests should take into account that the system is sometimes busy or at a different speed"). The PC the runner measures on is rarely quiet: 8 or more Claude sessions, a browser and the other agents' builds took 14 to 40% of every CPU while this was written. A test window runs at below-normal priority on its warden job's CPUs, so whatever else wants them comes first, and the processor's clock moves with the load: one CPU kept busy read 3.5 GHz (the turbo cap), CPUs busy an eighth to a quarter of the time 1.3 to 1.9 GHz, since Windows runs a lightly loaded core slowly. A number measured by cycles counts time at the nominal 3.0 GHz whatever the clock was, and the same work takes 2.7 times as long at 1.3 GHz as at 3.5, so what LBTerm costs moved with what else the PC was doing.

So around every span and every action's run, `Start-PcWatch` and `Stop-PcWatch` (harness.psm1) read what the rest of the PC did, and `ConvertTo-PcSpan` (budgets.psm1) makes it a sample's `pc`:

| What | How |
|---|---|
| `cpus`, `of` | how many CPUs the test could run on (the warden job's, by the GUI process's affinity) and how many the PC has |
| `own` | the CPUs' worth the measurement's own processes took: the runner, the GUI, its mux servers and everything they started (`TreeCycles`: by parent pid, a child started after its parent), so a pane's program (a ticker, a full-speed writer) is the test's and not another's |
| `others` | the share of the test's CPUs' time the rest of the PC took: their busy time (NtQuerySystemInformation's processor times, every processor's whatever the job's affinity) less `own` |
| `pc` | the share of the whole PC's time busy, everything included |
| `clock`, `ghz` | the test's CPUs' clock over the stretch as a share of the nominal one and in GHz: PDH's `% Processor Performance` (Task Manager's speed, the clock while a CPU runs, above 100 with turbo), each CPU's weighted by how busy it was |

`Add-PcSample` lays it on the sample and scales what's measured by cycles by the clock (`gui_thread_ms`, `cpu_ms`, `state_ms`, `server_cpu_ms`, `conpty_cpu_ms`, `frame_ms`, and an action's four), so each says what it would have taken at the nominal clock and a run with turbo holds up against one without; what was measured stays in the sample's `raw`. Times (`ms`, `gui_ms`, `task_ms`, `pass_ms`) aren't scaled: they're waits as much as work. The history's lines say `clock: "nominal"` since then, and a drift isn't measured from a line without it for what's scaled.

budgets.json's `pc` rules say when a span or run was measured on a busy PC: `others` over `others_max` (0.5: with others on half of the four CPUs' time, all four are taken at once about one moment in sixteen) or `pc` over `pc_max` (0.75). One that was is taken again, up to `retakes` (3) more a scenario, while fewer than `-Runs` were calm. The scenario is judged on its calm ones while at least half its `-Runs` were; with fewer it's judged on all, and over budget is then `busy`, not `fail`: over, but on a PC too busy to say it's LBTerm's. `busy` doesn't fail the run (exit 0) and the summary counts it: run it again when the PC is quieter. The table's last two columns are the judged ones' medians of `others` and the clock, and a scenario with busy spans or runs says how many and why.

The first whole suite measured this way (Lachlan's E5 of 2026-11-03), on the installed build 20261103-011219-de955813, 19:03 to 19:44, unlocked, the new-tab, gpu-memory and fonts-core tracks' agents working beside it: others took 8 to 38% of its CPUs (median 23%), the clock was 2.4 to 3.5 GHz (median 2.9), and 3 spans or runs in the whole suite were busy. 65 within their budgets, 6 over and 3 not measured:
- `conductor-view` (finding 12) and `busy-scroll` and `busy-triggers-scroll` (`task_ms` 52.8 and 56.4 ms a second against 46 and 48, a time, not scaled) over as in every run since 2026-11-01;
- `cursor-blink` over on its tasks alone (4.58 a second against 4), its CPU now within (the GUI thread 2.94 and the process 4.31 ms a second at the nominal clock, against 5 and 8);
- `four-windows` over on timers (0.59 a second against 0.5) for the first time: its spans read 0.17, 1.17 and 0.59, the update-status tick of each of four windows (every 20 s) and the slow log's waker (every 5 s), 0.4 a second on average, so a span catching several windows' ticks goes over;
- `conductor-view-opening` over on its 95th percentiles alone, one run of 15 at 68 ms (the next slowest 21, the median 8.9);
- `close-pane` timing out waiting for the pane gone after F3, as in every run since 2026-11-01 21:57 (it passed at 20:37); `one-tab-local` and `projects-card` stopped by windows of Lachlan's that opened during them (Chrome, the tray's overflow).

The drift it said was against the last runs of each scenario, perf/gpu-memory's builds of 2026-11-01: `private_mb` moved in 41 scenarios (`twelve-tabs` 289 to 542 MB), since main doesn't have gpu-memory's smaller GPU allocations yet.

### Two builds against each other

`lbterm.ps1 budgets -Against <dir> [-Rounds 5]` holds `-Bin`'s build (after) against the build in `<dir>` (before; a copy of the installed build for a branch's before and after, its top-level files, as the track rules say): each scenario `-Rounds` times on each, before first in the odd rounds and after first in the even, so a PC getting busier or hotter over the run falls on both alike (`Invoke-BudgetComparison`). Each build is judged on its rounds' spans or runs together; each round is a window of its own, so how a build's rounds vary is what a window, its placement and the PC at the time add, which the runs inside one round can't show.

`Compare-BudgetRounds` holds each metric's difference to that, by Welch's test on the rounds' own values (each round's median or 95th percentile): the difference of their means and its 99% interval, said as `+-99%`, the half width. A difference whose interval leaves out nothing is `higher` or `lower`, else `same`, and `same` is "not told apart with this many rounds": the half width is about the smallest difference the run could have told. A difference of no more than half a count of what counts whole events (frames, messages, missed refreshes; at rest half an event over a span) is `same` whatever the rounds say. With fewer than two rounds of either build a difference is `unknown`. report.json in `target\budgets\<run>-vs`, a table a scenario (both builds' PC, then each metric either measured something of: before, after, the difference, the half width and the verdict), what was told apart, and a history line for each build, the before first. The exit is the after build's, as a plain run's.

Why that test. The first rule held a difference to the wider of the two builds' ranges from round to round. Run on one build against a copy of itself (20261103-011219-de955813, `one-tab` and `spinner-tall`, two rounds each, 2026-11-03 18:03, others on 11 to 30% of its CPUs), it called 4 of 22 metrics different: `one-tab`'s messages (0.69 against 0.78 a second, its rounds 0.012 apart: under one message in a 5 s span), `spinner-tall`'s p95 and the GUI's p95 (each round's slowest run of 15, 18.5 against 14.4 ms) and the mux server's CPU a run (0.81 against 0.96 ms). Simulated on identical builds, a range rule calls a metric different 22% of the time at two rounds and 6% at three, and sees less the more rounds it's given, since a range only widens; Welch's test at 99% calls one different under 1% of the time at any number of rounds, and sees a shift of three of a round's standard deviations 80% of the time at 5 rounds, of two at 8. Five rounds of the same pair under Welch's test (18:12, others on 10 to 16% of its CPUs, 8.2 min) called all 19 metrics the same. There `spinner-tall`'s echo was +-1.91 ms: a round's median echo varies about 0.9 ms from one window to the next, so 10 rounds tell apart about 1.2 ms either way and 20 about 0.8, and a lean of 0.6 ms (PITFALLS 71's) is under what a run of any practical length can tell. When it comes out `same`, say the branch moved it by less than the half width, not that it moved it by nothing. And a copy of the installed build isn't the same speed as a slot's build of the same commit: perf/gpu-memory found `throughput-local` 0.5 MB/s apart between them ("What a window's GPU memory holds"), so a difference against the installed copy is the branch's only once it holds against the branch's own slot's build of main's code too, and a verdict holds once a second run says it again (PITFALLS 78).

Both builds read the runner's config, which `dofile`s Lachlan's live `lbterm.lua`, so a build older than a key his config has since gained can't load it: its windows run on their built-in config beside a Configuration Error window of the same process, and nothing in the comparison says so (PITFALLS 177). On 2026-11-07 that was every after round of perf/mux-client's close-pane stage, about 265 MB more private bytes in every scenario and a message or two more, until main was merged in; the next run, on 2026-11-08, had the two builds' private bytes the same in all nine scenarios it ran. A difference of a whole window's size in every scenario at once is that before it's anything else: look for `Configuration Error` in the round's window log (its pid in the round's pids.txt).

#### The rounds paired

Each round's two builds run back to back, so a test on each round's difference (after less before) takes the PC's swings from round to round out of the comparison, where Welch's counts them as each build's spread (PITFALLS 113: perf/gpu-memory's `atlas-refill`, 5.5% later in 15 rounds of 16, +-22.1 paired against Welch's +-29.2 and `same`). It pays only where both builds' rounds swing together, and costs where they don't: a t-test on n differences has n - 1 degrees of freedom where Welch's has nearly 2n - 2, so at 5 rounds its 99% interval is 4.604 standard errors against Welch's 3.355, with the same standard error when the two builds' rounds move apart. Held to this PC's comparisons so far (19 runs of `budgets -Against`, 2026-11-03 18:31 to 2026-11-04 04:27, every metric past its floor whose rounds both builds kept whole, rounds paired by position: 400 metrics, 341 of 5 rounds and 59 of 10), the paired interval was the narrower in 42% and the wider in 58%, the median paired over Welch's half width 1.05 (the 10th percentile 0.73, the 90th 1.53), because the two builds' round values move together little: their correlation round by round had a median of 0.17 (the 10th percentile -0.59, the 90th 0.77). Told apart at 99%: Welch's 73, the rounds paired 69. Paired alone: `atlas-refill`'s ms (the rounds correlated 0.79), `throughput-unicode-local`'s private bytes (0.79), `start-local`'s CPU (0.63) and `spinner-tall`'s paint (0.42); Welch's alone, 8, their rounds correlated -0.75 to 0.25. "Either test at 99.5% each" (under 1% false on identical builds, as either at 99% alone) told apart 71: 3 Welch's missed, 5 it caught lost.

So Welch's test decides, and the rounds paired are shown beside it (Lachlan's 1a of 2026-11-04, picked between that, either at 99.5%, and the paired deciding): `Compare-BudgetRounds` pairs each build's round by the round it was judged in (`Invoke-BudgetComparison` gives each its `round`), and the comparison has `paired_n`, `paired_diff`, `paired_within`, `paired_verdict` and `paired_later` (the rounds the after came higher in) beside Welch's; the table a `paired` column (its half width and what it would call it), and under "told apart" what the rounds paired alone tell apart, "not a verdict"; the report's summary counts them (`paired_only`). A lean the paired test alone tells apart is a lead to run again, or with more rounds, not a result.

Each cell is a round's median over its three spans, this branch's two rounds, then the installed build's two. Every scenario came out the same in all four rounds: 21 within their budgets and 3 over, on both builds alike. conpty hosts spent 0.01 ms a second at most anywhere; the state file's writer, left out of the process's, 2.6 to 3.3 ms a second with a blinking cursor, 9 to 10 with the Conductor's view sending and none at rest.

Within the budgets, a resting window spends next to nothing on its GUI thread (0.02 to 0.14 ms a second) and 1.6 to 3.0 ms a second in all, most of it on two of NVIDIA's D3D12 driver threads (`nvwgf2umx.dll`, about 1.0 and 0.4 ms a second, named by the module they started in) and notify-rs's file watcher (0.14 ms a second). Each scenario's first span is higher (2.7 to 5.8 ms a second for the process): upstream's `update_checker` thread spends about 13 ms in a window's first seconds, though `check_for_updates` is off. On a mux domain the GUI runs 0.77 tasks a second at rest, the cli listener's (`wezterm-mux-server-impl/src/local.rs:24`) and none locally, and a timer each 5 s (`window/src/spawn.rs:53`, the slow log's waker). With metrics, this branch's counts in a resting scenario: no frames, no repaints asked for, no messages, 0.4 wakes a second on a mux domain, none locally.

Three are over, the same on both builds, each a finding below (12, 14, 15): the Conductor's view with its seat sending, a blinking cursor and the tasks card. The other track's fix of the Conductor's view was what `conductor-view` waited on; `conductor-view-quiet` shows the view itself costs nothing while the seat says nothing. Since that fix came in all three are still over, each for less ("The whole suite after main's fixes came in", below): the view still paints for every line its seat sends.

The GUI process's private bytes, said and not yet judged: 525 MB for one tab locally, 541 to 567 MB on a mux domain, and 1,851 to 1,879 MB for four windows of one GUI, about 440 MB a window (finding 16).

### An action

A scenario of `kind` `action` is something done to the window and timed, then costed: set up once (`perf/budgets.ps1`'s `$Actions`, a window like a scenario at rest's, given the keyboard), then done its `runs` (15 by default, `actions.runs`) after its warm-ups (`warmup`, 1), each from a window at rest. A run is, in order:

1. what it needs first (`Before`: a tab for a close to close), then the window left until nothing has painted for 300 ms, then what it starts from read off the state file (`Mark`: the cursor, the open tab, where the pad is);
2. `metrics` reset, the process's frame count, the cycles of the GUI thread, the process, the state file's writer and the mux servers, and the time now (`Get-UnixMs`, Unix milliseconds with the fraction);
3. what starts it, posted (`Do`): a key, a click, a move, `SetWindowPos`, a cli call;
4. the frames the recorder kept gone through in order for the first after it whose state shows it (`Shown`), its `time_ms` the time the action took (`ms`), then the first after that with nothing animating (`settled_ms`); which frame after the action that was (`shown_frame`, 1 the first) and when the first ended (`first_frame_ms`) are said too, the first judged only where a scenario gives it a budget (its 95th percentile, the slowest of 15 runs: the picker's and the pick's, held to 1, the frame after; a count of frames, which a loaded PC doesn't move as it moves milliseconds, Lachlan's word of 2026-11-03); a cli call is timed to its return instead;
5. the window left until it's quiet again (no frame for 300 ms), then the cycles, the frame count and `metrics` again: what one cost (`gui_thread_ms`, `cpu_ms` less the state file's writer, `server_cpu_ms`, `frames`, `messages` and among them `WM_PAINT`'s), and the GUI's own time for it where it times it (`gui_ms`, from `budget.actions`); what the GUI process and the mux servers hold then (`private_mb`, `server_private_mb`); and where its time went by upstream's timed histograms, each one's count times its mean (`spent_ms`, said, not judged);
6. put back (`After`): the tab closed, the menu shut, the pointer gone.

The state file's `time_ms` is taken as the frame ends, after its present (render/paint.rs), on the same clock as `Get-UnixMs` (the system's precise time, in .NET and in Rust's `SystemTime`), so the time is from the post to the frame being on its way to the screen, to the millisecond; what DWM and the screen add after is part 4's. The recorder (`Start-FrameRecorder`, a FileSystemWatcher on the state file's folder that reads each snapshot as it's renamed into place) keeps every frame the writer wrote; the writer only drops one when a newer is handed over while it's still writing (lbterm/STATE.md).

| `key-echo-load`, `key-echo-load-local` | the same, a writer at full speed in the pane beside it (lines of 104 bytes, 64 KB a write, no pause; its title says each second how much it wrote, `writer_mb_per_s`): latency under load. | `repaint` | the window told to paint (InvalidateRect: no input, no focus) over two panes of text it has drawn | its next frame; `lines_laid_out` (`metrics`' `caches`, every action's) held to none | |
| `spinner-tall` | `X` posted to a program that writes its last line again in place for each key, as Claude Code's spinner is, in a window like Lachlan's (1080 by 1920, a screenful of coloured lines, the Conductor's pad accepting most and its outlines round two rows) | the cursor moved (`drawn_cursor`) | `key` |
| `atlas-refill` | a key to a writer, which prints a screen of CJK ideographs the glyph atlas doesn't hold (six screens from U+4E00 in turn) and then sets its title; its window made 55 rows tall, so a screen (about 3,200 glyphs) is more than half the atlas and less than all of it, which every run fills, clears and fills again (`atlas_rebuilds`, held to at least 1). Each glyph is staged in an upload buffer of its own on its way to the atlas, so this is where the GPU allocator's upload blocks show (PITFALLS.md, trap 77) | the title | |

A start's cost is its whole cost to its window quiet, from nothing: the process's cycles, the GUI thread's and the server's.

Where the GUI times the action, each run also says, from `metrics`' `present` ("What's hard to time", below): from its message's arrival to its frame's paint starting (`paint_ms`), and once the frame is seen going up, to the refresh it went up at (`screen_ms`, with `screen_p95_ms`), from the post to that refresh (`post_screen_ms`) and from its presents to it (`after_present_ms`); and for any action, the motion it played: its refreshes, those that came without a frame (`missed`, which every action is held to 0 of), when its first frame went up (`motion_first_ms`), and the GUI's paints meanwhile longer than a refresh (`slow_paints`, the longest `motion_paint_ms`). None of those is there for a run whose window others hid (`screen_hidden`, `motion_hidden`), so it isn't judged on them.

### The budget runner's actions: budgets and what they measured

Five rounds of every action, interleaved: this branch's build (20261030-030119-e6903ac1) at 04:03, 04:26 and 04:50, and the installed build (20261029-213152-f9d36b5e, copied to a scratch folder; it has no metrics, so no GUI's own time or messages, but the state file, so the same times, frames and CPU) at 04:15 and 04:38, 2026-10-30, each round 11.6 to 12.1 minutes. The PC was locked throughout (LogonUI running); at each round's start no cargo or rustc ran, and 2 to 4 `lbterm-gui` processes did (Lachlan's daily one and other sessions' test windows). In each cell, this build's three rounds, then the installed build's two; each value is the round's statistic over its runs (15, a reload's 8, a start's and a throughput's 6, after one warm-up), in milliseconds but frames, messages and MB/s. GUI thread, process and mux server are the CPU one run cost, from just before it to the window quiet again; a start's is all it spent from nothing.

The two builds come out close: in most cells this build's three rounds and the installed build's two overlap. The one steady difference is a new tab on a mux domain, 131 to 133 ms of the GUI thread on this build against 122 and 125, and 49 to 55 ms to show against 47 and 48; with three rounds against two that may be the time of night, and it wasn't looked into. In one round each of `tab-by-click` and `inbox-opening`, the installed build's 95th percentile was one run of 134 and 211 ms.

The budgets (budgets.json, each with its why): one and a half times the slowest of this build's three rounds, its median for a median's budget and its 95th percentile for a p95's, rounded up, with floors (10 ms for a time, 16 for a p95, 2 ms of the GUI thread, 3 of the server's, 2 frames); a throughput's least is its slowest round's over 1.5. Every action has them for its time to the frame, its p95, the GUI's own time and p95 where it times it (which the window's `lbterm_budget` is given too, so a run over it is said in its log with what the time went on), the GUI thread, the process, the mux server and the frames. Where what was measured is the bug, the budget is what it should be, and the scenario fails until it's fixed. These were the budgets until main's fixes came in; since then they're the merged build's, set the same way ("The whole suite after main's fixes came in", below):

- `key-echo`: a key's echo on a mux domain is held to the local's time (35 ms, p95 80; finding 7), and both key scenarios to what a key should cost: 10 ms of the GUI thread and 20 window messages, what stage 1 measured a key costing with the WM_PAINT loop fixed (2.4 to 5.3 ms a frame, about five messages a key; findings 1 and 23), not 34 to 44 ms and 275 to 316 messages.
- `cli-list`: the server's CPU is held to one walk of the PC's processes for all twelve panes, 60 ms, not 527 to 616 (finding 22).
- `start`: a cold start on a mux domain is held to showing its tab within the local's budget and 300 ms (1,900 ms), not 2,751 to 2,770 (finding 25).
- `throughput`: output on a mux domain is held to the local's speed and time (at least 4.5 MB/s, 3,500 ms, p95 3,700) and its GUI thread to the local's (2,200 ms), since on a mux domain the server parses the output (finding 24).
- `picker` and `pick` (feat/new-tab, 2026-11-04, two rounds on its build, 01:10 and 01:11, the desktop unlocked, another track's GPU job running beside it): each held to the frame after (`shown_frame`, 1 in all 30 runs of each), and as the others to 1.5 times the slower round. The picker: 2.29 and 2.34 ms to its frame (p95 4.47, 3.06), the GUI's own 2.09 and 2.15, 3.9 and 3.7 ms of the GUI thread, 2 frames. A pick: 4.58 and 5.73 ms to the Starting row (p95 8.52, 7.85), the GUI's own 4.69 and 5.53, 28.2 and 29.1 ms of the GUI thread and 33.2 and 34.0 of the server's with the tab it opens, 8 and 9 frames (lbterm/NEW-TAB.md has the rounds of the actions a new tab touches, before and after). Those rounds didn't show "no regression", as the stage said: key-echo's medians were 7.41 and 7.22 ms before against 8.59 and 9.19 after, and the review's 8 interleaved rounds (02:17 to 02:22, the lock held, the desktop locked) 7.07, 6.99, 6.67 and 6.80 against 7.97, 7.66, 9.43 and 8.26, p95 9.1 to 14.3 against 17.2 to 21.4, with the same GUI-thread CPU a key, the pad on or off alike, and rail-opening's GUI time 12.9 and 13.1 against 14.7 and 14.4 ms, all within their budgets (PITFALLS 116). The before was the installed build, not a slot's build of main's code (PITFALLS 78). Held then against a slot's build of main's code (2404cfc7f; five rounds, 2026-11-04 04:00 to 04:27, the lock held), with main merged into the branch: key-echo 6.69 to 7.04 ms on a mux domain (+0.34, +-0.56, `same`, three of five after rounds above every before), 6.20 to 6.17 locally, rail-opening 161 to 159, its GUI's own 10.7 to 9.27, all `same`: the gap was the old main's, and a lean of 0.4 ms a key on a mux domain is what's left, which neither test tells apart, so `-GuiMs 1` wasn't run. `picker-first`, the cold first open, 24.5 to 26.8 ms with two projects (three rounds, 04:31 to 04:35), budgeted at 1.5 times; the warm picker 1.89 to 2.07, a pick 4.79 to 5.16 (lbterm/NEW-TAB.md has the rest).
