A key's echo starts its frame's paint 17.5 to 17.7 ms after the key: the frame the key asks for itself, before the pane has echoed, holds the echo's back a pacing period (finding 35); the paint and its presents then take 4 to 5 ms. With a writer beside it, 24 to 25 ms, and a local one's presents end 23 ms after its paint starts, against 6 on a mux domain (finding 44). The writer meanwhile wrote 11.1 MB a second on a mux domain and 10.4 locally. A tab switch, a close and the InMail list start painting within 0.6 to 4.2 ms and take 4 to 10 ms to present; a split and a new tab spend 24 to 54 ms before their frame's paint even starts, the spawn's.

Every action with motion opened one episode a run, and all of them were covered. The GUI's paints while they played: none longer than a refresh of the 60 Hz screen in any run of any action; the longest in a run's median 3.8 ms (a tab switch, a close, the InMail list) to 9.0 ms (a writer's output on a mux domain).

`present.ps1`, run by hand between the rounds (both variants passing, all hidden): a key alone, from its arrival, painting at 17.5 to 17.6 ms and presented at 21.7 to 21.8 ms; with the writer beside it, painting at 22.9 (local) and 25.7 (mux), presented at 38.0 and 32.1.

## What the cards cost

The cards (the projects card, the objectives list and its detail card, the strip's detail card, the tasks card), the InMail button's list, the tab menu and the tab panel are drawn by the paint pass, which builds every layer's quads again for every frame the window paints, whatever made it paint. The compositor draws and presents only the visuals whose quads changed (lbterm/COMPOSITOR.md, "Only what changed is drawn"), so what an unchanged card cost was on the GUI thread, building it: one left open while a Claude session streamed in the open tab was laid out again, and every word in it shaped again with HarfBuzz (`compute_element` has no cache), 16 to 120 times a second, for a picture the compositor then threw away as the same. Since 2026-10-31 (perf/lbterm-layers) the work happens when what's shown changes.

### What's kept

- **Words.** Every card's, list's and the panel's words are kept from the frame before by what each was laid out from: its text, colour, place, z-index, font and cell size (`render/words.rs`, its `Words`, on `lbterm_extras::kept`'s `Kept`). A frame lays out only the words that are new and drops those it didn't draw. All of them go when the shape generation moves (a config reload, `InvalidateShapeCache`, the glyph atlas made again, the GPU lost), the DPI or the window's size changes. Their quads are kept too, while the words are the same words in the same places, and drawn all into one allocator (`lbterm_render_words_into`, render_element's text branch for plain words, where each word took render_element and an allocator of its own: three mappings of the layer's vertex buffers). The tab menu and the tab panel's relayout take kept words the same way (`lbterm_take_word`).
- **Parts kept as quads** (`render/kept_quads.rs`). The tab panel's ring, its glow and lit gradient, the card, the open tab's marker, the running light's two discs, the rows' pills and pads, their parts on the open panel, the bars' fills and the words are each made once into a heap (`HeapQuadAllocator`), turned into their vertices and the hashes the compositor compares (quad.rs, `lbterm_vertices`), and copied into the layer's vertex buffer in one go every frame (renderstate.rs, `lbterm_extend_all_hashed`). Each is made again only when what it's made from changes (a hash of its layout, colours and how lit it is), or the shape generation, the quad generation (a resize), the window's size or its z-index.
- **The projects card** makes its projects' folder keys once per list of projects (`lbterm_extras::projects::Folders`), where it made all 47 again for each of about 90 asks a frame, counts the mail again only when the inbox's list of messages is another, and is laid out again only when its counts or its place change; it was built twice a frame.
- **The InMail list** is laid out once and kept by what it's laid out from (the messages, the projects, the day, the scroll, the fonts and its place), where it laid itself out every frame and again for every mouse event in the window, each time asking which project every message's folder is in by making every project's folder key again, and the paint asked a second time.
- **The rail's rows** are hashed in two looks, the closed rail's and the open panel's words' (`lbterm_panel::look`, lbterm/TAB-PANEL.md), where every row was formatted with `{:?}` and hashed each frame: while the panel is closed a new status, message or age no longer dissolves a row the rail shows the same. The rail's tabs are listed once a paint, for its draw, its plans, its progress, its marker and its glints, where each listed them again.

`metrics` replies with the words laid out and kept (`words`) and the parts made and kept (`quads`), and the budget runner's busy scenarios hold `words` to none a second: in every busy sample of the build with this, none was laid out.

### What it measured

The budget runner's busy scenarios (above), on 2026-10-31, 06:09 to 06:22, in two rounds of each build interleaved (this branch's build at 142081980, then the installed build, 20261030-191144-301f18e6, then the same again), three spans of 5 s each a round, the PC locked, with other sessions' builds running beside them (cargo and one to five rustc) and up to two other test windows. The installed build is main before perf/budgets, so its numbers come without the counters (and without `pass_ms` and `words`, which need them); the counters cost less than a run's own spread (What they cost). Medians of the six spans, the range after:

| Scenario | GUI thread a frame, before | after | GUI thread ms a second, before | after | pass a frame, after |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `busy-one-tab`: nothing open | 5.12 ms (4.97 to 5.26) | 3.74 (3.69 to 3.82) | 103.3 | 75.1 | 1.24 ms |
| `busy-tabs`: fifteen tabs, 47 projects, 70 messages | 5.52 (5.33 to 5.62) | 4.34 (4.12 to 4.58) | 110.9 | 87.8 | 1.63 |
| `busy-projects-card` | 17.91 (17.56 to 18.65) | 4.58 (4.42 to 4.79) | 360.9 | 92.2 | 1.82 |
| `busy-inbox-list` | 47.13 (46.20 to 47.59) | 4.61 (4.34 to 4.99) | 949.8 | 92.6 | 1.73 |
| `busy-rail-open` | 5.55 (5.33 to 5.96) | 4.24 (4.08 to 4.59) | 111.5 | 85.1 | 1.57 |
| `busy-strip-card` | 5.51 (5.32 to 5.70) | 3.99 (3.81 to 4.06) | 111.1 | 80.2 | 1.34 |
| `busy-objectives` | 6.05 (5.89 to 6.73) | 4.00 (3.93 to 4.22) | 122.1 | 81.6 | 1.40 |

Both builds painted 19.9 to 20.5 frames a second in every span. With the InMail list open the installed build's GUI thread was spending 95% of a core: 47 ms a frame at 20 frames a second. The mux server's CPU is the same on both (55 to 72 ms a second, the ticker's output), and so is the conpty host's.

Where a frame's time went, from a profile of the paint pass kept only while measuring (200 frames of `busy-tabs`, the build before the parts were kept as quads against the build after, before the rows were): the pass from 3.7 to 2.3 ms a frame, the tab panel's share of it from 2.2 to 0.9 ms, its glow from 214 to 54 µs, its lit gradient from 407 to 66, the card from 263 to 56, the running light from 454 to 95 and its words from 222 to 150 µs. Keeping the rows, their parts on the open panel and the bars' fills took about 0.17 ms more off every frame with fifteen tabs.

The mouse, with the InMail list open (`perf/inbox-mouse.ps1`, 400 moves posted along one row, so nothing changes on the screen): on the build before, 8,562 to 9,194 µs of the GUI thread a move (3.4 to 3.7 s for the 400), against 37 to 40 µs a move with the list shut; on this branch's, 34 to 49 µs a move with it open and 38 to 44 with it shut, the difference a median of -2.6 and -1.8 µs in its two runs (-9.3 to 5.7 in the six rounds). Two runs of each build, three rounds each, interleaved, 06:25 to 06:27 on 2026-10-31, other sessions' test windows opening beside them. No move within the row painted a frame on either build, and on both the list's focus followed the pointer down its 23 rows in view. At 8.7 ms a move, 115 moves a second were the GUI thread's whole time.

What it draws is what it drew: every visual's texture in a window's first two frames (`LBTERM_DUMP_FRAMES`, read from the GPU, so it works with the PC locked), a window of one tab drawn as Lachlan's with the panel closed, was the same to the byte on both builds: the panel's body, cap and its glow and light, glow, lit gradient, marker, rows, row words, running light, spray and wave, the strip's (`over`), the cards' edges and what's under the panes. Its open words and bars drew nothing there, closed; only the pane's first frame differed, pwsh's start-up a moment apart, and its second was the same. The open panel and the cards weren't compared: that's the pixel tests', below.

### What's left

- A kept part is still copied into the layer's vertex buffer every frame, and the compositor hashes it to learn it's the same: about 0.3 ms of a `busy-tabs` frame for the glow, the lit gradient and the running light's discs alone. The compositor could leave an unchanged part's layer out of the pass altogether, as it does the wave's.
- A row's words the closed rail doesn't show still repaint the window when they change: `perf/status-storm.ps1`'s words case paints 32 frames (lbterm-panel's `claude.rs`, which perf/at-rest is changing too).
- Each paint still reads every tab's panel state and copies its user vars (about 90 µs with fifteen tabs).
- `compute_element` still has no cache of its own, so what isn't kept yet lays out and shapes every frame it shows: the prompt list, the Conductor's menu and a link drag's cards (a voice draft's card and the questions' markers and ticks are kept since the lines' stage, What a line costs, below). A glyph's key hashes its whole text style (perf/fonts-core's).
- The pixel tests of what these draw (`rail-labels`, `rail-feedback`, `objectives`, `inbox`, `strip-summary`, `strip-card-fade`, `tab-links`, `tab-mouse`, `rail-tasks`, `pad-waiting`) rest on captures, which a locked PC leaves DirectComposition's content out of; they weren't run.

## What a line costs

Every line a pane draws goes past LBTerm's own looks at it before its cached quads can answer: the triggers' rules (lbterm/TRIGGERS.md), the thumbnails' look for a picture's link (lbterm/THUMBNAILS.md) and, for a line holding a link, whether the link under the pointer is another. Over the panes a voice draft's card and the question colours (lbterm/CONTROL.md, "Drafts"; lbterm/QUESTIONS.md) looked at their pane's whole screen, and the easter egg's hearts moved. Each did its work on every frame the window painted, whatever made it paint. Since 2026-10-31 (perf/lbterm-layers, its second stage) each does it when what it looks at changes.

### What's done when

- **Links** (finding 61). wezterm's two line caches (the quads, and the shapes they're made from) took the pointer on no link, before and now, for a change of what's under it (`same_hyperlink` is false for two nones), so every line holding a link missed both on every frame unless the pointer rested on a link, and all of them were laid out again whenever it moved from one link to another. Each cache entry keeps the links its line holds now (`lbterm_links`), and a line is laid out again only when which of its own links is under the pointer changes (`lbterm_extras::link_looks::hover_changed`). A line's cells are looked through for a picture's link only when it holds a link drawn by state (`link_looks::by_state`, found once with its shape hash), where every line with any link was looked through each frame.
- **Triggers** (finding 62). What a line matched was kept by its seqno, but each frame copied every line with a match, recoloured it and hashed it again (the copy never kept the shape hash it was given, its appdata), and told what it matched to `Seen` again, a String a match into a HashMap; a changed line was read as a String a cell. Now what a line matched is kept with its recoloured copy until the line changes or its row goes out of view, so an unchanged line costs a lookup and its copy's hash serves it from the line caches; a changed line's text is made once (`lbterm_triggers::LineText`); and `Seen` hears of a line as it comes onto the screen and as it goes, its places keyed by where they are without a copy of the text. The `lbterm-trigger` Lua event runs only when the config has a handler: Lachlan's has none, and each new match was a task, a window handle and Lua's arguments for nobody.
- **What's new to the triggers** (finding 62) was wrong three ways, which the measuring found: a match on the screen was remembered for ten seconds from the last frame that drew it, so a window at rest that long took it for new at its next frame (love's hearts again for a key typed elsewhere); a match moving a row with its line, as Claude Code's fullscreen view scrolls, was new at each row; and a new line scrolled into a row with its match at a column another's had been in that row was the same match. Now what's new is settled once a frame has drawn its panes (`Seen::settle`): a match is in its line's place (that text without the blanks after it, the rule, the column) and its row's (the text before it in that row), each counting how many of the pane's lines on the screen have it, and it's new when both counts went up over the frame, a place gone within ten seconds taken back first. A line scrolled a row keeps its line's count, a reply streaming on in its row keeps its row's, and a place stays seen while it's on the screen and ten seconds after. For the first version of this, a line of the same text as one anywhere on the screen was the same line, so the same line printed again while the first showed (`echo love` twice) played nothing; the review of 2026-10-31 found it, and counting made it new again. A matched line's kept copy is coloured again when its pane's palette changes (OSC 4, 10), which the copy made each frame had followed.
- **Hearts** (finding 64). The GUI painted the whole window every 16 ms for a burst's two seconds to move seven hearts: 67 frames. DWM plays them now: their picture is drawn once into a sheet, each heart is a visual showing its pink's square of it, and its rise, sway, growth and fade are handed to DWM once as it rises (`lbterm_triggers::hearts::Path::tracks`, within a tenth of a pixel of where the GUI drew it at every millisecond; `compositor.rs`'s `prepare_hearts`). A burst paints 3 frames: the key's, its first, and the state file's frame as DWM's animations end. Each frame says where each pane with a burst in the air is, so a burst follows its pane and is cut to nothing while the pane isn't shown (`hearts::follow`), as the GUI's drawing has them; the first version left a burst where its pane had been and over another tab (747 pink pixels on the other tab in `perf/triggers.ps1`). DWM plays them only with motion on: with it off, which the default `'system'` is on a PC with Windows' animation effects off, the GUI draws them frame by frame as before, where the first version drew none.
- **A voice draft and the question colours** (finding 63). Each frame a draft showed, its pane's whole screen was read again (every line copied out of the mux client, a glyph made of each cell), the input box found in it, each answer's question colour looked up in the session's summary (a copy of the summary each time) and the card's words shaped; and the colours were laid out again for any output in the pane, its status line's turning included. Now the screen is read again only when the pane has changed and shared between them (`render/questions.rs`'s `lbterm_screen`), the box found again only in a screen read again, the card's lines made only when the draft or the questions change, the layout kept while the conversation is the same (`lbterm_extras::questions::conversation_hash`), and the card's, the bands' and the markers' shapes and words kept (render/kept_quads.rs, render/words.rs).

### What's left

- On a mux domain every visible line is copied out of the mux client three times a frame and its shape hash computed again (on a copy, so it's never kept), plain text included: busy-lines' 26 lines cost about 1.0 ms of the pass a frame over busy-one-tab's (findings client1 and core-1, the mux client's). A line a trigger matched is cheaper there now than a plain one, its kept copy's hash served.
- busy-links still costs about 0.9 ms of the pass a frame over busy-lines', with no line laid out again. Likely what's left: on a mux domain the hyperlink rules are run over a fresh copy of each visible line every frame (the mux client hands out copies), and a line with a URL in it gets its link made again and its cells spread out each time (finding core-8, perf/fonts-core's). Not measured apart.
- A fullscreen view scrolling still matches, recolours and hashes each changed line: busy-triggers-scroll's pass is 0.3 to 0.5 ms over busy-scroll's, about 15 µs a changed line. That's work for what changed.
- A line printed again just as its twin leaves the screen (in the same frame, or within ten seconds of it) keeps the line's count where it was and isn't new.
- A burst handed to DWM is cut off at its pane's top, where the GUI's drawing kept a heart whole until it was all above the pane.

## What a shell's marks cost

At a shell the scroll strip marks each prompt in the open tab's scrollback (lbterm/SCROLL-STRIP.md, "A shell's marks"), up to 50,000 rows with Lachlan's `scrollback_lines`. Its look was written to read only the rows changed since the last, and a long scrollback to cost once, but neither held: every frame with output went through every row, and on a mux pane, which every tab of Lachlan's is, the first look fetched the whole scrollback from the server and marked none of it. And the strip's list of prompts read all of Claude Code's history.jsonl for each click. Since 2026-10-31 (perf/lbterm-layers, its third stage) each reads what changed.

### What's done when

- **A look** (finding 65) reads the rows that went into the scrollback since the last look (they were on the screen then and may have changed before they went) and the screen's rows changed since; a row in the scrollback isn't written again, so it's read once (`lbterm_extras::marks::plan`). A frame with output used to ask for every row from the scrollback's first changed since the last look: locally a walk of the terminal's 50,000 lines, on a mux pane 50,000 lookups in the client's line cache, each counting a row the cache didn't hold as changed.
- **The first look**, or one after a new width rewrapped the scrollback and numbered every row again, reads the screen at once and the scrollback a chunk of 5,000 rows at a time between frames, newest first (about 7 ms a chunk locally), with one frame after the last for what they found. It read all 50,000 rows in the frame that first showed the tab: 146 ms locally. A row is a prompt when its zones say so, but its cells are looked through first, once, for a prompt cell at all, so most rows make no zones (`mux/src/lbterm_prompts.rs`): that alone took the local first look from 146 ms to 81 before it was spread out.
- **A mux pane** is read from the lines the GUI holds as the server has them, looked at in place without a fetch (`wezterm-client/src/pane/lbterm_held.rs`), and the server is asked which rows of the rest hold a prompt, a chunk of 5,000 at a time with one ask under way (`GetPromptRows`, PDU 7503; the answer, 7504, is the prompt rows alone, a few bytes). The first look used to fetch every row the GUI didn't hold in one GetLines, the server's main thread copying and compressing 50,000 lines while every tab's output waited, the GUI making a blank line for each until they came (85 MB) and then putting each through the hyperlink rules; and what came back was no newer than the look, so it was never read and nothing was marked (7 of 100 prompts, those on the screen). Rows that scrolled past between the server's pushes were fetched again on each frame of output: 3,000 GetLines in 10 s of a line every 50 ms, each answer a frame of its own.
- **A look after a long run of output** a tab printed in the background (more than a chunk went into the scrollback since its last look) reads those rows a chunk at a time between frames too, and on a mux pane asks the server for them straight away (`marks::split_since`). The review of 2026-10-31 found it read them all in the frame that showed the tab: 80 ms locally after 50,000 rows, 17 ms on a mux domain, looking through the client's held lines for the few it had.
- **A server of an older build** (one still running when a newer GUI is installed, until its last tab closes) answers ErrorResponse naming the PDU it doesn't know: the GUI says so once in its log and marks the prompts it has had on its screen. Checked with this branch's GUI on the build before's server: the warning once, no fetch, output going on. Any other error asking it (a pane gone as it's respawned, a connection made again) has its rows asked again at the first look a second on (`marks::server_lacks_prompt_rows`), where the first version took any error for an old server.
- **The prompt list** (finding 66) and the strip's jump to the last prompt read Claude Code's history.jsonl on from where the last click's read stopped (`lbterm_extras::prompts::History`, one for every window), where each read the whole file (2 MB and 6,600 lines of 1,200 sessions on Lachlan's PC) and went through it again; a file written anew or shorter is read from its start. A jump waiting for Claude Code to draw reads the pane's rows again only when its sequence number moved, where it read them every 25 ms, or while a row it read was still on its way from the mux server (a fetch that completes moves no sequence number; `lbterm_holds`), and the screen a voice draft's card and the question colours keep is read again for the same reason. What's kept of the history is where each session's records are, 16 bytes a record, not the prompts: a click reads its session's back from the file.

### What's left

- The mux server went through every row of a pane's scrollback for each push it sent (upstream's `PerPane::compute_changes` asks for the rows changed from row 0): busy-scrollback's server spent 87 to 105 ms a second against busy-shell's 66 to 76 in the rounds above. Main's perf/mux-server, merged into this branch after them, finds a screen's changed lines without reading every line (`term/src/lbterm_changes.rs`): on the merged build busy-scrollback's server spends 65 and 81 ms a second against busy-shell's 62 and 71 (13:15 and 13:25), so busy-scrollback is held to the busy scenarios' 110 again.
- The prompt list lays itself out and shapes its words for each frame it shows (stage 1's "What the cards cost", what's left): 14 to 17 ms of the GUI thread for its two frames.
- The first look at a long local pane still reads every row once, 70 ms of the GUI thread in ten chunks between frames, and again after each new width; on a mux pane that's the server's, 75 to 100 ms of its main thread in ten asks.
- A GUI newer than its server marks only what it has drawn until the server restarts.
## What font setup costs

The audit of 2026-10-30 found three things in how LBTerm's fonts are made (fonts-3, fonts-6 and fonts-5, its names), and the first benchmark against other terminals (perf/bench-terminals, lbterm/BENCH.md there) a window taking 1.2 to 1.3 s to show, of which finding 5 put about 0.9 s on WebGpu's start. Each was taken as a lead, looked for in the code and measured before anything changed, on the installed build (20261030-191144-301f18e6, copied to a scratch folder) and this branch's, in test windows drawn as Lachlan's, the PC locked throughout (LogonUI running), 2026-10-31 04:40 to 06:15.

### What it did, counted

A test window with `WEZTERM_LOG=wezterm_font=trace`, its lines counted after each step: the system font files GDI's lookup copied and parsed (`Got .. from gdi`), fallback searches (`Looking for .. in fallback fonts`), shapers built again with a new fallback (`revised fallback`) and harfbuzz's shapes (`info_clusters`). The fallback line is ⏺ ✻ ⎿ ⚠, two Han and two Hangul characters, in the pane and its tab's title.

| Step | files read, installed / this branch | searches | shapers again | shapes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the start | 14 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 45 / 45 |
| a reload of the same config, twice | 11 and 11 / 0 and 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 28 / 24 |
| a zoom a size bigger, and back | 11 and 11 / 0 and 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 48, 32 / 34, 33 |
| the fallback line | 4 / 0 | 7 / 3 | 6 / 6 | 260 / 135 |
| a reload with it on screen | 11 / 0 | 4 / 0 | 3 / 0 | 212 / 24 |
| a zoom with it on screen | 11 / 0 | 4 / 0 | 3 / 0 | 237 / 103 |
| a new window | 11 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 45 / 45 |

All three findings held:

- **fonts-3, font setup from nothing on every save, zoom and window.** `config_was_reloaded` threw away every font, the title's, the rail's labels' and the Conductor's sizes, then `apply_scale_change` dropped them again and made a new glyph atlas, whatever the save changed. Each style was found again through the system's font locator, and for a family Windows hasn't installed (the bundled JetBrains Mono, and Noto Color Emoji and Symbols Nerd Font Mono after it in every style; the window frame's font names Symbols Nerd Font Mono twice) DirectWrite missed, GDI selected a substitute, and its whole file was copied out (`GetFontData`) and parsed with FreeType only to be turned down by name: 11 files a save, 14 at a start. The window's caption font was read the same way at every reload. The bundled fonts were parsed for every font configuration, a window's and the throwaway one a new window's size is worked out with, which turned out cheap (1.6 ms: FreeType opens them lazily).
- **fonts-6, each font searching for fallbacks on its own.** The pane's regular font and the title's searched for the same characters, each find sent `InvalidateShapeCache` (the window's every shape cleared), and the next shape with that font rebuilt its shaper and threw `ClearShapeCache`, which ended the paint pass part-way to clear and shape again; a reload or a zoom made the fonts again without what they'd found, so it all happened again.
- **fonts-5, a full atlas thrown away whole** (the audit's verdict was plausible, unmeasured). It is: a full atlas is made again at the same size, every glyph rasterized again and every shape with it, and the pass that filled it runs again; and a window's first atlas was 128 px a side, cleared before each doubling. A first screen of 500 distinct characters in three styles (`atlasexp`, a scratch script, four rounds on one build, the start size set apart): from 128, three atlases made (one cleared, two grown) and 307 to 331 ms of the GUI thread, the longest pass 207 to 218 ms; from 1024, none, 238 to 272 ms and 150 to 175 ms. How often a long-running window fills its atlas wasn't known, and still isn't: `metrics`' `fonts.atlas_cleared` now counts it in every LBTerm, Lachlan's included.

### What changed

- **A reload of the same font settings keeps the fonts** (`config::lbterm::font_key`: every font, its size, the cells' metrics, the glyph cache's settings, erring on the side of more, and the bidi settings, which give a run the direction the kept shapes were made in, since the shape cache's key has only the style and the text), with what they found as fallbacks, the glyph atlas and the cluster shapes (`shape_cache`), so a save lays the lines out again for its colours and nothing else; `FontConfiguration::lbterm_config_changed` says whether it kept them, and a save that changes the fonts goes the old way. So does a save after a file in the font dirs was added, removed or written (`FontDirs`, walked at each reload only when the config has `font_dirs`, which Lachlan's hasn't): upstream read the font dirs again at every reload, so a font put there was used after the next.
- **GDI is asked the selected face's name** (`GetTextFaceW`) before its file is copied, and a substitute is turned down there (`fonts.substitutes`); the caption font is kept while its LOGFONT and dpi are the same (`parse_log_font`); the bundled fonts are parsed once a process.
- **Fonts share what their searches found** (`wezterm-font/src/lbterm.rs`'s `Found`, one for every font configuration with the same `config::lbterm::fallback_key`, which is where a search looks and how it picks, not which fonts or sizes, and the same files in the font dirs, so it lasts through a zoom, a font size changed and every window of the process). A character another font searched for is answered from it when this font meets it and the text shaped again with the faces that search found for it, with no search, no completion and no reshape; a character answered so between two of `shape_impl`'s passes takes its faces on the next; a request queued behind another's search for the same characters is answered from it on the fallback thread; and a font's own find joins it without `ClearShapeCache`, since the search's completion has already cleared the window's shapes once. A font takes only the faces of characters it meets: the first build gave a font made later every face found so far, and drew some symbols from another face than main does (Which face draws a fallback, below). A character searched for that no face had is searched for again after a reload that makes the fonts again (`Found::forget_missing`), as upstream's fresh fonts did, so a font installed in Windows since is found then (DirectWrite's system collection sees it through the font cache service); a reload that keeps the fonts, a zoom or a new window doesn't search again for it, where upstream did at every reload, so until the fonts change or LBTerm starts again such a character stays missing.
- **The first atlas is 1024 a side** (`config::lbterm::ATLAS_START`, 4 MB), a later window's the largest any window of the process has had, and an atlas that hasn't seen a frame through grows instead of being cleared first. That largest only rises, so once a window's atlas has grown, every later window, and every window rebuilt after a lost GPU device, starts at its size, in the GPU's memory (4 bytes a pixel): 16 MB at 2048 a side, 64 MB at 4096 and 256 MB at 8192, a window. Nothing caps it, since Lachlan leans to speed (a cap where a new window starts, 2048 say, is a line in `counters.rs`'s `atlas_start`, and such a window would grow its atlas once, drawing that frame again). How far his window's atlas grows in a day isn't known yet: `metrics`' `fonts.atlas_side` says it once this is installed.
- **The GPU's instance and adapter are made once a process** (`termwindow/gpu_warm.rs`), begun on a thread of their own once the start isn't handed to a running LBTerm (`main.rs`'s `run_terminal_gui`, after `Publish::try_spawn`: begun before it, a second lbterm-gui that handed its request over and exited made a D3D12 device on every adapter for nothing), and taken by every window. That moves the adapter's choosing off the GUI thread; the first frame still waits for it, so taken out alone it made the first frame no sooner (A window's start, below), though this branch's whole start against main's came about 160 ms sooner on 2026-11-03 (Against main's build of 2026-11-03); a lost device forgets them, so its rebuild chooses again (a driver update can take the adapter with it). wgpu's checks of indirect draws and dispatches, which LBTerm never makes, are off: each device compiled two compute pipelines for them as it was made (`WGPU_VALIDATION_INDIRECT_CALL=1` turns them back on).
- **What it costs is counted, always**: `metrics`' `fonts` and `start` (lbterm/CONTROL.md), and `lbterm::start` at debug says each step of a window's start, its GPU state's and the adapter's, and each compositor surface made.

### What it costs now

The budget runner, two rounds of each build interleaved (before, after, before, after) and then two more, 6 to 15 runs a scenario after a warm-up: the medians of each round, the installed build's first. Other sessions' builds ran beside rounds 2 and 4 (2 to 4 cargo and 1 to 5 rustc processes), and 1 to 4 other test windows beside every round.

| Action | to the frame, installed | this branch | GUI thread, installed | this branch |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `reload` (F5, over twenty lines and a title with symbols) | 226.6, 230.6, 312.0, 279.9 | 73.7, 109.0, 74.8, 79.2 | 262.9, 263.1, 349.9, 317.0 | 71.7, 105.0, 74.4, 73.5 |
| `font-zoom` (a size bigger or back, over the same) | 285.1, 293.0, 338.0, 326.3 | 190.8, 184.0, 209.9, 217.0 | 318.9, 319.1, 363.4, 351.7 | 198.0, 203.7, 198.8, 200.9 |
| `fallback-line` (a new script in the pane and the title; two rounds of the script that prints in UTF-8) | 102.5, 109.6 | 69.7, 59.2 | 121.4, 135.4 | 104.9, 91.9 |
| `new-window` | 447.4, 772.0, 456.1, 557.7 | 382.0, 418.1, 519.0, 462.4 | 302.3, 446.2, 314.6, 383.4 | 282.0, 290.5, 335.8, 317.0 |
| `start-local` (to its first frame) | 947.0, 1664.3, 943.7, 966.1 | 801.9, 771.7, 958.2, 965.1 | 789.8, 1145.9, 786.2, 768.0 | 432.4, 436.4, 516.3, 507.5 |
| `start` (on a mux domain, to its first frame) | 933.5, 1326.4, 914.1, 996.8 | 780.6, 883.6, 964.5, 809.4 | 1017.9, 1341.1, 979.1, 1064.8 | 567.9, 591.8, 712.8, 564.1 |

This branch's counts in every run: a reload made no font, read no file, searched for nothing, made no atlas and cleared no shape; a zoom made 3 fonts and 1 atlas and searched for nothing; a new window made 3 fonts and 1 atlas, read no file and searched for nothing; a fallback line searched once and cleared the window's shapes twice (the pane's font's find and the title's font handed it). A config save word for word (`perf/config-save.ps1 -Measure -NoCount`, twelve tabs on a mux domain, no symbols, two rounds interleaved, the save after the window's first): the GUI's main thread 59.7, 56.9 and 55.2, 53.9 ms a save on the installed build, 29.0, 30.2 and 29.0, 26.8 on this branch; the GUI process 91.6, 87.4 and 86.8, 84.5 ms, and 68.9, 70.7 and 66.0, 61.6.

What a zoom still costs is mostly not its fonts. Around one step in a plain window (`zoomprobe`, a scratch script, four steps): the GUI thread 78 to 109 ms, of which the frame's draw 75 to 77 ms (the compositor's surfaces made again at the window's new size: each `surface.configure` waits for the GPU to be idle and makes its swapchain, finding 79), its pass 22 to 25 and the fonts 8 (three made).

### Which face draws a fallback

Sharing what searches found changed which face draws some glyphs, which the counts above can't see: the review of this branch (2026-11-01, unlocked, two rounds of each build in warden GPU jobs) printed a bold line of Hangul, cleared the screen, zoomed a size bigger and back (which makes the fonts again), then printed a regular line of symbols none of the bundled fonts has (★ ☆ ※ ① ② ◆ ◇ ♠ ♣ ♥ ♪ √ ∴ ≒ 〓). Main's build drew that row the same with or without the Hangul first. This branch's first build drew ※ ① ② ≒ 〓 from the Korean face the bold font had found (718 pixels, x 80 to 346, the same in both rounds), because every font made after a search started with every face found so far, after its own, and the first face in a font's list that has a glyph draws it. Without the Hangul it matched main pixel for pixel.

A font now takes a found face only for a character it meets (`shape_impl`, through `Found::split`), so a zoom, a new window or another style makes its fonts with their own faces alone, as upstream did. `perf/fallback-faces.ps1` holds it by pixels: three windows, each its own process, the same steps ending in the symbols; the one after a bold line of Hangul must draw them as the one with nothing before.

What's still shared, for Lachlan to decide: a character one font has searched for, when another font meets it, is drawn from the face that search picked for it, without a search of its own. A search picks for its characters as a set (DirectWrite maps runs of them, then the faces are sorted by how many of the set each covers, `sort_fallback_fonts_by_coverage`), so a character searched for beside others can get another face than when searched alone. `fallback-faces.ps1`'s third window shows it, said and not judged unless `-Exact`: a bold line with the Hangul and the symbols together, then the regular symbols. On main each font searches for itself, so there the regular font's symbols come out as they do alone; with the sharing they come from the faces the bold font's search picked. Either way a font's own earlier finds decide too (by the code, not measured): on main, a font that met Hangul first keeps the Korean face it added and draws ※ from it, and the same font made again by a zoom, without the Hangul on screen, searches ※ alone and may not. Keeping the sharing draws a character from one face across a window's fonts, its zooms and its windows, and a zoom or a new window searches for nothing found before (a zoom over the fallback line: 4 searches and 237 shapes on the installed build, 0 and 103 here, What it did, counted); searching per font as main does costs each font its own search, and each find the window's shapes cleared again.

### A window's start, step by step

`metrics`' `start` on this branch, a local window from nothing (`startexp`, a scratch script, five rounds of each of four variants of one build, interleaved): `main` reached its config's end at 62 to 68 ms (Lachlan's `lbterm.lua` about 43 ms of it), the first tab at 108 to 251 ms (its ConPTY and pwsh about 45), and the window began then: its fonts under 0.1 ms, the operating system's window 57 to 64 ms, its GPU state from 175 to 335 ms, which waited for the process's adapter (217 to 377 ms: the warm-up, begun at 62 to 141 ms, took 308 to 524 ms, nearly all in choosing the adapter), then its device (64 to 117 ms), its swapchain (4 to 8) and its pipeline (26 to 74); its render state 9 to 25 ms. The first paint began at 542 to 804 ms and ended 170 to 270 ms later: the pass 35 to 56 ms, the draw 118 to 198, of which the compositor's 27 surfaces made 126 to 169 ms. The state file's first frame came 746 to 1,108 ms after the process was created. (The warm-up begins about a millisecond later now, after `build_initial_mux` and the hand-off to a running LBTerm, which it came before when these were measured.)

Taking each change out in turn, the same build (medians of five): with everything in, the first frame at 912.5 ms and 442.8 ms of the GUI thread; without the warm-up, 881.7 and 737.5; with the indirect checks back on, 801.5 and 439.3 (the device 108 ms against 78); with the atlas at 128, 777.7 and 418.2. So the GUI thread spends 290 to 300 ms less on a start, always, and its device 30 to 40 ms less; but the first frame comes no sooner than its adapter, which the warm-up thread chose in 339 to 344 ms in two of the five rounds (the frame at 714 and 715 ms where without the warm-up it came at 817 to 849) and in 417 to 458 ms in the other three, beside the GUI thread's own start, which ate the gain. Two rounds of `start-local` came out 145 and 892 ms sooner, two the same. An empty window's first frames fit an atlas of 128, so its size changed nothing at a start; it's the first screen of text that pays (fonts-5, above).

What LBTerm can still change, largest first:

1. **The adapter.** wgpu chooses one by making a D3D12 device on every adapter DXGI lists (wgpu-hal's `enumerate_adapters` and `Adapter::expose`: the RTX 4080 here, and the software adapter, WARP, which wgpu keeps, if DXGI lists it on this PC, which wasn't checked), 308 to 524 ms, and only then picks by power preference. An adapter picked by `IDXGIFactory6::EnumAdapterByGpuPreference` and made alone needs wgpu-hal's `Adapter::expose`, which isn't public. Beginning it before the config is read (63 ms sooner) means guessing the backends the config will ask for.
2. **The compositor's surfaces** (finding 78): 27 swapchains at a window's first frame, 3 to 15 ms each whatever their size (a 1 by 1 one 3.2 ms), 126 to 169 ms in all, and every one made again when the window's size changes. The second face of each part (the strip's, the rail's: there for dissolves) and the spares could be made when first needed, at the cost of a few ms at the first dissolve.
3. **The device and pipeline, once a process.** Each window makes its own (64 to 117 ms and 26 to 74 ms); a new window could take the first's, which needs `gpu_lost.rs` to rebuild every window on one loss.
4. **A mux domain's server**, started only after ten tries at its missing socket over 2.25 s (finding 25), which is the whole of `start`'s `ready_ms` (2,748 to 2,835 ms).

What it can't: the time from the process's creation to `main` (about 30 ms here, an 83 MB executable), and the NVIDIA driver's own device setup.

## What printing costs

The audit of 2026-10-30 found three things in how the terminal model prints (core-3, core-8 and core-12, its names), and the first benchmark against other terminals (lbterm/BENCH.md, on perf/bench-terminals) that wide characters and emoji show locally at 4.6 MB/s with 7.7 s of GUI CPU for 32 MB, the slowest of its patterns, and that 100,000 lines of scrollback cost LBTerm about 20 MB more than upstream WezTerm with the same setting. Each was taken as a lead, looked for in the code and measured before anything changed: through the model on its own (`term/tests/lbterm_print.rs`, below), in test windows (the budget runner's throughputs, three new pairs of actions, `perf/scrollback-memory.ps1`), and where the time goes, by sampling the test window's threads (`perf/sample.ps1`), on the installed build (20261030-191144-301f18e6, copied to a scratch folder), this branch's font stage (14c1d3522) and this stage's builds. The PC was locked throughout (LogonUI running), 2026-10-31 06:40 to 08:15, and other sessions' builds and test windows ran beside most of it (each table says).

### Through the model

`term/tests/lbterm_print.rs` feeds the model the budget runner's output patterns as the mux's parser thread does: parsed 128 KB at a time into actions, then performed on a terminal of 110 by 30 with Lachlan's scrollback of 50,000, full before it's timed; 16 MB a pattern, release, its allocations counted by a global allocator of the test binary's own, per thread. With `LBTERM_TERM_BENCH=<file>` set, `lbterm.ps1 test -Package wezterm-term` writes the numbers to the file. Before is the tree before this stage (the model as the installed build has it); after, this stage's last; one run each, 06:50 and 07:16 (later runs under heavier load came out 20 to 30% slower all round, sgr's parse too, which nothing here touches).

| Pattern | parse, ns a byte | perform, ns a byte | the model, MB/s | perform's allocations a MB |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| plain lines (the throughput writer's) | 21.1 → 3.0 | 53.3 → 6.3 | 12.8 → 102.1 | 30,881 → 30,841 |
| Claude Code's 24-bit look (`rgb`) | 19.2 → 13.3 | 72.2 → 15.7 | 10.4 → 32.9 | 492,331 → 33,266 |
| the same in palette colours | 28.2 → 14.6 | 34.7 → 15.9 | 15.2 → 31.3 | 18,803 → 18,746 |
| dense SGR, a colour a cell (lbterm/bench's) | 18.1 → 18.8 | 8.8 → 8.6 | 35.5 → 34.8 | 4,139 → 4,139 |
| wide characters and emoji (lbterm/bench's) | 21.8 → 20.1 | 34.2 → 31.3 | 17.1 → 18.5 | 42,138 → 42,090 |

The parser's own allocations a MB of plain lines went from 51,509 to 10,376. A walk of a line's cells (what drawing, the shape hash, zones and triggers do): 19.0 ns a cell for plain lines with upstream's iterator (08:02) and 15.9 with the ASCII path (07:58, a run whose every other number, sgr's parse among them, came out 30% slower than the 08:02 run's), Unicode's 23.3 and 28.2 the same way, so no slower within the runs' spread.

### The findings, measured

- **core-3, a heap block a cell for 24-bit colours and links: held.** A pen with a 24-bit colour keeps it in `fat`, a `Box`, and `flush_print` copied the pen for every character: a line of 100 such characters printed with 104 allocations against 3 in a palette colour, and a copy of a line held a cell at a time with 101 (a box a cell). Claude Code's look performed at 72 ns a byte with 492,331 allocations a MB, twice its palette twin's time.
- **core-12, printing a grapheme at a time: held, larger than its estimate.** Plain lines performed at 53 ns a byte (the audit guessed 1 to 2 ns a character less): each character segmented, measured, its pen copied and written with a write's bookkeeping, and the parser's string pushed again a character at a time; the parser itself 21 ns a byte, a byte at a time through its state table and a call each. Two of its parts weren't worth changing: the two locks a TermConfig lookup takes (`TermConfig::with_config`, `scrollback_size`, `bidi_mode` and `Configuration::get` together 0.5 to 0.9% of the parser thread's samples in local Unicode output), and vtparse's 2 KB array an escape dispatch zeroes (none in the patterns measured: ConPTY ends its titles with BEL). `ClusteredLine::new`'s 80 bytes stay: a line printed a grapheme at a time grows from them.
- **core-8, a line with a link left a cell at a time: held.** `Line::scan_and_create_hyperlinks`, which the mux client runs on every line that reaches it, left a clustered line with a link in cell storage with a box a linked cell: a scanned line of Claude Code's look copied in 21 allocations and 2,527 bytes, against 2 and 249 for one with no link (`apply_hyperlink_rules`, the local pane's, already compressed it again); now it copies in 2 and 318. The scan itself costs more than the audit thought: 33 to 40 µs a line with Lachlan's eight rules, 30 times its estimate; the three bracketed URL rules 0.2 µs each (a literal to look for first), the five with Unicode word boundaries 4 to 10 µs each, and their regexes' `is_match` alone as slow, so it's the regex engine and not the captures. Not changed here: links per line are the LBTerm layers' track.
- **The scrollback's 20 MB: not the scrollback.** In a window with a config like lbterm/bench's and its scrollback of 9,001 lines, 100,000 lines grew the installed build's private bytes 66.4 MB and the font stage's 57.5 MB; with the four line caches at 16 entries, 24.0 and 16.0. It was the caches of what the GUI drew, full at 1,024 lines each, and of them the quads: LBTerm's corners and gradient (`BoxedQuad`, the rail's light and a rainbow trigger's glyphs) were inline in every quad, 220 bytes where upstream's are 84. It grew the same whether 9,001 lines were kept or 100,000, so it never was the lines.
- **Unicode, locally: the GUI and the parser taking turns on the pane's lock.** Sampled (`perf/sample.ps1`, 10 to 12 s of `throughput-unicode-local`, every sample a thread's whole stack, waits included): on the font stage's build the parser thread spent 32% of its samples parked on the pane's lock, 28% waiting for the console host's output and 40% working (performing 25, parsing 14); the GUI thread 29% parked on the same lock, 26% asleep, and the rest painting, which holds the lock for a local pane's whole paint (`LocalPane::with_lines_mut`): the shell scroll strip's prompt search (`lbterm_scroll_sought`, its zones computed for every line it walks) 25 to 35% of its samples, the triggers 14 to 17%, the rail's titles 9 to 13%, drawing the lines 32 to 39% and the hyperlink scan 2.5 to 4%. On this stage's the parser works faster and waits for the console host less (15%), but still waits on the lock a third of its time (31%), and the GUI's paint is what it was. So Unicode shows locally at 4.9 MB/s against 4.7, where the model prints it at 18.5 and the console host takes it at about 18 on a mux domain: the rest is the GUI's paint, held under the pane's lock, and the scroll strip, the triggers and the rail are the LBTerm layers' track. On a mux domain it's the client pulling a busy pane's lines (finding 24).

### What changed

- **Shared fat attributes** (`wezterm-cell`): a cell's 24-bit colours, link and images are an `Arc`, copied only when one sharing them changes them (`Arc::make_mut` in each setter, which leaves them alone when the value is the same), so every cell a pen writes holds the pen's block and a line's copy copies none; `clone_sgr_only` shares a block that holds only colours.
- **The pen by reference** (`Line::set_cell_grapheme_ref`, `Screen::set_cell_grapheme_ref`, `ClusteredLine::append_grapheme` taking `&CellAttributes`): copied only where a clustered line starts a cluster with it.
- **A run of ASCII at once**: in the ASCII set, outside insert mode, a run of printable ASCII goes to its line a stretch at a time up to the right margin (`lbterm_print_ascii`, `Line::set_cells_ascii`): appended to a clustered line's end as one piece of text and at most one cluster, or written over its cells with the bookkeeping (links and zones forgotten, its sequence number, a wide character cut before it) once; the cursor, the pending wrap, autowrap off and the margins as a character at a time leaves them, which a test holds case by case.
- **The parser's runs**: vtparse hands a run of printable ASCII in the ground state over at once (`VTActor::print_ascii`), the escape parser gives it as one `PrintString` when asked (`Parser::lbterm_print_runs`: the mux's parser thread and the model ask; its other callers and its tests get a `Print` a character as before), the performer takes a `PrintString` whole, and a `PrintString` the parser starts has room for a line.
- **A scanned line stays clustered**, and a link's cells share one block.
- **A clustered line's cells are walked** without the grapheme segmenter for printable ASCII followed by ASCII.
- **A quad keeps LBTerm's corners and gradient apart.** This branch put them behind one pointer (96 bytes a quad); main's feat/switch-calls, landed first, did the same with two (`BoxedQuad`'s boxed `corners` and `gradient`, 104 bytes, held by a compile-time assertion, c0a0eb9d7), and the merge keeps main's: one way, not two. The numbers below were this branch's 96.

### What it costs now

The budget runner, two rounds of each build interleaved (the installed build, this stage's, the installed, this stage's; 07:34 to 08:02), the medians of each round's runs after a warm-up, the installed build's first. At the rounds' starts: in the first, another session's cargo and rustc (beside this stage's round), in the second, the same beside the installed build's and four other test windows beside this stage's.

The Unicode rows went the other way in these rounds, both of this stage's beside other load, so they were run again on their own, `throughput-unicode-local` three rounds of three builds interleaved (08:05 to 08:14, other test windows and at times another session's build beside every build): the installed build 3521, 3381 and 3417 ms (4.56, 4.75, 4.70 MB/s; the process 4739, 4535, 4555 ms), this stage's 3278, 3231 and 3265 (4.90, 4.97, 4.92; 4424, 4405, 4432), and the build before the parser's runs 3212, 3272 and 3186 (5.00, 4.91, 5.04; 4221, 4355, 4207). So locally Unicode is 5% faster for 6% less CPU, and the first rounds' numbers were the load's. On a mux domain its runs spread from 5.9 to 16.2 s in one round, the server's main thread answering the client's `get_lines` for 1.5 to 7.2 s of it (finding 24); its parser thread took 2.3 s the run on the installed build and 2.5 to 2.9 s on this stage's beside four other test windows, where the model's own numbers have it a little faster. The parser's run check was made to look at two bytes before it counts a run after these rounds, so other text pays a compare a byte; two more rounds of the three builds interleaved, this stage's last build among them (08:23 to 08:32, other sessions' builds and test windows beside most), had it the same as the build before the change: `throughput-unicode-local` 3378 and 3410 ms on the installed build (4.75, 4.71 MB/s; the process 4547, 4605 ms), 3266 and 3283 on the last (4.92, 4.89; 4409, 4434), 3294 and 3249 on the one before (4.88, 4.94; 4428, 4379); `throughput-local` 2062 and 2066 ms (7.61, 7.59 MB/s; 2587, 2584 ms) against 739 and 737 (21.2, 21.3; 872, 867) and 744 and 721 (21.1, 21.8; 884, 847). The installed build is over every budget `throughput-local` has now.

`perf/scrollback-memory.ps1` (800,032 lines, a config like lbterm/bench's, scrollback 9,001), interleaved with the rounds: the installed build grew 70.4 and 72.7 MB (322 and 346 frames), this stage's 37.9 and 38.3 (69 and 88 frames), and in the earlier probes 39.3 against the font stage's 64.1, where upstream WezTerm grew 35 MB in lbterm/BENCH.md's run. With the four line caches at 16 entries, the first 100,032 lines grew the font stage's build 16.0 MB and this stage's first build 14.9: what this stage's grows above WezTerm's 35 is about 3 MB.

## What upstream's metrics cost

The audit of 2026-10-30 found upstream's metrics recorder three times (fonts-7, paint-12 and events-10, its names): a thread waking every second to read a setting that's off, and a global lock and a hash lookup taken by every record, a few a line every frame, one a glyph and one a task, for histograms nothing read. Each was taken as a lead, looked for in the code and measured, on the build before this stage's changes (this branch at 16a0824f0, with main's perf/at-rest merged in: "before" below) and this stage's, in test windows drawn as Lachlan's, 2026-10-31 08:50 to 09:45, the PC locked throughout (LogonUI running) and one to three other sessions' cargo or rustc beside most of it.

### The findings, measured

- **The stats thread waking every second: fixed already.** `Stats::init` started a thread that slept a second at a time to read `periodic_stat_logging` and find it 0 (the default, and Lachlan's), for the GUI's whole life. Main's perf/at-rest has it asleep until a config reload while the setting is off (lbterm/AT-REST.md, its table of threads), merged into this branch before this stage, so nothing was left to do here.
- **A lock and a lookup a record: held.** metrics 0.23's `histogram!` and `counter!` ask the recorder for the handle on every call, and upstream's recorder (`wezterm-gui/src/stats.rs`) answered by locking its one mutex, hashing the key's name and labels with SipHash for a `HashMap` and cloning an `Arc`, before the histogram's own mutex (and for a `.rate` key the clock) took the record. Counted from `metrics`' histograms in a window of two panes of 61 lines that stay still (`perf/line-cache.ps1 -Measure`): 369 records a frame, three a line (the line state cache's hit, from the shape hash's touch of it, the line quad cache's hit and `quad_buffer_apply`) and five a frame. A line laid out again adds `render_screen_line`, the line-to-element and shape caches' lookups, `cached_cluster_shape` and a glyph cache hit or miss for each glyph; every main-thread task records `executor.spawn_delay`; every PDU two to four, with a label list built for each; and every read of a local pane's output `read_from_pane_pty.bytes.rate`, on its parser thread, under the lock the GUI thread's records took. What reads them: `metrics`' `histograms` (every one; the budget runner's `spent_ms` the eight largest timed ones a run, and `perf/metrics.ps1` `gui.paint.impl`), Lua's `wezterm.metrics` and `periodic_stat_logging`, neither of which Lachlan's config uses (read 2026-10-31). Nothing but those two read the caches' `.hit.rate` and `.miss.rate`.

### What changed

- **Each thread keeps its handles** (`lbterm_extras::recorder::Handles`, a thread-local in `stats.rs`): a key the thread has recorded before is found by its own hash, which a key with a fixed name works out once and keeps in its static, with no lock; the recorder's maps are asked only for a key the thread hasn't seen. Every histogram and counter is still there and read as before.
- **The caches count what they did** (`lfucache::counts`): each cache name's hits, misses and entries put out to make room, a relaxed atomic add each, every window's caches of a name together and the glyph cache's as `glyph_cache`, said by `metrics` under `caches` ("What the caches keep", below), where each lookup recorded a `.rate` histogram. Lua's `wezterm.metrics.get_throughput` no longer has them.

### What it costs now

- **A record**, by `recorder`'s test (a window's records for 20,000 frames: 60 lines with three a line, two a frame and a PDU's label built for its call, 183 a frame; upstream's lookup copied into the test, the same record behind both): 105.0 ns through upstream's lookup (three runs: 104.8, 105.0, 119.5), 55.0 through a kept handle (53.5, 55.0, 56.4), 18.3 with no recorder at all (18.3 each). A cache's count is a relaxed atomic add.
- **A frame of lines the window has drawn**, two panes of still text:
  - `perf/line-cache.ps1 -Measure` (122 lines, the window made to paint 50 frames a second for 10 s by InvalidateRect, three runs a round, two rounds of each build interleaved, 09:27 to 09:30): the GUI thread 7.527, 7.526, 7.760 and 7.498, 7.562, 7.671 ms a frame before; 7.525, 7.389, 7.304 and 7.448, 7.327, 7.566 after. `paint_pane.lines` a pane 1.290 to 1.390 ms before, 1.228 to 1.290 after.
  - The budget runner's new `repaint` (a frame asked for at a time, 15 runs a round after a warm-up, three rounds of each build interleaved, 09:33 to 09:36): the GUI thread 5.10, 4.97 and 5.01 ms a frame before, 5.04, 4.95 and 4.94 after; `paint_pane.lines` for both panes 1.11, 1.03 and 1.03 ms before, 1.01, 1.02 and 1.02 after.

  About 0.1 ms of the GUI thread a frame, 1 to 2% and near the runs' own spread, where 370 records each 50 to 150 ns cheaper would give 20 to 55 µs. Finding 89 has what the rest of such a frame costs.
- **Output** (the budget runner, two rounds of each build interleaved, 09:38 to 09:42, one or two other sessions' cargo beside every round): `throughput-local` showed its 16 MB in 744 and 739 ms before (21.1, 21.2 MB/s; the GUI thread 684 and 676 ms, the process 881 and 867), 696 and 698 after (22.6, 22.5 MB/s; 626 and 632; 830 and 827): the GUI thread 8% less, where the parser thread recorded each read under the lock the GUI thread's records took. `throughput-rgb-local` 3135 and 3222 ms before (the GUI thread 1510 and 1544), 3212 and 3144 after (1519 and 1455): no difference past the runs' spread.

## What the caches keep

The audit's fonts-8, which its checker called plausible: upstream's LFU caches (`lfucache`: a window's line state, line quad, line-to-element and shape caches, 1,024 entries each in upstream and 4,096 in LBTerm since main's feat/switch-calls, `config::lbterm::default_line_cache_size`, and the glyph cache's 256 decoded images) put an entry in with a count of nothing and take the least frequently used out first, so a tab shown for the first time could have its lines put out by each other.

- **The rule: held.** In a cache whose entries have all been looked up many times lately, an entry put in is the least frequently used, and the next one put in puts it out. lfucache's test, 64 entries looked up 100 times each in turn, then rounds of 32 new keys each put on its miss: every new key missed again for five rounds and 29 of 32 in the sixth.
- **A window: not seen.** The decay upstream added (each eviction divides the least recently used entry's count by a tenth of the lookups since its last use) takes any line seen fewer than about a tenth of a cache's length of lookups to nothing, since a full cache's least recently used entry is at least its length of lookups old, and a count of nothing goes out first. So a cache gets there only when every entry in it was seen many times within a few of its lengths of lookups: a window showing nearly as many lines as its caches hold. In a model of a window's frames (17 to 40 tabs of 60 lines, each shown 5 to 400 frames in turn, then a new tab; lfucache's test) a new tab's lines missed in its first frame only, and so they did in a test window: `perf/line-cache.ps1`, 18 tabs of a screen of text shown in turn twice at 50 frames a second, the caches full of lines seen a hundred times or more, then a tab never shown, which laid out its 61 lines in its first frame and none in the 146 after it on the build before the change.
- **Changed all the same**, since the rule is wrong where it applies and putting it right is small. An entry used within the latest half a cache's worth of lookups (a few frames' lines) is kept whatever its count, the least recently used going instead while it wasn't; and of the least frequently used the least recently used goes first, so an entry whose count has just decayed to nothing goes before the ones just put in, where it went after them. The same rounds miss in the first only, however the cache was warmed; the model and the window as before (in the window, 61 lines and then none in 150 frames). The tick is 64 bits and the decay's divisor capped at `u16::MAX`, where 32 bits went round after days of a busy window and the cast wrapped past 655,360 ticks.
- **Tabs visited in turn**: in the same window every revisit of the second round laid out its whole screen again, before the change and after (61 lines a visit): 18 screens of 61 lines don't fit in 1,024 entries, and the caches' sizes are the feat/switch-calls track's (finding 90), which has since made them 4,096 by default on main; `perf/line-cache.ps1` sets upstream's 1,024 to fill them in a test's time.

`metrics`' `caches` says each cache's hits, misses and evictions in every LBTerm. The line state cache's misses aren't counted: it's looked up only while a line still holds its entry (a weak link), and a line whose entry went is hashed again and put in, which shows as the put's eviction. In the test window before the new tab, on this stage's build: the line quad cache 217,770 hits, 2,196 misses and 1,172 evictions; the line state cache 217,770 hits and 1,172 evictions; the line-to-element cache 35, 2,161 and 1,137; the shape cache 43,951, 157 and none; the glyph cache 8,936 and 85.

## What a window's GPU memory holds

Where a window drawn as Lachlan's (WebGpu with the compositor, so D3D12) puts its memory is the groundwork's report, `lbterm/bench/mem/README.md`, with its tools beside it. Its first item is finding 95, the GPU allocator's blocks, and its second finding 96, D3D12's view heap. Where a window's memory goes after both is the table in "Where a window's memory goes now", below.

### What's done

- **The allocator's blocks** (finding 95). `request_device` (`termwindow/webgpu.rs`) took wgpu's default `MemoryHints::Performance`: D3D12's allocator, gpu-allocator through wgpu-hal's `dx12/suballocation.rs` (`create_allocator`), takes blocks of 256 MB of the GPU's memory to place buffers and textures in, and upload blocks of 64 MB in RAM, and keeps its first of each for the device's life. A terminal's own fit in a few MB. Each window makes its own device, so each window held both. Now it's `MemoryHints::Manual` with `config::lbterm::GPU_MEMORY_BLOCK`, 128 MB, which D3D12's allocator takes as 128 MB blocks of the GPU's memory and 64 MB upload blocks (64 MB and 32 MB until the atlas filling again was held to a test, "A screen of glyphs it hadn't drawn, held to a test", below). Only on D3D12, the compositor's backend, where it was measured: a window without the compositor may get Vulkan, whose allocator (wgpu-hal's `vulkan/adapter.rs`, gpu_alloc) takes a hint as its free-list chunks and its buddy allocator's first chunk, which for this one would be 8 times Performance's, unmeasured, so it keeps wgpu's default there.
- **Why not smaller.** wgpu stages each `queue.write_texture` in an upload buffer of its own, 64 KB at least (D3D12 places a buffer on 64 KB), and `WebGpuTexture::write` writes each glyph the atlas takes with its own. So the atlas filled again with a screen of glyphs it hadn't seen stages about 2,000 of them, some 128 MB, in blocks made for it and let go again once the GPU is done with them. With main's blocks a burst of such screens left two or three more 64 MB upload blocks resident at rest (GPU shared 200 MB) till a frame let their staging go; with MemoryUsage's 4 MB blocks each refill made and let go dozens, and the burst's paints took a quarter longer (PITFALLS.md, trap 77).
- **The view heap** (finding 96). wgpu-hal's D3D12 backend makes the device's shader-visible view heap whole as the device is made, `max_non_sampler_bindings` views of 32 bytes each here (`dx12/device.rs`, `capacity_views`), and every bind group's views live in it from its making till wgpu lets it go. `request_device`'s limits were `Limits::downlevel_defaults()`, which carry wgpu's 1,000,000, and wgpu-core hands the limits asked for to wgpu-hal as they are (`instance.rs`, `Adapter::create_device_and_queue`): 30.5 MB a window, the GPU's own memory mapped into the process, all zeros. Now they ask for 65,536 (`config::lbterm::GPU_VIEW_DESCRIPTORS`), 2 MB. Only D3D12 makes a heap of it; Vulkan and OpenGL take nothing of it, and every backend allows at least 1,000,000.
- **What a window binds.** The atlas's two bind groups, two views each (its texture, and the buffer of sampler indexes wgpu-hal gives a group with a sampler), made again only for another atlas, and a uniform's for each place a visual is drawn at, one view each, kept while it's drawn within 600 frames (termwindow/gpu_work.rs): a window of four panes at rest keeps 18 groups. Thumbnails, the Conductor's view and the cards are quads in the same atlas and visuals, with nothing bound of their own. A window resized kept one for each place at each size: 1,703 after 150 sizes of four panes drawing every visual, about 11 a size, so 600 frames of resizing would keep about 6,600, and nothing but the frame count held them, which stands still while nothing's drawn. Each is a buffer, which D3D12 places on 64 KB of the GPU's memory however small (PITFALLS.md, trap 111), so with 64 MB blocks they took new blocks: 600 sizes held 490 MB of GPU dedicated memory (the review, below). A uniform's projection is made from the window's size, so now a window drawn at a third size lets go of those from the size before the one before (`lbterm_motion::content::UniformsAt`, trap 112), keeping the last size's so a restore after a maximize makes none again, and they're held to 512 (`GPU_UNIFORMS_KEPT`, 32 MB, a quarter of a block; 256 while the blocks were 64 MB), past which the half drawn longest ago go (`lbterm_motion::content::uniforms_kept_since`); a frame that draws with one that goes keeps it till the GPU is done with it (wgpu holds it). First they were held to 16,384, a quarter of the heap, which allowed 1 GB of them. Running out of the heap would be an OutOfMemory from `create_bind_group`, which `gpu_lost.rs` takes for a lost device, building the window's GPU state again (PITFALLS.md, trap 75). The state file's `visuals.bind_groups` says how many a window keeps.

Speed, `lbterm.ps1 budgets` with main's build and this branch's, 2026-11-01, unlocked, with other agents' test windows and scripts running beside it throughout:

- **The suite**, 22 scenarios that paint, open, start, print or rest (key-echo and key-echo-local, key-burst-local, tab-by-key, tab-by-click, new-tab, split, resize, reload, start and start-local, throughput and throughput-local, spinner-tall, busy-one-tab, busy-tabs, busy-scroll, hearts, conductor-view-opening, one-tab, one-tab-local, four-windows), four rounds of each build alternating, 18:53 to 19:34. The PC's load rose through the second and third rounds, so a round's numbers moved together more than the builds' did: in each scenario this branch's two rounds fell within or next to main's two.
- **Each on its own**, the builds alternating six times a scenario, 19:37 to 20:14; the medians of three runs, main's then this branch's: key-echo 10.5 and 10.0 ms (the GUI thread 5.5 and 5.1 ms a key), key-echo-local 9.3 (two runs) and 8.6 (4.8 and 4.4), tab-by-key 4.5 and 7.4 (two runs) against 4.5 and 4.9 (two), split 49.0 and 41.7, resize 40.1 and 39.4, reload 114.3 and 110.3, busy-one-tab's frame 2.06 and 2.22 ms of the GUI thread, busy-tabs' 2.24 and 2.27, throughput on a mux domain 15.15 and 15.16 MB/s. spinner-tall was 15.2 and 16.2 ms (the GUI thread 10.8 and 12.3), so it ran again:
- **In a warden job** (four CPUs, below normal priority), spinner-tall, throughput-local and reload alternating eight times each, 20:19 to 20:37 (for the first five minutes with a second copy of the same runs beside them, started twice by mistake and stopped): spinner-tall 11.0 ms against 10.8 (the GUI thread 7.5 against 7.4; seven runs and six), reload 119.0 against 113.9.
- **Six more pairs in a warden job**, spinner-tall and throughput-local each alternating run by run, 20:39 to 20:51: spinner-tall 10.4 against 11.1 ms (the GUI thread 6.7 against 7.4), this branch slower in five pairs of the six by 0.3 to 4.2 ms and faster in one by 2.0; throughput-local 6.18 against 6.12 MB/s, faster here in four pairs of six. Over the nine spinner-tall pairs run one after the other (the three on its own and these six) this branch was slower in seven by ms, the median difference +0.6 ms, and in eight by the GUI thread, +0.85 ms. What a spinner's frame costs, part by part, says it isn't the frame: `perf/paint-cost.ps1 -Spinner -IntervalMs 100 -Measure` in the same window (1080 by 1920, Lachlan's look, the Conductor's pad, a row written again every 100 ms), four runs of each build alternating in a warden job, 20:54 to 20:59, about 245 frames a build, the medians main's against this branch's: the GUI thread 6.83 and 6.86 ms a frame, the pass 4.75 and 4.81, the draw 0.851 and 0.852, present 0.495 and 0.506; writing the layers' GPU copies 206 and 204 µs, recording 150 and 148, submitting 284 and 288 (GPU memory 470 MB against 278, private 549 to 551 MB against 323 to 325). The draw, the present and the writes are the only part the allocator's blocks reach, and they cost the same, so spinner-tall's lean is taken as noise; it's said here so it can be judged. Lachlan asked for it run again, clean, with the PC's load taken into account: with both items, held against the installed build by the budget runner, below, it wasn't told apart. Over every run of throughput-local, fifteen of each build, 6.21 against 6.23 MB/s.
- **throughput-local** (16 MB printed in a window's own tab) is the noisiest, 5.5 to 8.8 MB/s on either build and under its 11.5 budget on both while the PC was loaded: medians 5.83 against 6.85 in the suite, 6.06 against 5.82 on its own, 7.76 against 6.85 in the warden's job, and over all nine runs of each 6.41 against 6.41. Nothing this change touches runs there: following the GUI's private allocations about every 19 ms while a tab printed 16 MB (six runs, three of each build, alternating, 20:41 to 20:46, in a warden job), no allocator block came or went on either build.

`perf/gpu-memory.ps1` on the blocks' build and the heap's, 21:56 to 21:58: GPU dedicated 181.8 MB and 153.1 at rest; resizing a window of four panes that draws every visual 150 times kept 18 bind groups at its start and 1,703 after, over 283 frames that drew 23 or 24 visuals each, with no GPU error and the window drawing on; with the most kept made 64, the groups kept rose to 64 and fell back six times over 20 sizes, 38 after, the window drawing on.

Once main was merged in (16ce14801, 2026-11-03), on this branch's build (20261103-182622-ae4dfd97), each in a warden GPU job, unlocked: `perf/gpu-memory.ps1` passed at 18:30 (private 239.1 MB, the working set 180.9, GPU dedicated 153.1 and shared 73.2 at rest; 1,703 bind groups after 150 sizes, 64 at most with the most made 64, no GPU error), and the windows that bind the most at once drew on, 18:53 to 18:55: `perf/paint-cost.ps1 -Spinner -IntervalMs 100 -DrawAll` (every visual drawn in every frame, 23 a frame, the bind groups kept 40 at most, a frame 18.9 ms of the GUI thread at the median of three runs, each check of it passing), `perf/conductor.ps1` (the Conductor's pad, outlines, menu and view), `perf/thumbnails.ps1` (two images drawn, one half scrolled out, an SVG rewritten 30 times in one place in the atlas) and `perf/gpu-lost.ps1` (the device removed at 3 s and the window's GPU state built again with the same limits in 473 ms, the panes going on). With main merged in again (a07648c59, 2026-11-04: perf/fonts-core's GPU instance and adapter shared from `gpu_warm.rs`, `request_device` and this branch's limits and hints in `webgpu.rs` as they were), on 20261104-000710-b474846a, 00:15 to 00:18, unlocked: `gpu-memory.ps1` passed again (private 239.6 MB, the working set 181.8, GPU dedicated 153.1 and shared 74.2; 1,703 bind groups after 150 sizes, 64 at most with the most made 64), and so did `paint-cost.ps1 -Spinner -IntervalMs 100 -DrawAll` (23 visuals a frame, a frame 11.4 ms of the GUI thread at the median), `thumbnails.ps1`, `gpu-lost.ps1` and `conductor.ps1`, whose every check passed in all three runs and whose window check failed the first two on windows of Lachlan's coming up. The speed below was measured on the first merge's build.

Speed after both items, with the budget runner holding two builds against each other (`budgets -Against`, "Two builds against each other": Welch's test on the rounds at 99%, `same` meaning not told apart), 2026-11-03, unlocked, each a warden GPU job of its own with nothing else measuring, other sessions' builds and checks on other CPUs at times. ### A window resized

The review (2026-11-04) found the numbers above held only for a window that hadn't been resized lately. A uniform's projection is made from the window's size, so once the window changed size none of the uniforms kept could be drawn with again, yet each was kept 600 frames, and for good in a quiet window, whose frame count stands still. Each holds a buffer, which D3D12 places on 64 KB of the GPU's memory (PITFALLS.md, trap 111), and with the branch's 64 MB blocks then they took new blocks where wgpu's 256 MB first block had room for them: on the review's probe (Lachlan's config, four panes, the window a pixel wider at a time, memory read settled before and after), 150 sizes raised a window from 153.5 to 222.0 MB of GPU dedicated memory and 600 to 490.4, where one with wgpu's defaults went from 374.2 to 378.6 and 647. The cap (`GPU_UNIFORMS_KEPT`, 16,384, set from the view heap) allowed 1 GB of them, and `perf/gpu-memory.ps1` read memory only before its sizes and required the dead sizes' uniforms kept.

Now a window drawn at a third size lets go of the uniforms from the size before the one before (`lbterm_motion::content::UniformsAt`, keeping the last size's, so a restore after a maximize makes none again; trap 112), and they're held to a quarter of a block (`GPU_UNIFORMS_KEPT`): 256, 16 MB, with the 64 MB blocks this was measured with, 512 and 32 MB with the 128 MB blocks since. Measured with the review's probe on this branch's build with the fix and its build before it (6781ad79e), alternating, one warden GPU job holding the `lbterm-measure` lock, 2026-11-04 01:15 to 01:19, unlocked, other sessions' builds on other CPUs. In MB, settled before the sizes and after them, the window left at its widest:

| | before the fix, 150 sizes (two runs) | the fix, 150 sizes (two runs) | before the fix, 600 sizes | the fix, 600 sizes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| GPU dedicated | 153.5 to 221.9, both (+68.4) | 153.5 to 157.9 and 158.0 (+4.4, +4.5) | 153.5 to 490.4 (+336.9) | 153.5 to 170.4 (+16.9) |
| private bytes | 251.9 to 365.6 (+113.7), 254.8 to 366.2 (+111.4) | 252.6 to 292.1 (+39.5), 253.5 to 293.8 (+40.3) | 254.1 to 647.6 (+393.5) | 253.0 to 294.1 (+41.1) |
| GPU shared | 74.2 to 98.4, 75.2 to 98.1 | 74.2 to 98.3, 74.2 to 98.1 | 75.2 to 98.1 | 74.2 to 98.4 |
| bind groups kept | 18 to 1,702, both | 18 to 26, both | 18 to 5,570 | 18 to 26 |
| a size, median, p95 and longest, ms | 76.8, 110.4, 246.4; 77.7, 100.2, 161.0 | 76.6, 109.4, 235.0; 76.9, 109.9, 197.6 | 76.6, 95.3, 129.2 | 76.8, 96.6, 136.6 |

With the fix the bind groups stay at two sizes' places and the atlas's two whatever the sizes. The 4.4 and 16.9 MB of GPU memory left are the window's own size: 150 and 600 px wider, its swapchains hold more (`gpu-memory.ps1`, which ends back at the window's first size, below). GPU shared rose 24 MB on both builds alike, and private bytes about 40 MB with the fix (from 98 and 100 to 105 on gpu-memory.ps1's window drawing every visual): neither is the uniforms, and neither was looked into further.

`perf/gpu-memory.ps1` reads the window drawing every visual settled before 150 sizes and after them, back at its first size, and fails a rise of more than 16 MB of GPU dedicated memory or 64 MB of private bytes, more bind groups than the start's and two sizes' places, or any made when the window goes back to the size before; its oldest-go part moves the panes' divider at one size with the most made 16. Run on both builds in one warden GPU job holding the lock, 2026-11-04 01:57 to 02:00, the PC locked (memory reads no capture), the fix twice: on the build before the fix it fails each of those (GPU dedicated 153.6 to 217.8 MB, +64.2, private 285.8 to 380.0, +94.2, 1,707 bind groups at most, 1,706 after); with it, it passes (GPU dedicated +0.2 MB in both runs, private +23.3 and +27.6, 30 bind groups at most and 26 after, none made going back to the size before; the oldest-go part 18 at most with the atlas's two, falling 10 times, 17 after).

The windows that bind the most at once drew on with the fix, 2026-11-04 02:04 to 02:05, the PC locked: `paint-cost.ps1 -Spinner -IntervalMs 100 -DrawAll` (every check passing, the bind groups kept 28 at most, where stage 2's runs kept 40), `gpu-lost.ps1` (the window's GPU state built again in 382 ms, the panes going on) and `thumbnails.ps1`. And `paint-cost.ps1 -Spinner -IntervalMs 100 -DrawAll -Measure` on the build before the fix and with it, two runs each alternating, 02:06 to 02:08, the PC locked: a frame 11.7 and 11.9 ms of the GUI thread before, 12.6 and 11.3 with it, the pass 3.0 and 3.0 ms against 3.2 and 3.0; the draw took 98 to 121 ms of the wall clock on both builds alike, where stage 2's unlocked runs took 3.2 to 5.9, so that's the lock's (PITFALLS.md, trap 12), not the change's.

Speed, held against the build before the fix by `budgets -Against`, five rounds each, the order turned each round, one warden GPU job holding the lock, 2026-11-04 01:19 to 01:34, unlocked (others on 15 to 42% of the test's CPUs, the PC 29 to 44% busy, alike for both builds): everything `same`. `resize` (80 px wider or narrower, to its frame) 35.4 to 35.4 ms (+-8.79), the GUI thread 30.3 to 29.7 ms (+-3.94), private 283 to 282 MB; `split` 65.5 to 54.4 ms (+-41.9), the GUI thread 22.9 to 21.5 (+-4.32), private 270 to 269. `split` lost rounds on both builds to its own close timing out (20 s waiting for the split closed, three times) and to windows of Lachlan's coming up, and was judged on four rounds each.

### A screen of glyphs it hadn't drawn, held to a test

The block size reaches a terminal's speed in one place: the glyph atlas filling again (trap 77), and stage 1's seven runs a hint (above) put 64 MB's bursts 2.9% behind the default's with no test, on builds from before perf/fonts-core changed the atlas. So the budget runner has a scenario for it now, `atlas-refill` ("An action"): a screen of CJK ideographs the atlas doesn't hold, about 3,200 glyphs in a window made 55 rows tall, so every run fills the 1024 px atlas, clears it and draws its screen again (`atlas_rebuilds` 1 in every run; with the window's 32 rows a screen of 1,800 cleared it every other run and the rounds' medians fell either side of the two).
