Held against this branch's code with `request_device` as on main (wgpu's default blocks and view heap), built in this slot the same way (trap 78), ten rounds each and then ten more, each a warden GPU job holding the `lbterm-measure` lock, 2026-11-04 01:05 to 01:15 and 01:34 to 01:47, unlocked, others on 9 to 27% of the test's CPUs, the clock 3.33 to 3.43 GHz, alike for both builds; the second run lost rounds to windows of Lachlan's coming up and one to a PDH counter read failing, and kept eight of the defaults' and seven of this branch's. Each round's median of 12 runs, in ms:

| | the defaults (256 and 64 MB blocks) | 64 and 32 MB blocks (the branch then) | difference | Welch's test, +-99% | the rounds paired, +-99% |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| to the frame with the screen (`ms`), first run | 522 | 542 | +20 (means +24.3) | 31.8, `same` | +24.3, 16.2: `higher`, later in 9 of 10 |
| the same, second run | 523 | 560 | +36.1 | 67.1, `same` | +35.6, 76.4 over the 6 rounds both kept, later in all 6 |
| both runs, 18 and 17 rounds | 521 | 549 | +28.5 (+5.5%) | 29.2, `same` | +28.5, 22.1 over 16 rounds: `higher`, later in 15 |
| the GUI thread (`gui_thread_ms`, at the nominal clock), both | 537 | 550 | +13.2 (+2.5%) | 28.6, `same` | +14.3, 19.9: `same`, more in 12 of 16 |
| the process (`cpu_ms`), both | 542 | 554 | +11.8 (+2.2%) | 28.1, `same` | +12.6, 19.5: `same` |
| private bytes at the runs' end (`private_mb`), both | 1,077 | 798 | -280 | 80.4, `lower` | -276, 52.9: `lower` |

The runner's test, Welch's on the rounds, calls every speed `same`: its interval takes in how the rounds swing with the PC, which here swings more than the builds differ. But each round's two builds ran back to back, the order turned each round, and held round by round the screen came later on the 64 MB build in 15 rounds of 16, by 28.5 ms of 521 (5.5%), which the paired test tells apart at 99%; the GUI thread's CPU leaned 2.5%, not told apart. So 64 MB blocks are slower here, beyond the noise, by about 5% of a screen of new glyphs, and under Lachlan's rule (speed first, memory traded for it) that decides against them. The other option, 128 MB blocks (D3D12 upload blocks of 64 MB, as the default's), was built in this slot the same way and held against the defaults the same way, ten rounds, 2026-11-04 01:47 to 01:57, unlocked, others on 7 to 24% of the test's CPUs (quieter than the first runs, so every time is lower), one round lost to windows of Lachlan's: the screen 485 to 480 ms (+-16.4, `same`; paired -4.8, +-16.1, later in 4 rounds of 9), the GUI thread 499 to 496 (+-18.6, `same`), private bytes at the runs' end 1,107 to 949 (`lower`). So the 64 MB blocks' cost is their upload blocks' 32 MB; with 64 MB upload blocks the screen is as fast as the default's.

What each holds, `perf/gpu-memory.ps1` (-Measure for the two variants), one tab at rest with Lachlan's config, 2026-11-04 01:57 to 02:01 (the PC locked), in MB:

| | the defaults (256 and 64 MB blocks, 1,000,000 views) | 128 and 64 MB blocks, 65,536 views (now) | 64 and 32 MB blocks, 65,536 views |
|---|---|---|---|
| private bytes | 491.7 | 336.4 | 239.1 to 240.0 |
| GPU dedicated | 373.8 | 217.2 | 153.1 |
| GPU shared | 106.2 | 106.2 | 73.2 to 74.2 |
| the biggest private allocations | 256, 64, 32 and the heap's 30.6 | 128, 64, 31.9 | 64, 32, 32 |
| `atlas-refill`, against the defaults | | -4.8 ms (+-16.1 paired), `same` | +28.5 ms of 521 (+5.5%, +-22.1 paired), `higher` |

So under Lachlan's rule in LBTerm (speed over memory: the memory reported, asked about only when it's extreme), the blocks are 128 MB (`GPU_MEMORY_BLOCK` at `128 << 20`, 2026-11-04): they cost nothing measured and save 155 MB a window against the defaults (157 MB of the GPU's), where 64 MB blocks saved about 97 MB more (64 MB more of the GPU's own and 32 MB of RAM) for about 5% (28 ms) on a screen of glyphs the atlas doesn't hold. gpu-memory.ps1's first part wants no allocation over 128 MB, GPU dedicated under 235 MB and private bytes under 400. Writing a frame's new glyphs in one upload ("What's left"; FLOWS.md, the pane-output flow's proposal 11) would take the refill's cost away with any blocks, and smaller ones would save the memory for nothing then.

`perf/gpu-memory.ps1` on the 128 MB build (03550aa27), three runs, each a warden GPU job holding the lock, 2026-11-04 02:29 to 02:37, the PC locked: one tab at rest held 334.8 to 335.9 MB private and 217.2 MB of the GPU's own memory, its biggest private allocations 128, 64 and 32 MB; resized 150 times it rose 0.3 MB of the GPU's and 27.4 to 28.1 MB private, kept 30 bind groups at most and 26 after (the most kept 512), and made none going back to the size before; the oldest-go part fell 10 times, never past 18. The third passed whole. The first two failed the window check on another session's test window (ITMan's taskbars, coming up as its agent tried them), and the second also on its oldest-go part, whose move of the divider went unseen once (15 s waiting for the pane's columns; filed in Later).

### Where a window's memory goes now

A test window with Lachlan's config at rest with one tab, the same nine runs' first step, sorted by what owns each allocation (LbMem.cs's regions, the GPU Process Memory counters), each build's median in MB: committed (in private bytes), what of it is in the working set or the GPU's, and how much of what's resident is zeros:

| What | main's base | blocks of 64 MB (finding 95) | and the view heap (finding 96) | Counted in | Whose |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| The GPU allocator's device block | 256 committed, none resident | 64, none resident | 64, none resident | private bytes, GPU dedicated | wgpu (gpu-allocator), finding 95 |
| Its upload block | 64, all resident, 61.8 zeros | 32, all resident, 29.8 zeros | 32, all resident, 29.8 zeros | private bytes, working set, GPU shared | finding 95; What's left |
| The view heap | 30.6, valid pages, all zeros | 30.6, valid pages, all zeros | 2, in one of the driver's allocations | private bytes, GPU dedicated; not the working set | finding 96 |
| The driver's own allocations (in 32 MB reservations) | 60.4, 44.1 resident, 41.9 zeros | 60.7, 44.1, 41.9 | 63.2, 46.1, 43.9 (the heap's 2 among them) | private bytes, working set | nobody |
| The process heap | 46.5, 45.4 resident, 9.0 zeros | 46.7, 45.5, 9.0 | 46.5, 45.3, 9.1 | private bytes, working set | LBTerm, and the driver's 129 KB blocks (the groundwork's item 5) |
| Thread stacks | 1.5, 0.9 resident | 1.5, 0.9 | 1.5, 0.9 | private bytes, working set | |
| The exes and DLLs | 353, 56 resident, 32.6 writable | 353, 55.9, 32.6 | 353, 55.9, 32.6 | the writable part in private bytes, the resident part in the working set | accounting; nvwgf2umx.dll 24.6 writable, under 1 resident |
| Mapped files and sections | 14 to 16, 2.2 resident, 1.7 writable | the same | the same | | |
| **Private bytes** | 495.0 | 270.7 | 242.3 | | |
| **Working set** | 211.3 | 179.0 | 178.8 | | |
| **GPU dedicated** (the GPU's own memory) | 373.8: the device block, the heap and 87.3 more | 181.8: the same 87.3 more | 153.1: the device block, the heap and 87.1 more | | the 87: nobody |
| **GPU shared** (RAM the GPU uses) | 105.2: the upload block and 41.2 more | 73.2: the same 41.2 more | 73.2: the same 41.2 more | | the 41: nobody |

Of the 78 MB of a window's private allocations outside its heap that were resident with the 64 MB blocks, 74 MB were zeros: the upload block's 29.8 and the driver's 43.9. With the 128 MB blocks a window has had since 2026-11-04 the device block is 128 MB (none of it resident) and its upload block 64 MB (61.8 of it zeros, as main's base): private bytes 336.4, GPU dedicated 217.2 and GPU shared 106.2 ("A screen of glyphs it hadn't drawn, held to a test").

### What's left

- The atlas stages each glyph it takes in a buffer of its own: a refill costs hundreds of milliseconds of paint with any blocks (a burst's longest paint was 250 to 400 ms). Writing the glyphs a frame adds in one write would make a refill faster and let the blocks be MemoryUsage's: about 170 MB private, 112 MB of the GPU's and 60 MB of RAM a window less than the 128 MB blocks hold (FLOWS.md, the pane-output flow's proposal 11).
- What nobody owns yet, from the table above: the driver's own allocations, 63 MB committed a window and 46 MB of the working set, 44 MB of it zeros, in 32 MB reservations of the D3D12 driver's (nvwgf2umx.dll points into them); which calls make them isn't known, and an elevated xperf trace with VirtualAlloc stacks would say (the groundwork's "Not done"). 87 MB of the GPU's own memory beyond the allocator's block and the heap: the compositor's swapchains (23 to 25 visuals with four panes, several the window's size, two or three buffers each), the atlas and the card templates, not split further. 41 MB of GPU shared beyond the upload block, not identified. The upload block's 64 MB of RAM is 61.8 MB zeros at rest, and goes with the first item here.
- Upload blocks a burst filled stay resident till the next frame lets their staging go: a window at rest after a refill held 137 MB of GPU shared memory here, 201 MB on main's build.
- throughput-local showed about 6 MB/s on main's build and this branch's alike on 2026-11-01, under half its 11.5 budget. On 2026-11-03 it was 9.3 to 11.7 MB/s a round on every build measured (the installed 20261103-011219-de955813, this branch's and none), under 11.5 in 66 rounds of 68; what moved it from 6 isn't known.
- A slot's build of the same code was slower at local output than the installed build, by 0.5 MB/s of 10.9 (`throughput-local`, above), and nothing in this branch's code accounts for it. Release builds here are incremental (`lbterm.ps1`, `CARGO_PROFILE_RELEASE_INCREMENTAL`), so the code they make can depend on what each tree built before; whether that's it, and what it costs Lachlan's installs, isn't measured: a clean build of one commit held against an incremental one would say (PITFALLS 78).
- The blocks are 128 MB because smaller ones cost `atlas-refill` speed (64 MB's 5%, "A screen of glyphs it hadn't drawn, held to a test"). The atlas's staging, a buffer for each glyph, is what makes the block size cost speed there at all; writing a frame's new glyphs in one upload (the first item) would take the cost away with any blocks, and the blocks could shrink then.
- `budgets -Against` judges by Welch's test on the rounds, which leaves out that each round's two builds ran back to back: `atlas-refill`'s 5.5% was `same` by it and told apart by the rounds paired (PITFALLS.md, trap 113). A paired verdict beside Welch's in `Compare-BudgetRounds` would tell such a lean from the PC's swings without more rounds; the runner is every track's, so it's for Lachlan to decide.
- A resized window's private bytes rose about 40 MB on the review's probe and 20 to 30 on gpu-memory.ps1's, and GPU shared 24 MB, alike before and after the fix: not the uniforms, not looked into.

## What the measuring has found

The first runs, 2026-10-29 (the counters) and 2026-10-30 (the budget, then the budget runner at rest from 12 on, its actions from 22, after main's fixes came in from 28, and what's hard to time from 39). Each is for the stage that fixes or budgets it; none is fixed by this branch, which only measures. Main's fixes merged into it on 2026-10-30 (perf/conductor-inbox, fix/mux-socket-guard) changed some, and each of those says what's left ("The whole suite after main's fixes came in", below).

1. **The GUI thread spins on WM_PAINT between frames (upstream, `window/src/os/windows/window.rs`, `wm_paint`).** After a frame, a repaint asked for within 1000/max_fps ms is held back (`paint_throttled`): `wm_paint` notes it and returns without `BeginPaint`/`EndPaint` or `ValidateRect`, so the window's update region stays invalid, and Windows hands the GUI thread another WM_PAINT each time its queue is empty, until the throttle's timer runs. The thread never waits. Typing 400 keys on a mux domain took 116,617 window messages and 19 wakes, and the GUI thread spent 5.8 s of 6.4 s: 90% of a core while typing. With one line added (`ValidateRect(hwnd, null())` in that branch; the timer's `InvalidateRect` still brings the frame), tried in this slot and taken out again: about 1,950 messages and 760 wakes for the same 400 keys and the same frames, and the GUI thread spent 0.95 s and 2.1 s in two runs. Full-speed output: 4.7 and 5.1 s against 7.8 s, the same frames painted. Locally, typing 1.0 and 0.9 s against 3.9 s, output 2.2 and 2.6 s against 6.9 s. It wants a branch of its own and the e2e scripts run, since everything that paints goes through it. *Fixed by main's perf/frame-loop (bbb5c45af):* a held WM_PAINT is validated and the hold's end is a high-resolution timer in the message loop; a key now costs the GUI thread 9.3 to 11.7 ms and 5 to 7 messages ("The whole suite on the branch's last builds").
2. **The measuring itself, caught before it landed.** The first `metrics` listed the threads from a Toolhelp snapshot, about 140 ms of CPU a reply, which showed in the next span as a window "at rest" spending 3% of a core. Now about 0.5 ms (What they cost).
3. **A tab out of sight still costs the GUI on a mux domain.** A pane printing at full speed in a background tab (the first mux series, before the writer's tab was opened) cost the GUI thread a median 2.7 s and the GUI process 6.0 s in 8.8 s, both builds alike, while the window painted 3 frames. The client pulling a hidden pane's changes is the first place to look (`mux` shows its PDUs by type). *Since perf/mux-client:* what a push of a tab out of view cost the GUI thread was every line in it scanned for links as it came and two tasks to find it out of view; a line is scanned as it's drawn now, a quiet tab's screen 250 ms after it stops, and the output is news for the budget with no task. Six Claude-like tabs out of view cost the GUI thread 132 to 178 µs a push where main's build took 3,163 to 6,771, and six tabs printing without a pause 395 and 400 ms of each second where they took 849 and 851 (lbterm/MUX.md, "Tabs out of view").
4. **At rest a test window is quiet.** With WebGpu, locally, 4 s with no frames, no tasks, no messages, no wakes and no CPU at all. On the OpenGL front end, three `WM_GETTEXTLENGTH` (the harness's window watch reading the title) and as many wakes. On a mux domain, three runs of the cli listener's connection task (`wezterm-mux-server-impl/src/local.rs:24`, 0.2 to 0.3 ms) in 4 s, and now and then a clock tick of CPU on `update_checker` or an unnamed thread.
5. **Starting holds the GUI thread for seconds.** The task that runs the GUI (`wezterm-gui/src/main.rs:809`) held it 1.15 to 1.19 s in one run, and with WebGpu the task that makes the window (`frontend.rs:428`) 1.1 s more; on OpenGL that one is 0.05 s and `enable_opengl` (`window/src/os/windows/window.rs:916`) 0.3 to 0.38 s. Those are single runs' wall times, waiting included: the GUI thread's CPU over its first 5.3 s was 1.0 s. Start to first frame wants a budget in the runner.
6. **The first key** in a window still pays the keyboard layout's probe: WM_KEYDOWN's longest 11 to 35 ms (`lbterm-extras/src/layouts.rs` keeps it to once per GUI).
7. **A key's echo on a mux domain takes twice as long as locally.** In the quiet rounds of `budget-log.ps1 -Measure` (Expected times), p50 41 to 44 ms on a mux domain against 20 ms locally, p95 61 to 76 against 41 to 45; the check's own mux run saw 58 and 108 ms. Lachlan's tabs are all on a mux domain, so this is what his typing gets. The budget's lines for the keys over 50 ms in those runs (five, each the first over in its 10 s, from five windows, four of them on a mux domain) say where it went: the key's own WM_KEYDOWN 11.8 to 31.8 ms (local too: 12.2 ms), two or three frames' presents 12 to 21 ms and their draws 8 to 16 ms, and 216 to 622 WM_PAINT messages going round between them (finding 1), with 10 to 29 ms of `the rest`, the GUI thread waiting. What the WM_KEYDOWN spends 12 ms on wasn't looked into (stage 1 saw the layout probe make the first key's 11 to 35 ms; these aren't first keys). *Since perf/key-latency* the two are within a millisecond: 10.4 and 9.8 ms on a mux domain against 8.9 and 9.4 locally, each in the first frame after its key (findings 35 and 68, with 67's fix; "A key's echo, after perf/key-latency").
8. **Every frame costs a timer and four task runs, whatever it shows.** Painting at rest from a Lua timer (the check's step 3), 182 frames came with 372 runs of the paint throttle's task (`window/src/os/windows/window.rs:1819`, its timer armed after every WM_PAINT, which fires 1000/max_fps ms later) and 184 of its end (`:1825`), and 188 runs of `:1061`, `set_text_cursor_position`, which `update_text_cursor` calls on every paint and which spawns a task each time to move the IME's window, 0.16 ms a run (30 ms for 188 frames) though the cursor hadn't moved. A `set_right_status` costs 0.2 ms more (`wezterm-gui/src/scripting/guiwin.rs:102`'s notify, `update_title_impl` building the tab bar's state again). For each frame painted at rest by a Lua status loop, about nine task runs. *Since main's perf/frame-loop* the IME's window moves only when the caret does and a frame arms no async timer: a blink four or five task runs where it was eight.
9. **Something outside connects to a test window's own GUI socket every few seconds.** At rest, with nothing of the test's touching it, the cli listener's task (`wezterm-mux-server-impl/src/local.rs:24`) ran 3 times in every 12 s, locally and on a mux domain, in every run of the check (the first stage saw 3 in 4 s). It's under `rest_tasks`, so nothing is said. Which program it is wasn't looked for; one that checks every `gui-sock-*` under `~/.local/share/lbterm` for a live GUI would do it.
10. **A paint's p95 is two refreshes.** Across the six rounds, a paint's p50 was 12 to 14 ms and its p95 24 to 32 ms (the longest 76 ms): one frame in ten to twenty took longer than 25 ms, on a locked PC with other sessions' test windows running. Where it went is in each `paint` line (pass, draw, present); stage 4 times present to screen.
11. **A window's first frame takes 125 to 180 ms.** The first frames build the glyph atlas and the GPU's pipelines (178 ms locally and 125 ms on a mux domain in the check's start-up, before a window's first three frames were left out of `paint`). Start to first frame wants a budget in the runner (finding 5).
12. **The Conductor's view repaints for every line its seat sends, and each of its frames costs 18 to 20 ms of the GUI thread.** With its seat sending the view every 250 ms, as the real one does while the hub's guesses move (one session's time left counting down, which the view shows as "~4 min" for a minute at a time, so nothing it shows changes), the window painted 4.0 to 4.2 frames a second, every one asked by `wezterm-gui/src/seat_client.rs:122` (`repaint_windows` for each line), for 73 to 78 ms a second of the GUI thread and 78 to 84 of the process, 26 to 27 tasks and 4 timers a second, on both builds. The daily build's own warning names it: `window-0 painted 41 frames in 10 s at rest, asked by wezterm-gui/src/seat_client.rs:122 (41)`. With the seat quiet the view costs nothing (`conductor-view-quiet`: no frames, 0.02 to 0.05 ms a second of the GUI thread). So the cost is lines times frames: the 80% of a core Lachlan saw is the same with the seat's deltas every 100 ms while the Conductor writes and a longer conversation, all of which each frame lays out and shapes again (the audit's finding, which the Conductor's view track is fixing).
13. **On a mux domain, every frame a window paints makes its mux server work.** The client asks the server for the pane's changes each time it paints it (upstream's `ClientPane::poll`, `wezterm-client/src/pane/renderable.rs:605`, a `GetPaneRenderChanges`): 2 to 3.5 ms of the server's CPU a blink of the cursor, 2.5 to 4.7 ms a frame of the tasks card. At rest without frames the server spends nothing. *After main's fixes:* the same frames cost the server 0.1 ms a second in all (a blinking cursor 0.10 to 0.13, the tasks card 0.09 to 0.11), so what it spent wasn't working out the pane's changes, as this said, but the process walk main's 4c705a05e stopped: every paint asks each pane its title, a pane without a title of its own takes it from its foreground process, and on a mux domain the server walked every process on the PC for it (finding 22's walk). A tab switch's server CPU went from 13 to 15 ms to 0.4 to 0.7, a zoom's from 58 to 88 to 3 to 4. *Since perf/mux-client* a unix domain's pane is polled on its backoff alone, which a push, a key or the mouse no longer sets back to 20 ms (a resize or a zoom still does): a tab in view printing ten lines a second sends 1 to 6 polls in 6 s where it sent 51 (`wezterm-client/src/pane/lbterm_poll.rs`).
14. **A blinking cursor repaints the whole window, 13 to 18 ms of the GUI thread a blink.** At `cursor_blink_rate` 800 the window paints 1.2 to 1.4 frames a second, one a turn on or off, as it should; but each costs a full frame, 18 to 21 ms a second of the GUI thread and 23 to 25 of the process, with the server's poll (finding 13), the paint throttle's timer, the animation's and about eight task runs a blink (finding 8). Lachlan's cursor is steady, so his window doesn't pay this; anyone's with a blinking cursor does, all day.
15. **The tasks card repaints every second while a subagent is on it.** It reads the subagent's transcript again each second for what it says, and the look is a frame (`render/tasks_card.rs`, "a look each second"), changed or not, though every age on the card moves by the minute: 1.0 frame a second asked by `render/tasks_card.rs:190`, 17 to 20 ms a second of the GUI thread, 2.5 to 4.7 of the server's. The daily build allowed it as a clock until the review (finding 52); now it says it too.
16. **Each window holds about 440 MB of private memory.** One tab's window holds 525 MB locally and 541 to 567 MB on a mux domain; four windows of one GUI hold 1,851 to 1,879 MB, on both builds. Where it goes (the swapchains, the compositor's surfaces, the glyph atlases, the D3D12 driver's heaps) wasn't looked into; it wants a budget once it's known what a window should hold. The groundwork's memory report (`lbterm/bench/mem/README.md`) found where, in Lachlan's mode; 225 MB of a window was its GPU allocator's blocks, 64 MB since finding 95, and 30.5 MB its D3D12 view heap, 2 MB since finding 96 ("Where a window's memory goes now").
17. **At rest the process's CPU is NVIDIA's driver's.** The two threads that run at rest are `nvwgf2umx.dll`'s, NVIDIA's D3D12 user-mode driver's, about 1.0 and 0.4 ms a second whatever the window shows; then notify-rs's watcher, 0.14 ms a second. The GUI thread sleeps. And a window's first seconds spend about 13 ms on upstream's `update_checker` thread, though `check_for_updates` is off (LBTerm's default).
18. **On a mux domain the GUI's cli listener runs 0.77 times a second at rest, and never locally.** `wezterm-mux-server-impl/src/local.rs:24`, about 0.3 ms each: something connects to a test window's GUI socket every 1.3 s while its tabs are a mux server's, and nothing does while they're its own. Finding 9 saw it locally too, in other runs. Which program it is wasn't looked for.
19. **A mux server started for a connecting GUI runs its default program, never the one named.** `lbterm-gui connect <domain> -- <program>` connects, and the server opens its first tab with `default_prog`, not `<program>`. `perf/budgets.ps1` opens the program in a tab of its own and closes the server's first.
20. **The state file's `overlay` stays null while copy mode or search is up**, on a mux domain at least, though STATE.md says it names the overlay pane's title: the active pane in `panes` is the overlay's (`title` "Copy mode: pwsh.exe", no `server_id`). `budgets.ps1` waits on that instead.
21. **The rail's first opening in a window takes 160 to 260 ms**, said by the daily build's budget (`rail took 259.8 ms, 199.8 ms over its 60 ms: paint window-0/draw 199.7 ms (2) ...`), two frames' draws and 480 to 640 `WM_PAINT` messages going round meanwhile (finding 1); and a window's paints just after its first three take 95 to 120 ms (`paint took 121.1 ms ...`).
22. **Every `cli list` has the mux server walk every process on the PC, once for each pane.** With twelve tabs a `cli list` against the server took 63 to 75 ms, and cost the server 527 to 616 ms of CPU (the installed build 609 and 640). It's spent on threads that start and end within it: in a probe, the 54 threads the server had before and after spent 1.5 ms of it, and none alive after was new. `mux/src/localpane.rs`: an `AllowStale` lookup (`cli list` asks one of each pane) answers from the pane's list and, when that's over 300 ms old, refreshes it on a thread of its own, `CachedProcInfo::fetch`, which snapshots every process on the PC and reads each descendant's memory for its cwd and argv: about 50 ms of CPU a pane here, where 8,500 threads run. Against a GUI's own socket the server spends nothing. At 18 panes (what `perf/cli-list.ps1` measures) that's about 0.9 s of CPU for every `cli list` made through the server, and the Conductor, APCommand, the Claude project's `ai.ts` and Blueprint's Projects page all reach Lachlan's tabs that way; how often each calls it wasn't looked at. One snapshot shared by every pane refreshed at once, or a cli call that doesn't start refreshes, would make it about 50 ms or nothing. `cli-list` held the server to 60 ms. *After main's fixes:* fixed by main's 4c705a05e (a pane that hasn't printed for 2 s keeps its process list, however old): a `cli list` with twelve quiet tabs costs the server 1.2 to 1.3 ms at the median, on the merged build and the installed one alike, once the lists are taken (in one round the warm-up cost it 1.0 s; in all three the first measured run 256 to 334 ms and the second 1.6 to 22), and its budget is now this build's (3 ms). A pane that's printing still walks again each time its list is over 300 ms old, until perf/key-latency, since which a pane is walked only when its processes start or end (finding 49).
23. **A key costs the GUI thread its whole wait.** On a mux domain a key's echo took a median 36 to 43 ms, and for each key the GUI thread spent 34 to 40 ms of CPU and went through 275 to 302 window messages, nearly all `WM_PAINT`, for two frames; the server 16 to 22 ms. Locally the echo took 15 to 20 ms, but the GUI thread spent 36 to 44 ms on it, 297 to 316 messages: after the echo it keeps going round until the paint throttle's timer fires. This is finding 1's loop, key by key; with it fixed (stage 1's `ValidateRect`), a key cost the GUI thread 2.4 to 5.3 ms a frame and about five messages. Locally the process spends 55 to 67 ms a key, about 20 ms more than its GUI thread, on threads not looked at yet (finding 49: a process walk on a thread of its own each key). `key-echo` and `key-echo-local` hold a key to 10 ms of the GUI thread and 20 messages. *Fixed by main's perf/frame-loop:* 9.9 to 11.7 ms and 6 to 7 messages on a mux domain, 9.3 to 9.7 ms and 5 to 6 locally. *Since perf/key-latency* a key's echo is one frame: 5.6 and 6.4 ms of the GUI thread a key on a mux domain and 5.1 and 6.3 locally, three messages (finding 35).
24. **Output shows 2.6 times slower on a mux domain, and not fast locally.** 16 MB of plain text, lines of 104 bytes written 1 MB at a time: on a mux domain shown in 5.8 to 6.0 s, 2.6 to 2.7 MB/s, though the writer had finished its writes in 0.83 to 0.85 s: the GUI took 5 s more to show the end, its GUI thread spending 2.7 to 2.8 s and the server 3.4 to 3.5 s. Locally 2.2 to 2.3 s, 6.8 to 7.0 MB/s, the writer's writes 1.34 to 1.40 s (the console host waiting on LBTerm to read), the GUI thread 1.4 s with 6,200 to 7,000 window messages a run (finding 1 again). On a mux domain the server parses the output, so the GUI thread should spend less than locally, not twice as much: it's the path finding 3 saw a hidden tab take, the GUI pulling a busy pane's changes. `throughput` holds the mux domain to the local's speed and time. Most of it was the scroll strip's prompt marks (finding 65): each frame of output went through the 50,000-row scrollback and fetched every row that had scrolled past. Since 2026-10-31 the mux domain shows the 16 MB in 1.4 to 1.9 s (8.5 to 11.1 MB/s), faster than locally. Since perf/mux-client (2026-11-05, `perf/mux-throughput.ps1`, MUX.md, "Output at full speed"): plain lines show on a mux domain in 260 ms at the median (60.4 MB/s) against 301 in the window's own tab, with six tabs flooding out of view beside them in 299 ms, and Claude Code's look in 2,158 ms against 2,900 locally; the writer (ConPTY) takes 154 to 209 ms and 1.7 to 1.9 s of those, so the window no longer holds a mux domain back. The installed build that day (f9f79048) still fetched rows it hadn't been pushed, 107 to 124 GetLines a run of the look, which the branch's lent rows don't.
25. **From nothing on a mux domain, the window waits 2.25 s before it starts its server.** A start's first frame came 0.9 to 1.0 s after the process started, locally and on a mux domain alike (the GUI thread spending 0.83 to 1.13 s of CPU by the window's quiet; finding 5's WebGpu start). Locally that frame has the tab; on a mux domain it's the "Connecting..." pane, and the tab came at 2.75 s. One run's log: the first frame at 31.68, `Will try spawning the server` at 33.21, the tab at about 33.35. `unix_connect_with_retry` (`wezterm-client/src/client.rs`) tries the socket ten times, sleeping 50, 100, up to 450 ms between (2.25 s in all), before `unix_connect` starts the server, though the socket file isn't there; the server then has the tab up within 0.2 s. A socket that isn't there could start the server at once. A restart finds its server running and doesn't pay this; a start after the server has gone (a login, the last window closed) does. `start` holds the tab to the local's budget and 300 ms. *Fixed by perf/key-latency:* the GUI starts the server at once when no server holds the socket, which the server's own named mutex on it says (a connect refused can't: no file and a dead one's are refused alike), and tries the new server's socket every 10 ms; a socket a live server holds is waited for and never started over, and two windows opened together start one server (lbterm/MUX.md, "Starting the server"). A start's tab on a mux domain is up in 1,046 and 1,060 ms at the median, against 2,752 to 2,761 on the builds before and 906 to 962 locally ("A cold start, after perf/key-latency").
26. **First times cost several times the rest.** The first run in each window: the Conductor's view's first opening 137 to 147 ms against 17 to 28 after it; a split 104 to 110 against 59 to 70; a new tab 89 to 110 (155 locally once) against 49 to 72; a reload 109 to 159 against 85 to 96; a key's echo on a mux domain 64 to 96 against 36 to 43 (the layout probe, finding 6). They're each scenario's `first_ms`, said and not judged.
27. **A new tab, a split and a zoom cost the GUI thread twice their time, and the server as much again.** A new tab on a mux domain showed in 49 to 55 ms and cost the GUI thread 131 to 133 ms, the server 72 to 87, seven frames and 556 to 727 messages; a split 59 to 70 ms, 139 to 150 and 92 to 121, seven frames and 701 to 1,081 messages; a zoom 20 to 31 ms, 59 to 72 and 57 to 88. A frame of a whole-window change costs the GUI thread about 12 ms (a tab switch by key: two frames, 25 to 29 ms), and the messages are finding 1's loop. A tab switch by a click costs 37 to 47 ms of the GUI thread against a key's 25 to 29, three frames to two: the pad's own answer to the click.
28. **The same output painted 20 frames in the small hours and 35 later.** A writer's 16 MB shown locally took 20 to 21 frames in the rounds from 04:03 to 05:27 on 2026-10-30 and 33.5 to 36.5 from 11:26 on, on all three builds alike, the old one too (12:41 to 12:50: 33.5 and 34 on 20261029-213152-f9d36b5e, 35.5 and 34 on the installed 20261030-030621-0411a99a, 34 and 35 on the merged build), for the same time and GUI thread. It isn't the lock, as first thought: the rounds at 14:39 and 15:00, the PC locked, painted 34.5 to 35.5. The early rounds' PC had been locked for hours, so perhaps its screens were asleep and DWM composed less often; that wasn't checked. So a budget set from those rounds could fail later, as `throughput-local`'s frames did on every build; the merged build's budgets come from the later rounds.
29. **A test window held the foreground twice, both times in `new-tab`.** With Lachlan at the PC, the harness found a `new-tab` scenario's own window had the foreground when its runs ended, in two of three suite rounds: at 12:01:54 on the installed build (360 ms after his last real input; that test's process had its configuration error window open, finding 31) and at 12:28:53 on the merged build (594 ms after). In no other scenario, and `close-tab` sends the same keys (F8 opens a tab, F6 closes it). The harness looked at the foreground only as it posted input and at a test's end, so it couldn't say when it moved, nor whether it was his click or Alt+Tab, Windows activating the next window in its order as one of his closed, or the test's window itself. Its desktop watch now notes the foreground each time it changes (every 100 ms, 629f2f7fd), so the next time says when, what had it just before and where it went next. Test windows open at the bottom of the z-order without activating (`LBTERM_NO_ACTIVATE`), and nothing in a new tab's path calls for the foreground (`focus()` does nothing there). In the seven rounds after the watch came in (14:39 to 18:21, Lachlan at the PC for five of them), and the e2e scripts run between, no test window had the foreground. Open.
30. **A mux server spends a little at rest now.** 0.04 to 0.09 ms a second in every scenario at rest on the merged and the installed build, where the pre-merge build's spent nothing. That fits fix/mux-socket-guard's look at its socket file every 3 s; it wasn't checked. Under its budget of 3.
31. **A build without the budget opened its configuration error window in every action the GUI times.** budgets.ps1 gave such a scenario's window `lbterm_budget` (the GUI's own time for the action), which the installed build doesn't know, so it opened its configuration error window, a second window of the test's: in the installed build's action rounds after the merge and in the two before it (04:15 and 04:38), whose numbers were taken with it open, idle beside the one measured. budgets.ps1 now asks the build first, through `config_builder`'s strict mode, which refuses a field the build doesn't know without a word; checked on the installed build (no error, one window) and this one (its own time still there).
32. **Lachlan's own desktop fails the window check.** Unlocked, with him at the PC, scenarios failed for windows that weren't the test's: Chrome's status bubble (now noise to the harness: an untitled `Chrome_WidgetWin_1` of Chrome or Edge at most 40 px tall), Explorer's tray overflow, Earshot's window, and DisplayFusion's tooltips and taskbar previews. A scenario that fails so twice is an error, though what it measured is still in its report: in each of the merged build's two rounds one at rest errored so (`cursor-blink`, then `projects-card`), and five more passed on their second try (`one-tab-local`, `thumbnail`, `key-echo-local`, `tab-by-key`, and `new-tab` once before its foreground). Runs while he's at the PC will go on meeting them.
33. **Full-speed output on a mux domain once left the window not responding.** In the installed build's `throughput` (12:10 to 12:12 on 2026-10-30, its first try), Windows put up its ghost window for the test's three times, at 12:11:13, 12:11:38 and 12:12:14 (`dwm.exe`'s `Ghost` class, titled with the window's title: `[1/8] pwsh.exe`, then the writer's `wrote 6 1193 16453824`). DWM does that when a window hasn't taken a message for 5 s, so the GUI thread went that long without one. Its runs took up to 10.1 s to show the 16 MB, against 5.7 to 7.5 s in every other round. It happened once in the nine `throughput` scenarios run that day (54 runs, three builds), with the PC loaded by Lachlan's work and other sessions' tests; never locally. On a mux domain the GUI thread spends its time on the client's task for the pane's changes (`wezterm-client/src/client.rs:127`, 774 runs and 1.0 to 1.1 s a run of the writer), and how that keeps the thread from the message queue for seconds wasn't looked into (finding 24). What held it, as perf/mux-client found (2026-11-05): a single task on the GUI thread, the answer to a fetch of the pane's rows applied whole with every line scanned for links as it went in (`renderable.rs`'s `schedule_fetch_lines`), while the message loop drains the task queue until it runs dry and stops only for input or a posted message (PITFALLS 140), so no message was taken until it ended. On the installed build of 2026-11-05 that task still took 52 to 376 ms in full-speed runs, and the window went 231 to 377 ms without taking a message; on the branch's (rows lent where they're held, lines scanned as they're drawn, a hidden tab's output no task) the longest task was 1.2 to 5.3 ms, the longest drain 2.4 to 6.0, and the longest wait for a message 18 to 21 ms, with six tabs flooding beside the writer too (`perf/mux-throughput.ps1`, which fails a run past 1 s; MUX.md, "Output at full speed"). The drain's rule is unchanged: a stream of short tasks could still keep the thread from a paint or a sent message, which `metrics`' `gui.longest_drain_ms` now shows.
34. **A local key's echo looked twice as fast before main's fixes, because the state file said a frame showed what it hadn't drawn.** In the same conditions (12:53 to 13:24 on 2026-10-30, unlocked, interleaved) a key at a local pwsh showed in a median 12.1 and 12.9 ms on the build from before perf/conductor-inbox (20261029-213152-f9d36b5e) and 26.9 on the merged build (the suite's rounds: 22.7 and 27.0 merged, 27.5 on main's installed build). A probe that says which frame after the key showed it and when the first frame ended (budgets.ps1's `shown_frame` and `first_frame_ms`, 16:00, four rounds interleaved) found why. On the old build the first frame after the key, the one the key itself asks for, ended 10.5 to 20.2 ms after it, and in 25 of 30 runs was taken for the echo; when it wasn't, the second came at 37 to 44 ms. On the merged build that first frame ends 3.4 to 7.1 ms after the key, since it draws only the visuals that changed and nothing has yet, and the echo comes in the second, at 22.9 to 31.8 ms (three runs of 30 showed it in the first, at 4.6 to 6.3 ms). The state file's `cursor` is the pane's as the snapshot is taken, after the frame, so the old build's slower first frame was overtaken by the echo while it was drawn and was said to show it, though it read the pane before the echo came; the echo it drew came a frame later, as on the merged build. The GUI's own time for a key judged it the same way (`Shown::Output` read the pane's seqno at the frame's end). Both now go by what the frame drew: each pane's cursor and seqno as `render/pane.rs` draws it, the state file's `drawn_cursor` (lbterm/STATE.md) and the seqno `Shown::Output` compares. What's left is the echo itself: locally the second frame, the one that draws it, comes 17 to 28 ms after the first, and where those milliseconds go (the pane's output on its way, or the GUI holding the frame) wasn't looked into before main's frame pacing came in (below).
35. **A key's own frame holds its echo back a refresh.** Upstream asks for a frame on every key it sends a pane, down and up (`termwindow/keyevent.rs`, `context.invalidate()` for any key not a modifier), so a frame is painted at once, before the pane has echoed, and it draws nothing new. Since main's perf/frame-loop the next frame is held back one period from a paint's start, a frame of `max_fps` and never less than a refresh of the window's screen, so the echo's frame waits for that hold, though the echo is in well before it ends: the state file's live `cursor`, which says when the echo came if not when it was drawn, had it by the end of the first frame in 25 of 30 runs on the build from before main's fixes (10.5 to 20 ms; not by 10.5 to 12.4 in the other five) and in 3 of 30 on the merged build (4.6 to 6.3 ms; finding 34). With the merged build and the drawn cursor (16:00 on 2026-10-30, two rounds locally and one on a mux domain, 15 runs each): locally every echo came in the second frame after the key, at 21.2 to 27.0 ms, the first ending 3.7 to 5.3 ms after it; on a mux domain at 21.2 to 28.0 ms, the first at 3.4 to 6.4. Test windows open on the far-right screen, a 60 Hz one, so the hold there is a refresh, 16.7 ms: the first frame starts about 0.3 ms after the key, the second at 17 and ends about 4.5 ms later. Lachlan's main screen runs at 160 Hz, where the hold is `max_fps`' 8.3 ms, so his echo waits about half as long. A key's own frame that changes nothing could be left out, or the frame the pane's output asks for could start without the hold when the one before it was the key's; either would show the echo when it comes. `key-echo` and `key-echo-local` hold it to 20 ms, p95 35: an echo in its own frame, about 5 to 13 ms for the echo and 4 for the frame, with headroom. *Fixed by perf/key-latency (2026-10-31), with findings 67 and 68:* a key typed into a pane asks for no frame of its own unless that frame draws something LBTerm draws itself: an overlay took the key (copy mode, search, quick select, the launcher), the key scrolled the pane back to the bottom, its pane draws local echo's guess, or its pane isn't a local or mux client one, the two that say when their output changes them (`lbterm_extras::echo`, `termwindow/echo.rs`). A binding that sends the pane text or a key (SendString, SendKey, as Lachlan's Shift+Enter does) counts as typed. The echo's output asks for the frame that draws it, and that frame starts at once. Of the two ways named above, this is the one that measures best: the other, letting the output's frame skip the hold after a key's, keeps the key's frame (4.3 ms of the GUI thread a key, stage 1's two frames against this build's one) and its key-up's twin, and can show the echo no sooner, since here the echo's frame starts the moment its output arrives. With the key's frame gone a local echo still showed at 16.6 to 21.5 ms, held by the parser's wait for more output (67), and a mux domain's at 21.8 to 28.8, held by the frame of the server's answer to the key (68). With all three, an echo shows in 8.9 to 10.4 ms at the median, in the first frame after its key, its paint starting 4.6 to 5.7 ms after the key where it started at 17.5 to 17.7; bursts and held keys follow ("A key's echo, after perf/key-latency", which also says what the swapchain's latency and DWM's clock would buy). *A dead key's or an IME's composition* (the review of 2026-10-31): Windows ends one (`AdviseDeadKeyStatus(None)`, `window.rs`'s `key()` and `ime_composition()`) just before it sends the composed character, and its frame, asked for at once, drew nothing that character's echo wouldn't (the composing text at the cursor gone) and held the echo's frame a pacing period, as the key's own had. *Since the fix-up (2026-11-01)* a composition's end asks for no frame of its own (`lbterm_extras::echo::composition_frame`, `termwindow/echo.rs`): the echo's frame clears it, and a frame is asked for `COMPOSITION_CLEAR_WAIT` (50 ms) on only when none has painted by then (a character the pane doesn't echo, a composition cancelled, a key a binding took). `perf/echo-frame.ps1`'s step 5 posts an IME's composition and its end as Windows sends them (the window's IME holds no text, so the composing text is empty): with a key right behind the end, its echo came in the first frame after them both, 30.0 ms after them locally (the crates' tests building beside it) and 10.4 on a mux domain, the end asking for nothing; an end alone painted 73.4 and 57.1 ms after it, asked for by `termwindow/echo.rs`. The installed build (20261101-134606-4afd2a40, 15:28 on 2026-11-01) painted the end's own frame first, the echo coming in the second frame after them (26.7 ms), and an end alone 5.6 ms after it.
36. **A tab switch by a key lost its frame three times on the build with perf/frame-loop.** In the final rounds (16:05 to 17:22 on 2026-10-30), `tab-by-key` timed out in three of its four tries on the merged build, after its 7th, 6th and 13th run: F4 posted, and no frame in 15 s, the last one recorded settled and not animating. Never on the installed build, nor in any round before frame-loop came in, nor in 60 runs alone at 17:27 with nothing else running; the rounds had Lachlan's Chrome and, at the third, another session's build beside them. Whether the key was lost (since perf/frame-loop the message loop's drain stops for a waiting key) or the frame (a hold whose wake didn't come) wasn't found: the scenario throws at the timeout and closes its window, so it should read `metrics` and the state file's `pace` and `work` first, which it doesn't yet. Open.
37. **Output locally shows 7 to 12% slower with frames paced, for twice the frames.** A writer's 16 MB shown locally took 2,179 ms in both of the merged build's final rounds (7.2 MB/s) against 2,032 on the installed build in the round between them and 1,946 and 1,953 on this branch before perf/frame-loop came in, with 61.5 to 63.5 frames where those painted 34 to 35.5: frames now come at the screen's rate while a pane prints (60 a second on the test windows' screen, finding 35's), and each takes the GUI thread from the parser. Its GUI thread spent 1,380 ms against 1,416. Smoother output for a little less speed; whether frames while output streams could be fewer without the motion showing it wasn't looked into. *Since finding 67's fix* (perf/key-latency's own at first, perf/mux-server's on main) the speed is back: 7.89 and 7.8 MB/s against stage 1's 7.27 and 7.27 in the same rounds, the GUI thread 1,256 and 1,253 ms against 1,378 and 1,362, once the parser's wait for more output ended at its 3 ms instead of on Windows' 15.6 ms tick. The frames aren't: 61 for the 16 MB, against the 53 `throughput-local` holds it to. *Fixed by perf/key-latency* ("Output, after perf/key-latency"): it wasn't the pacing, the loop between frames or the reader's handoff (the pacing held no frame of the 61, each paint taking longer than a period; the loop went round 66 to 107 times a run; the parser's read of the reader's socket waited for nothing at the median, every read a full 128 KB), but what a frame did with the pane's terminal held, which the parser takes to put each batch of output in: it laid out the rows it showed inside the pane's `with_lines_mut` (3.8 ms a frame), which went with the frames, so frame-loop's doubled frames doubled it; and the scroll strip's prompt marks worked out every new row's zones a cell at a time (11.7 ms a frame, finding 69), which went with the rows printed, whatever the frame rate. The parser waited 6.7 ms at the mean for each 128 KB batch it applied in 7.1 to 7.3. Now a frame copies a local pane's rows with the terminal held 0.2 ms and lays them out after (`render/pane_lines.rs`), and the marks read a row off its runs of attributes (0.6 ms a frame): the parser waits for nothing, and 16 MB shows at 12.1 and 12.4 MB/s against 7.59 to 7.75 on the builds before, the GUI thread spending 633 and 653 ms against 1,274 to 1,290, in the same 58 to 64 frames. Frames while output streams are at the rate each paints, about 46 a second here, and no longer cost its speed, so `throughput-local` holds them to 91 and its speed to 11.5 MB/s.
38. **A test read an earlier process's log as its window's.** Every `lbterm-gui` writes `lbterm-gui.exe-log-<pid>.txt` and nothing it logs names it, so a GUI given a pid an earlier one had appends to that one's log. `budget-log.ps1 -Mux` (17:30 on 2026-10-30) found a budgets run's `tab took 26.5 ms, 6.5 ms over its 20 ms` there before its own window had done anything, took it for its tab switch and failed three checks on it. The harness now notes where the log ends as the process starts and reads its windows' lines from there (dfbfb0168); run again, every check of its own passed.
39. **Nothing a test window draws reaches the screen on this PC: a topmost box covers the screen they open on.** A Dashboards box (`bun.exe`, class `DashboardsBox`, WS_EX_TOPMOST and WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW, opaque) covers the right-most screen, where the harness opens every test window, from below DisplayFusion's 37 px taskbar to its foot (1,080 by 1,883 of 1,080 by 1,920), on 2026-10-30 from at least 19:00. A topmost window is over every other, so every test window is wholly hidden: DWM presents nothing for it, its swapchains' frame statistics stay zeros, and its motion would read as every refresh missed (the first runs did: 23 to 90 missed of 25 to 93 refreshes, finding 45). Captures still see it (PrintWindow draws the window itself), and nothing else measured rests on the screen. What reached the screen is measured only once a test window can be seen: the box elsewhere, or the test windows given a screen with room, which is Lachlan's call.
40. **A composition swapchain's frame statistics: DISJOINT, then zeros while nothing of it has gone up.** GetFrameStatistics on a DirectComposition visual's swapchain (wgpu-hal's D3D12 one) answered DXGI_ERROR_FRAME_STATISTICS_DISJOINT (0x887A000B) the first time and then success with every count zero, for every present of a hidden test window; QueryInterface for IDXGISwapChainMedia answered E_NOINTERFACE. Whether its counts move once its presents go up couldn't be seen here (finding 39).
41. **DwmGetCompositionTimingInfo's counts are the caller's.** With no window (Windows 8.1 and later allow nothing else), three calls 0.5 s apart said `cRefresh` 2, 3, 4 and `cFrame` 1, 2, 3, while the main screen refreshed 160 times a second; only `qpcRefreshPeriod` (62,519 ticks, 160 Hz, the main screen's) and the last vblank's time are real. So it can't count frames missed.
42. **The compositor clock's per-screen counts are final only for completed frames.** Read for the newest created frame (DCompositionGetFrameId's COMPOSITION_FRAME_ID_CREATED) they move in bursts, 75 to 302 refreshes at once; for the newest completed (COMPLETED) they move a refresh at a time, 30 in each 0.5 s of a 60 Hz screen. The history keeps 256 frames (back 300 answers 0xD0000225), and each call costs about 33 to 75 us from PowerShell, its interop included. Only two of the four screens' counts moved in those 40 s, the two portrait ones; the main screen's (160 Hz) and the left one's stayed where they were, though an hour earlier the main screen's moved a refresh a frame through all 256 frames of the history. So the counts seem to move only while DWM has work for that screen. Inside an episode that's every refresh, and the walk counts only from the motion's first present, but what an episode on the main screen says wasn't seen, and it matters: Lachlan's own windows are there.
43. **A budget window is wider than its screen.** At Lachlan's config's size the runner's window is 1,206 px wide on the 1,080 px portrait screen it opens on, so an eighth of it lies past the screen's edge, on no screen. What covers a window is judged on its part on its screen (finding 45).
44. **A key's echo under load.** With a writer at full speed in the pane beside it (10.3 to 10.8 MB a second taken from it meanwhile), a key's echo took a median 33 to 35 ms on a mux domain against 22 alone, and 52 to 57 ms locally, where the GUI's own time from the key's arrival was 37 to 47: the key waited 5 to 19 ms in the queue before the GUI took it, behind the writer's frames and the notifications of its output. From the key's arrival, its frame's paint started 17.5 ms in alone and 23 to 26 with the writer, and its presents were done at 22 alone and 31 to 38 with it (present.ps1 and the suite, both domains). Locally that frame took 23 ms from its paint's start to its presents, against 6 on a mux domain and 4 to 5 alone. Why wasn't looked into; the first place to look is the paint waiting on the writer's pane, whose parser thread holds its terminal while it takes in output (on a mux domain the server parses it). *Since perf/key-latency* (findings 35 and 68, with 67's fix) on a mux domain 27.1 and 30.1 ms against 35.3 to 36.5; locally the same within six rounds' spread, 50.6 to 66.7 against 51.2 to 64.2, the GUI's own time 45 to 50 against 47 to 49: the queue and the writer's terminal are what's left.
45. **The measuring's own two bugs, caught before it landed.** The first look at what covers a window took its whole rectangle: a box over all of its part on screen came to 87%, under the 95% that counts as hidden, so split and close-pane counted a hidden window's refreshes as missed (22 to 25 a run) and keys got screen times nobody could have seen. And each frame that wanted an earlier wake started a timer beside those armed, each of which armed again as it fired: 2,300 runs of present.rs's task in 15 splits, 24 ms of the GUI thread. Both fixed in 5f5bee9c0.

What a review of the whole branch found on 2026-10-30, and what changed for it (46 to 55), each measured on this branch's build after the fix-up, 21:34 to 21:52 that evening, Lachlan at the PC, other sessions' test windows and benchmarks running beside them (their windows failed some runs' window checks, nothing of these tests' own).

46. **The daily build's warning cried wolf on a blinking cursor.** The review ran `budgets.ps1 -Scenario cursor-blink` (Lachlan's `animation_fps` 1, a blinking block at 800 ms) and the window logged `window-0 painted 7 frames in 10 s at rest, asked by render/paint.rs:159 (6) ...; 6 of them on animation ticks past the 11 it may make in 10 s` and `tasks ran 23 times in 10 s at rest`; the suite's last report had the same two lines. Three causes: the blink's tick said `animation_fps` (1 a second) though with `animation_fps` 1 a blink turns at its own rate, 1.25 a second; every tick due went toward the rate, allowed or not, so once a purpose was over its rate at all every tick after counted as at rest; and in a test window the state file's own wake after DWM's motion (`termwindow/state.rs`) was counted at rest. Now the tick says the rate its wait gives, between `animation_fps` and the fastest blink; only allowed ticks go toward the rate; the state file's wake is the measuring's own. After: `budget-log.ps1`'s new step 3 (a blinking cursor at `animation_fps` 1, the window told it has the keyboard, 12 s): 15 frames, 15 animation ticks, none past the rate, every frame, timer and task on purpose, nothing counted at rest and no line; `budgets.ps1 -Scenario cursor-blink`: no `lbterm::budget` line at rest (it still fails its CPU budget, finding 14). `budget.rs` holds a purpose 10% and 20% faster than it says to no span over `rest_frames`, and one twice as fast to a line.
47. **The measuring took locks and allocated on every frame of the daily build.** After a window's first three frames every frame is a timed paint: its end took the budget's totals lock and upstream's `metrics::histogram!` (a label list built, the recorder's global lock and a hash lookup, then the histogram's own lock), and present.rs, on in every window, took another histogram, a lock, a QueryInterface and GetLastPresentCount, a Vec or three, and wgpu-hal's swapchain lock and an AddRef for each visual, with the look at what's over the window every 250 ms (1.41 ms in the review's run) and a 20 ms timer after the frame. And the compositor's list of what plays kept every name ever played, copied out each frame. Now an action's counts and histogram are atomics (`lbterm-extras/src/hist.rs`) and its trace kept in place; present.rs does nothing until asked (What's hard to time, "Off until asked"), so a frame hands over no swapchain and the list isn't read; the list forgets what ended 5 s before; and `metrics`' snapshot of upstream's histograms holds the recorder's lock only to take its list, no longer while it works out every percentile, which every `histogram!` on the GUI thread waited behind. Measured by `budget::tests::what_the_hot_paths_cost` (five runs): an action begun and shown 323 to 326 ns, against 267 to 272 before (the trace kept in place is copied as the action goes in and out of the waiting list; upstream's `histogram!`, which each paint also took in the GUI and this doesn't time, is gone), a run timed 7.8 to 8.4 ns, a task 15.8 to 16.3, traced 34.0 to 34.3, a timer fired 53.4 to 54.0; `hist::tests`: a record 28.9 to 29.2 ns, a read and reset of a histogram 22 to 26 µs. The whole process, `metrics.ps1 -Measure` (400 keys, then 8 s of full-speed output), interleaved, 21:45 to 21:52, the installed build (none of this branch), this build and this build with the timing on (`-Present`), the GUI thread by its cycles in ms, every run: typing locally 1006.8, 956.0, 956.2 / 988.7, 1098.9, 952.6 / 1012.5, 989.7, 940.4; output locally 2583.6, 2644.5, 2551.2 / 3294.2, 2680.3, 2553.3 / 2778.0, 2543.2, 2471.3; typing on a mux domain 1055.7, 1092.7 / 1118.2, 1142.3 / 1172.0, 1088.3; output on a mux domain 4500.1, 4599.7 / 5197.5, 4592.4 / 4540.6, 4519.6. Every difference is inside a build's own spread (frames 375 to 388 typing, 466 to 486 output, alike). The windows were hidden, as every test window is here (finding 39), so with the timing on nothing was watched or walked: what it costs a window in view is still unmeasured, and it stays off unless asked.
48. **A window with any tab printing was never at rest.** Every pane's output or alert, background tabs' included, kept its whole window busy for `quiet_ms`, so with Lachlan's eight or more Claude sessions working behind his open tab his window was almost never judged, and the Conductor's view's four frames a second, which started this, would have gone unsaid in his daily use. Now only the open tab's output and alerts are activity; a background tab's are news (At rest, above), which keeps the process from being at rest for its own tasks but leaves the window's frames judged, those in the 100 ms after one of its alerts allowed up to `rest_news_frames` (30) a span. `budget.rs` holds a window whose background tab prints every 100 ms to naming its frames at rest, and an alert's own frames to its allowance.
49. **Each key's echo walks every process on the PC, about 18 ms of CPU a key.** The review saw the mux server spend 19.7 to 21.8 ms of CPU a key's echo, and the GUI's threads besides its GUI thread about 17 ms a key locally, and couldn't say where. The runner now reads the mux server's threads and the GUI's by name for each run, and says what threads that started and ended within it spent (`(threads gone by the end)`): in `key-echo`, 15 runs, the server's main thread 25 ms in all and threads gone by the end 269 ms, about 18 ms a key (its CPU a key 17.2 to 22.5 ms); in `key-echo-local`, the GUI's main thread 153 ms and threads gone by the end 283 ms, about 19 ms a key (the process 27.6 to 46.8 ms a key, its GUI thread 9.4 to 11.2). The echo is output, so the next paint's ask for the pane's title (a pane without one of its own takes its foreground process's) finds its process list over 300 ms old and the pane printing within 2 s, and refreshes it on a thread of its own (`mux/src/localpane.rs`, `proc_list_refresh`'s `InBackground`, `CachedProcInfo::fetch`): a snapshot of every process on the PC and each descendant's memory read, as finding 22's walk, which main's fix kept for panes that are quiet. A key typed at a prompt changes no process; a walk only when one could have changed (the console's own process list moving, or a while after output stops), or a title asked of the server not taken from the processes, would make it nothing. Not fixed here: `key-echo`'s `server_cpu_ms` and `key-echo-local`'s `cpu_ms` are held to what an echo should cost (2 ms and 13 ms), where they were budgeted at 1.5 times what this measured (30 and 45), and fail until it is. At eight keys a second that's about 15% of a core in the server, all day, in every tab Lachlan types in. *Fixed by perf/key-latency (2026-10-31):* each pane's program is put in a job of its own as the pane is made (`mux/src/lbterm_procs.rs`: breaking away still allowed, nothing killed as it closes, nested jobs fine), whose counts of processes started and running move exactly when one starts or ends, and which says so on a completion port that the process's one `lbterm-jobs` thread waits on, asleep at rest. A list taken at the counts there are is current, so output walks nothing. A walk comes of the job's message, for a pane whose title or cwd comes from its processes (a Claude Code session names itself and its shell says its cwd, so the tools it runs walk nothing), or of an ask that finds the counts moved, on a thread of its own, at most every 300 ms while they go on moving. A shell without OSC 7 has its foreground's cwd read alone in the ask that wants it, 17.9 us against 12.9 ms for a walk of the same program (`lbterm_procs`'s test), and a process just started, whose parameters its loader hasn't yet made pointers of, is read a pace later. What a walk or a read changes that consumers show raises the alert that has them read it again, so a program that prints nothing (ping to $null) now shows in its pane's title, the rail and the strip as it starts, 128 to 723 ms after its line was sent (most of it pwsh starting it), where the installed build never showed it (`perf/proc-title.ps1`, locally and on a mux domain). Measured by `budgets.ps1 -Scenario key-echo` and `key-echo-local`, 15 keys a run after a warm-up, two rounds interleaved, the installed build (20261030-191144-301f18e6, copied to a scratch folder) then this branch's (the code of its third commit), 05:20:59 to 05:23:32 on 2026-10-31, the PC locked, another session's build beside them (2 to 6 cargo and 2 to 7 rustc at a run's start) and 2 to 4 `lbterm-gui`: on a mux domain the server's CPU a key 15.8 and 15.5 ms before, 1.84 and 2.03 after (its budget 2), its threads gone by the end 209 and 204 ms over the 15 keys before and none after; locally the process's CPU a key 25.6 and 27.3 ms before, 10.6 and 10.6 after (its budget 13), its threads gone by the end 228 and 271 ms before and none after. The GUI thread (9.2 to 9.7 ms a key), the frames (2) and the echo's time (21.8 to 22.7 ms at the median, finding 35) came out the same on both. Two rounds each on the branch's first two builds: the cwd read on a thread made for it, the server 2.03 and 2.14 ms a key (threads gone by the end 3.4 and 3.8 ms over the 15, a thread's making and ending costing more than the read), and read in the ask, 1.94 and 2.05. What a key still costs a mux server, 1.2 to 1.4 ms on its main thread and 0.45 on an unnamed thread of its own, is the echo itself. *The review of 2026-10-31* found two things. A walk came of any process starting or ending in a pane whose title or folder came from its processes, printing or not, so a pane whose folder alone did walked every process on the PC every 300 ms while its programs came and went (13 to 18 ms a walk, about 5% of a core a pane): cmd and Git Bash tabs, and the Conductor's Claude tabs, which it starts as `pwsh -NoProfile -Command "claude ..."`, so no prompt says their folder until claudehook's first OSC 7 (the line above that a Claude Code session's shell says its folder holds only for tabs started at a prompt). And the list's logic, about 250 lines, sat in upstream's localpane.rs. *Since the fix-up (2026-11-01):* a job's message says which process started or ended (its pid is the message's overlapped pointer, `lbterm_procs::Move`), and a walk comes only of what changes what the pane shows (`lbterm_procs::walks_for`): a process starting in a pane titled by its foreground process (the youngest is its foreground), or the listed foreground ending in a pane that shows its name or its folder. Any other process ending changes nothing shown, so a pane that shows only its foreground's folder walks once that foreground ends and never for the statusline and hooks under it; an ask for the list itself (Lua's foreground process name) still walks when the counts moved. A walk for the foreground's end first waits for its handle to be signalled (up to 300 ms, `wait_ended`): Windows takes a process out of the snapshot a moment after its job says it ended, and every walk 1 ms after ping's end still found it. Whether a pane's title and folder are its processes' is read from its terminal as each batch of output is put in (`perform_actions`), where a title (OSC 0) or a folder (OSC 7) is set: asked as a consumer read them, a background tab nobody had asked since its start kept the answer from before its program said either. The logic is `mux/src/lbterm_proclist.rs`, a module of localpane.rs's own, which keeps a hook in `perform_actions`, the asks and the job's making. Measured on 2026-11-01, unlocked, other sessions' test windows beside them: `perf/proc-title.ps1`'s new step, five pings run from cmd (a pane that names itself and doesn't say its folder), heard as ten moves and walked none, locally and on a mux domain (15:29 and 15:30); typing walked nothing, and ping's run walked twice, its start and its end, as before. `perf/at-rest.ps1`'s new 1e, a tab that names itself and says its folder starting a ping every 2 s, printing nothing: locally (15:55) 10 programs heard as 20 moves, no walk or read, no frame, no task of LBTerm's on the GUI thread, lbterm-jobs 20 wakes and no other lbterm-* thread waking; on a mux domain (16:00, the whole script passing) the same, the jobs heard by the server's lbterm-jobs (20 wakes), and nothing on the GUI's side at all. ConPTY passes such a tab's title on only once a program has run in it (its folder at once), so until then it's titled by its process and its first program walks, as it should. ConPTY also sends a pane's title again as each program in it ends, unchanged, which the parser and the window take as upstream does (a title set again): an output notice, the held alerts' task and the window's subscription's on the GUI thread, three tasks a program in 1e. That's main's, not this, and on a mux domain, where Lachlan's tabs are, the server takes it (its main thread 10 wakes for the 10) and pushes the window nothing.
50. **`#[track_caller]` doesn't survive a call through a trait object.** The key, mouse and rail handlers hold their window as `&dyn WindowOps`, and their 63 calls to `invalidate` named `window/src/os/windows/window.rs:1046`, the implementation's own line, not theirs: 15 of the 32 frame credits in the review's key-echo run. They now call `context.lbterm_invalidate()` (an inherent method of `dyn WindowOps` in window/src/lib.rs, which hands its caller to the trait's new `lbterm_invalidate_at`), but for the ten in `render/conductor_view.rs` and `render/inbox.rs`, the other track's files, still named by window.rs's line. `key-echo`'s frames are now credited to `termwindow/keyevent.rs:815` (15) and `termwindow/mod.rs:1769` (15).
51. **Frames nobody asked for were never counted, and a window gone could keep its slot.** A frame with no request behind it (Windows' own WM_PAINT) was said in the line but never counted against `rest_frames`, so a paint loop Windows drives (an update region never validated, stage 1's finding 1) stayed silent; now it's counted, named `WM_PAINT`. And a request for a window already destroyed, from a task that outlived it, claimed one of the 64 windows' slots for good: once they ran out every frame was nobody's and the warnings went blind without a word. Now such a request claims none (IsWindow), and running out is said once.
52. **The daily build allowed the tasks card's look at a transcript as a clock.** Its rate counted the card's once-a-second look at a subagent's transcript, so the daily build never warned of finding 15, which the runner fails. Now only the ages' own minute and second ticks are a clock, and the two agree.
53. **A key whose program doesn't echo was LBTerm's time, and a key's wait in the queue wasn't.** A key sent to a password prompt or a program not reading waited up to 10 s for any output of its pane and was then logged as over its time (`key took 3000 ms ...`). Now it's given up on, without a line, at three times its time. And a key that waited in the queue behind a busy GUI thread (finding 44's 5 to 19 ms) was timed from when the GUI took it; now from Windows' stamp on it, less a tick of that clock, when the stamp says it waited more than two.
54. **Throughput's budget followed the regression down, and the history was never read.** After main's perf/frame-loop made local output 7 to 12% slower (finding 37), `throughput-local`'s least went from 5.1 to 4.8 MB/s and its frames from 53 to 96, set again from the slower build at its median over 1.5, which lets a third slower pass. It's held to the speed it had, 7.5 MB/s (`throughput`'s too) and 53 frames, with finding 37 as its bug. *Since perf/key-latency* finding 37 is fixed and it's held to what that build shows, 11.5 MB/s, and to 91 frames, which no longer cost its speed. And `history.jsonl` sat in each tree's own target, one a slot, read by nothing; it's one file in the main checkout's target now, and each run says what moved since the scenario's last ("The report and the history").
55. **Resetting the daily LBTerm's counts started every reader's span again.** A reset is the whole process's (counters, the budget's, present's), and `lbterm control metrics --reset` asked the daily LBTerm by default, so a second reader zeroed the first's span without either knowing. The daily class refuses a reset now unless it says `force`; test windows, each of a class of its own, reset as before.
56. **A window's loops kept DWM composing every refresh, minimized or covered.** The waiting pads' arcs and the Conductor's outlines and light are animations DWM plays for ever, which cost the GUI nothing, so the budget runner never saw them (`conductor-pad` passes at rest). DWM's own numbers (the harness's `Measure-DwmSpan`, `perf\dwm-loops.ps1 -Measure`) did: with one playing, the compositor clock completed a frame every refresh of the main screen, 159.9 to 160.7 a second, and never went 50 ms without one, in all 24 spans measured on 2026-10-31, the window under another or minimized; with none it went 67 to 160 and idle between, by what the rest of the desktop did. Since then they hold still while the window can't be seen (minimized; Lachlan's windows also locked or with the screens off), and `conductor-pad-minimized` holds that; lbterm/COMPOSITOR.md, "Loops", has the table. What a loop costs dwm.exe's CPU a second couldn't be told from what the rest of the desktop cost it, with other sessions' runners opening test windows throughout, and a window that's only covered still plays them (nothing says when a window is covered). The state file's `loops` came from the panel's plan at first, so a check on it held the plan, not what DWM was given; it's read from the compositor's own spinners, outlines and lamps since 2026-11-01, which showed the Conductor menu's arc keeping its turn, hidden, for the window's life once an item had waited, and it holds still once the menu closes now (`perf\conductor.ps1`).

What the busy scenarios found on 2026-10-31, and what perf/lbterm-layers changed for it (56 to 60; What the cards cost has the numbers).

56. **The InMail list's layout cost 47 ms of the GUI thread a frame, and 8.7 ms a mouse move.** It laid itself out every frame and again for every mouse event in the window, and each time asked which project each of its 70 messages' folders is in by making all 47 projects' folder keys again, and its paint asked a second time: with the list open over a tab painting 20 frames a second the GUI thread spent 95% of a core. Its layout is kept now, made again only when what it shows changes: 4.6 ms a frame, about busy-tabs' own.
57. **The projects card cost 18 ms a frame.** It counted every message's and tab's project by making every project's folder key again for each, and was built twice a frame; with its words shaped each time. Now 4.6 ms.
58. **Every open card shaped every word every frame.** `compute_element` has no cache, so a card left open while the tab under it painted laid its 20 to 250 words out and shaped them again for each frame. They're kept now, and the busy scenarios' `words` hold it: none laid out while what they show stays the same.
59. **The tab panel built 2.2 ms of every frame, whatever changed.** Its ring, glow, lit gradient and running light are a few thousand quads with a sine and a cosine each, made for every frame, a pane's line changing included, and each word went through `render_element` and an allocator of its own. Kept as quads now: 0.9 ms, and a frame with nothing open from 5.1 to 3.7 ms.
60. **The closed rail dissolved rows for words it doesn't show.** A row's look was its whole `{:?}`, so a new status, message or age, which only the open panel shows, restyled the row on the closed rail and dissolved it from a picture the same as the new one. The rail's look and the open panel's words' are hashed apart now. Not measured in a window: the dissolve shows in captures, which the locked PC didn't give.
61. **A line holding a link was laid out again on every frame unless the pointer was on a link.** wezterm's line caches took the pointer on no link, before and now, for a change (`same_hyperlink` is false for two nones). With 26 linked lines over a ticker on a mux domain, 543 lines a second were laid out where 20 changed, and a frame cost the GUI thread 6.9 to 8.7 ms (9.3 on the installed build) against 4.6 to 5.0 for plain text; the pointer coming onto a link or going off it laid out every linked line. Each cache entry keeps its line's links now, and a line is laid out again only when which of its own links is under the pointer changes: 20 lines a second, 5.6 ms a frame (What a line costs).
62. **Lines a trigger matched were copied, recoloured and hashed again every frame, and what was new was wrong three ways (four, with the first fix's).** Every line with a match of the easter eggs (LBTerm on most lines of a Claude session about it) was copied, recoloured and its shape hashed on every frame (the copy never kept its hash), and what it matched told to `Seen` again, a String a match. A match on the screen was new again at the first frame after ten seconds at rest (hearts for a key typed elsewhere), a line scrolled a row in a fullscreen view was new at each row (391 matches for ten lines, each a Lua event's task with no handler in the config), and a new line's match at a column another's had in that row wasn't new. Now a line is matched when it changes, its copy kept with what it matched (coloured again for a new palette), a place stays seen while it's on the screen, and what's new is settled at the frame's end by how many lines on the screen have each match's line and row, so a line moved a row or streaming on in its row isn't new and the same line printed again is (the first fix took any line of the same text for the same, the review of 2026-10-31); the Lua event runs only with a handler. busy-triggers 4.9 to 5.0 ms a frame to 4.5 (What a line costs).
63. **A voice draft read its pane's whole screen every frame.** Each frame a draft showed, its pane's screen was copied out of the mux client, the input box found in it, the answers' colours looked up and the card's words shaped, and the question colours were laid out again for any output in the pane, the status line's included: 0.35 to 0.51 ms of the pass a frame. Now the screen is read when the pane changes and laid out when the conversation does: 0.03 to 0.10 ms, within the rounds' spread (What a line costs).
64. **A burst of hearts painted the whole window 67 times.** The GUI moved the easter egg's seven hearts by painting the window every 16 ms for two seconds: 235 to 277 ms of the GUI thread a burst on stage 1's build, 343 to 355 on the installed one. DWM plays them now with motion on, handed over once as they rise and following their pane: 3 frames and 11 to 20 ms; with motion off the GUI draws them as before (What a line costs).
65. **A shell's prompt marks went through the whole scrollback every frame, and on a mux pane fetched it and marked none of it.** At a shell the strip asked for the rows changed since its last look from the scrollback's first row, every frame with output: on a mux pane 50,000 lookups in the client's line cache, which counted each row it didn't hold as changed, so the first look asked the server for every one of them in a GetLines (the server's main thread 1.2 to 1.3 s on it, the GUI's longest task 1.4 s, 85 MB of blank lines while they came), and the lines that came back were never read: 7 of 100 prompts marked. Output under a full scrollback fetched what scrolled past again, 3,000 GetLines in 10 s, and painted 40 to 52 frames a second where 20 changed, 265 to 282 ms of the GUI thread a second. Now a look reads what was written since, a mux pane's from the lines the GUI holds and the rest asked of the server as rows, the first look a chunk at a time: 100 of 100 marked, the longest task 1.3 ms, 20 frames a second at 92 to 103 ms; and 16 MB of output on a mux domain shows in 1.4 to 1.9 s where it took 6.1 to 6.6 (finding 24) (What a shell's marks cost).
66. **The prompt list read all of history.jsonl for every click.** Each open of the strip's list and each jump to the last prompt read Claude Code's whole history (2 MB, every session's) and went through it on a thread of its own: 4 to 8 ms of CPU a click, growing with the file. It's read on from where the last read stopped now, about 1 ms, keeping where each session's records are (16 bytes a record) rather than every session's prompts for the GUI's life; the list showed in the same 26 to 29 ms either way (What a shell's marks cost).

What perf/key-latency found behind findings 35 and 37 while fixing them (67 to 70; numbered after perf/lbterm-layers', which landed first).

67. **The output parser's 3 ms wait for more lasted up to 18.6 ms.** `parse_buffered_data` (mux/src/lib.rs) holds what it has parsed for up to `mux_output_parser_coalesce_delay_ms`, 3 by default, while more is coming, so a TUI's frame written in several writes reaches the terminal as one. It waited with WSAPoll's timeout, in whole milliseconds, which ends on the system's 15.6 ms tick in a process that doesn't raise its timer resolution, as LBTerm doesn't: in mux's test a 3 ms WSAPoll took a median 11.9 to 12.2 ms (longest 15.5) over five runs. An echo is a write of a few bytes with nothing after it, so it waited all of that for nothing, in the GUI for its own tabs and in the mux server for a mux domain's: with the key's own frame gone (finding 35) a local echo showed at a median 16.6 to 21.5 ms, and at 6.2 with the delay set to 0. *Fixed by perf/mux-server* (`mux/src/lbterm_wait.rs`, main since 01659fc5d): the wait is a high-resolution waitable timer's, then a look at the socket, so what came meanwhile still goes into the frame. perf/key-latency found it behind finding 35 and made the same fix (`lbterm_coalesce.rs`: its median 3.41 to 3.49 ms in the same runs, and local output at 7.89 and 7.8 MB/s against 7.27, finding 37), but perf/mux-server's landed first, so the branch's went when main was merged into it (2026-10-31) and the wait is main's. The delay at 0 would take another 3 ms off an echo but lose the coalescing upstream keeps it for: not changed.
